### A.2 Project / Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

#### The Churia hills are the southernmost range of the Himalayan foothills, running east-west through Nepal and into India. They are characterized by steeply sloping mountain slopes and seasonally-populated riverine plains downstream, including groundwater regulation, prevention of silation, flood control, and livelihood services, including fuelwood, fodder and construction materials. The Churia hills are home to about 50% of Nepal’s population.

#### Climate change is already having a significant impact on the Churia's ecosystems and inhabitants. These impacts are projected to intensify in the coming decades. Increasing temperatures and changing precipitation patterns are expected to lead to more frequent and severe droughts, increased frequency and intensity of floods, and changes in the frequency and intensity of hazards such as floods. Communities in the Churia region are extremely exposed to such climatic conditions due to their dependence on rain-fed agriculture and limited access to alternative livelihood opportunities in climatic conditions. This vulnerability has been exacerbated by decades of unsustainable natural resource management practices that have led to soil erosion, loss of biodiversity, and other environmental degradation with severe consequences for the Churia region, downstream communities (including in northern India), and broader food security.

#### The Building a Resilient Churia Region in Nepal (BRCRN) project aims to confront these challenges by enhancing the resilience of ecosystems and livelihoods. It will promote widespread adoption of climate-resilient land use practices, confront the impacts of climate change on the BRCRN's Churia region. It will promote widespread adoption of climate-resilient land use practices, confront the impacts of climate change on the BRCRN's Churia region.

#### The project is conceived as a dual contribution to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). It will build the capacity of communities in the Churia hills to better manage their natural resources and adapt to climate risks and scale up much needed support after project closure. The project will be implemented through an integrated river system-level planning among sub-national government, community-based organizations (CBOs) and other partners, and strengthen their capacities to monitor natural resources and climate change impacts to inform future planning and investment decisions.

#### It supports the implementation of a landscape approach to natural resource management approaches by providing the training, expert guidance and inputs, materials and tools needed to overcome barriers to sustainable land use practices. The project will also support the development of an integrated river system-level planning among sub-national government, community-based organizations (CBOs) and other partners, and strengthen their capacities to monitor natural resources and climate change impacts to inform future planning and investment decisions.

# C1. Strategic Context

## Social, Political and Economic Context

### Nepal is one of the World’s Least Developed Countries (LDCs). Despite recent economic progress, the country has a per capita income of only USD 756. In addition, Nepal has experienced several shocks in the last three years that have had a substantial impact on its economy. These include the 2015 earthquake and subsequent aftershocks, which occurred shortly after the earthquake, and devastating floods in 2017 that affected over 1.1 million people.

### Agriculture (including livestock) and forestry are key economic sectors and are responsible for one third of the country’s GDP. These sectors are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their dependence on natural resources, as well as stable and predictable climatic conditions.


## Climate Change Context

### Climate change and vulnerability

* Nepal is ranked as the world's 4th most vulnerable country to anthropogenic climate change. The most profound impacts will be on the agriculture sector, water resources, public health and energy.

* Historical analysis of climate data indicates that a large part of the Eastern Himalayan region is undergoing warming. Over the last century, the average temperature has increased by approximately 0.5°C. Warming is widespread with warming generally of 0.01 to 0.04°C per year. The highest rates of warming are generally in the winter. Long-term trends for annual precipitation in Nepal indicate a general increase over time. However, there have been significant inter-annual and intra-annual variations across different regions of Nepal, and over the high mountains within the central region. Monsoonal and annual precipitation feature significantly more variability than other seasons. The highest rates of warming are generally observed on the western slopes of the hills. On the other hand, winter precipitation demonstrates a decreasing trend over most of Nepal.

* Future projections for Nepal indicate that there will be an increase in temperature with changes in precipitation patterns, with both increases and decreases expected. While the monsoon season will become wetter and the dry season will become drier – as well as an increased likelihood of heavy precipitation events. While there is considerable uncertainty in climate models with respect to future projections for Nepal, it is clear that there are significant differences between models and also significant differences in the regional and temporal distribution of this precipitation.

* Analyses of historical data indicate that extreme weather events are expected to increase the frequency and intensity of climate-related hazards in many regions of Nepal. This is particularly true of the Churia Terai-Madhyan region.


## Climate change context of the Churia Terai-Madhyan region

* The Churia hills are located between the Himalayan foothills to the north and the plains in the south of Nepal (Terai), where the Churia hills form a critical ecological and socio-cultural functions both upstream and downstream. Error! Reference source not found!. With more than 50% of its territory under forests and meadows areas, the Churia hills render hydrological services to the Terai region. The Churia hills are also an important habitat for wildlife and provide a corridor that allows animals to pass through the Churia hills before reaching the densely populated alluvial plains in the Terai and northern India. In addition, the Churia hills are critical for water resources as they act as a natural reservoir of water for the country’s agricultural production. Together, the Terai and Churia comprise about 25% of Nepal's land area, but harbour close to 50% of its population.

## The Churia-Terai-Madhesh region is already experiencing the impacts of climate change.

These impacts are expected to intensify in the coming decades. Although available data and models indicate that there is uncertainty regarding the severity of negative climate change impacts, the overall risks and associated trends are clear and warrant a concerted response from all stakeholders involved.

a) Average annual temperatures have increased – driven in part by particularly notable increases in temperatures during the summer months over the past few decades (see for example ICIMOD 2009). Temperatures are expected to continue increasing in both summer and winter months in the coming decades, with some models predicting that average annual temperature will increase by up to 1.8-5.8°C between 2070–2099 relative to the mean from 1966-2005.

b) Average annual precipitation has decreased over the past few decades (see for example ICIMOD 2009). Precipitation has also been decreasing in both summer and winter months, with some models predicting that average annual precipitation will decrease by up to 3.8–14% between 2070-2099 relative to the mean from 1966-2005.

c) The number of hot days has increased over the past few decades (see for example ICIMOD 2009). Some models predict that this trend will continue in the coming decades.


---
**Page 12**
---

average winter precipitation could decline by around 10% for the period 2021-2050 relative to the period 1961-1990.

c) The number of consecutive dry days has increased across Nepal and in the Churia region over the past 40 years, with a recent analysis finding that 80% of analyzed stations in Nepal (including in the region targeted by this project) have experienced an increase. This trend is expected to continue increasing in the coming decades.

d) Although the overall volume of precipitation during monsoon season has varied considerably over the preceding decades, there are indications that the number of very wet days and the duration of the monsoon season will decrease while the number of dry days increases. The number of consecutive dry days is projected to increase considerably in the future. Some stations have already experienced a significant increase in the number of consecutive dry days (e.g., 10% for the period 2021-2050 relative to the period 1961-1990). Over the course of the entire summer season, the number of very wet days is projected to decrease by around 30%.

e) Extreme precipitation events have become more common over the past 40 years, with some recent observations in the nearby Koshi river basin (from 2010-2015) suggesting increases in the number of heavy precipitation days at >75% of stations. The frequency and intensity of extreme precipitation events are expected to continue in the coming decades, particularly during monsoon season. The number of very wet days is projected to increase by around 30%. The number of dry days is also expected to decrease. In other words, increases in overall monsoon precipitation (as outlined in d) above) will likely fall into fewer but more intense events.

16. These past and future changes in climatic conditions pose serious challenges for households and communities in the Churia-Terai Madhes region, the broader Churia ecosystem, and (by extension) the food security, livelihoods and well-being of people living there. The impacts are likely to be felt across a range of sectors, as outlined here below and in Figure 2, and are explained in detail in Section 2 of the Feasibility Study.

a) The dry winter season is expected to become more pronounced with less seasonal water stress. As temperatures continue increasing, post-monsoon and winter precipitation continue decline in the coming decades, communities in the Churia region may increasingly struggle to cope.

b) Extreme precipitation events are projected to increase in frequency and intensity for the Churia region. As the number of consecutive dry days continues to increase, rainfall becomes more sporadic, and the water that does fall (during extreme events) is likely to be more intense. This will have a significant impact on communities who will have to find new ways to cope with water deficit. Unsustainable natural resource management practices are also expected to continue in the region due to demographic pressure.

c) The incidence of flooding events has increased considerably since the 1970s, albeit with significant inter-annual variation. Due to past changing precipitation patterns, the frequency of floods is projected to further increase in the Churia region over the coming decades (as outlined above). Unsustainable management of land and forest resources also contribute to this by: i) further reducing infiltration capacity; ii) increasing soil erosion and sedimentation; iii) increasing riverbank instability (described below), which contribute to riverbed rise, river channel change, and thus flash floods further downstream.

d) Rates of soil erosion and sedimentation have steadily increased, driven in part by the combined effects of drying soils and extreme precipitation events. The Churia region is characterized by steep slopes and fragile landscapes are particularly vulnerable to this due to continued deforestation and forest degradation, as well as the widespread use of inappropriate land management practices (e.g., slash-and-burn agriculture). The impacts on communities and ecosystems are considerable, including: i) the loss of top soil in upstream areas, which is reducing cultivable land; ii) increased sedimentation in downstream areas, which is contributing to river channel change, mire bank cutting, flash floods and loss of fertile farm land.


---
**Page 13**
---

17\. The Churia region and its inhabitants are particularly vulnerable to the above-mentioned climate change impacts. Key vulnerability factors are briefly summarized below, and elaborated in more detail in Section C.2 of the Funding Proposal, and in Figure 18 on pages 9 & 4.

a) The geological features of the Churia region leave communities particularly exposed to climate-related hazards. The riverbeds are characterized by steep slopes, which can lead to flash flooding during heavy rainfall events or when there is little or no water flow during the rest of the year. In addition, the Churia consists of highly fractured sedimentary rock and soil with low infiltration capacity. During the wet season, runoff can quickly exceed the capacity of the riverbeds, leading to riverbed rise downstream that increases the risk of flash flooding. It has also further exposed the Churia's already steep and fragile slopes, increasing risk of erosion and landslides upstream.

b) Unsustainable natural resource management (NRM) has reduced adaptive capacity and reinforced vulnerability to climate change impacts in the Churia region. Poor land use practices, such as overgrazing, deforestation, and unsustainable agricultural practices, have resulted in soil degradation, reduced vegetative cover, and further reduced water infiltration capacities of the landscape. This has led to increased runoff during heavy rainfall events, which can lead to riverbed rise downstream that increases the risk of flash flooding.

c) Proximity to the Himalayas means that the Churia region is highly sensitive to changes in local climatic conditions and a healthy natural resource base, and thus are particularly sensitive to the above-mentioned climate change impacts. Many communities depend on agriculture for their livelihoods, which are already under stress due to the current climatic conditions being optimal for the local terrain – particularly under the anticipated changing climatic conditions – including cultivation on steep slopes.

18\. These climate change impacts pose significant challenges for Churia communities and ecosystems, but also have a significant impact on the management of forest resources in the project area. Deforestation and forest degradation in the Churia region are already contributing to increased vulnerability to climate change impacts (see Section C.2 of the Funding Proposal and Figure 18).

E\. As communities and the predominant (agricultural) livelihoods on which they depend are undermined by climate change, there is a risk that these communities will be forced into more environmentally destructive practices in an attempt to accelerate both vulnerability and GHG emissions. While Nepal accounts for only 0.02% of global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, the country has already experienced significant deforestation and forest degradation over recent decades, with forests covering just 52% from agriculture and 15% from land use change, especially deforestation and degradation.

The country is committed to reducing GHG emissions and pursuing a more low-emissions development pathway – something that can only be done by addressing the aforementioned challenges.

# DETAILED PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 10 OF 11T

### Male Changes in Climatic Variables

- **Increasing temperatures** Temperatures projected to rise by °C by the end of the century.
- **Shifting precipitation patterns** Higher level of uncertainty/inconsistency than seasonal variability, but increasing evidence indicates:
    - More frequent heavy rain and winter precipitation.
    - Fewer but more intense summer and monsoon precipitation. Increased frequency of extreme precipitation events in monsoon season. Additional precipitation expected to increase.

### Primary Impacts

- **Seasonal Water Stress** Reduced availability and reliability of baseline livelihood strategies.
- **Extended dry periods** Reduced availability and reliability of baseline livelihood strategies.
- **Increased flooding and/or landslides and/or avalanches** Damage/hazard and loss of life, property, and impose economic costs.

### Secondary Impacts

- **Increased Soil Erosion** Driven by combined effects of climate change (increased temperature and intense precipitation events) coupled with unsustainable farming practices (land management and degradation) and unsuitable agricultural land use.
    - Upstream: Top soil loss and nutrient depletion.
    - Downstream: sedimentation, reduced water flow in the Churia Riverbed rise (increased vulnerability to floods).

By undermining the viability of baseline livelihood strategies and farming models in the Churia, primary and secondary impacts will accelerate a vicious cycle of vulnerability in the Churia Terai-Madhes region, creating a feedback loop that accelerates both vulnerability to climate change and emissions from deforestation and forest degradation.

19. Figure 3 illustrates the complex and intertwined interactions between climate change and unsustainable NRM in the Churia Terai-Madhes region. A vicious cycle of vulnerability is created by these changes, leading to increased climate hazards, causing cascading and interlinked impacts from the hills to the downstream areas. The combination of recent and projected changes in climatic variables, coupled with unsustainable farming, land management and NRM that are exacerbated by deforestation and forest degradation, will result in widespread damages and losses, while also exacerbating existing vulnerabilities in the future and increasing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. Both climate change impacts and emissions from deforestation and forest degradation are both increasing. This is expected to steadily undermine local livelihoods (which in turn) is expected to further reinforce unsustainable land use practices and NRM that will lead to more vulnerability, and a vicious cycle will be broken. Failure to do so could have severe medium/long term consequences for the Churia region and its inhabitants as well as for downstream communities and broader food security in Nepal.

### International and National Commitments, Policies and Strategies

20. The Government of Nepal is committed to responding to climate change. Nepal ratified the Kyoto Protocol (2002) and Paris Agreement (2015). It has submitted its National Communication Reports (2004 and 2016) and Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the UNFCCC. Nepal's NDC notes that given its status as a low-income economy, it will rely on international support for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

21. Nepal has initiated various national strategies, policies and plans to support climate change mitigation and adaptation, including the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), which identifies priority areas such as water resources development projects. Nepal's Climate Change Policy (2011) aims to improve livelihoods by reducing climate change impacts on agriculture, forestry, and sustainable natural resource management. These priorities are also highlighted in the Fourteenth Periodic Plan (2017–2021). The government has made significant progress towards these goals through various initiatives and calls for conservation, sustainable management of forests and watersheds.

This is further supported by a number of national strategies aimed at addressing climate change impacts such as forest degradation within the country. Meanwhile, the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) identifies a number of priority areas including i) water resources development projects, ii) land use planning and management, iii) restoration of degraded areas, iv) climate-related research, information and awareness generation, and v) disaster preparedness.

Additional information on relevant policy commitments is included in Feasibility Study Section 2.


---
**Page 16**
---

## Strategies focusing on the Churia Tarai Mahesh region of Nepal

22. Recognizing the scale and extent of environmental degradation in the Churia Tarai-Mahesh region and its vulnerability to climate change hazards, the Government has enacted various policies, programs and strategies focusing on the Churia region. This includes the 'President's Churia-Tarai Mahesh Conservation Development Board' (PTMCDB) which was established by the President of Nepal in 2015 to coordinate efforts for conservation in the Churia region. More specifically, it aims to support the integrated management of upstream and downstream ecosystems, including forests, water resources, agriculture, and human settlements, with a view towards poverty reduction through conservation and sustainable management of the Churia region's resources and improvement of ecosystem services.

23. The PTMCDB prepared the Churia Master Plan with an investment budget of NRs 13 billion (c. USD $122 million) per annum over five years from 2015 to 2019, which was approved by the President in August 2016 for implementation. The Plan, which prioritized 64 out of 164 river systems but can only work on 29 of them due to a budget constraint, is expected to mainstream climate change considerations into the Churia Master Plan and provide a framework for Nepal to secure additional external funds needed to bridge the gap between the investment required by the PTMCDB and the available resources.

## Political transition and institutional restructuring

25. While Nepal has been successful in mainstreaming climate change into national development strategies and sectoral plans, there is still a need to strengthen the capacity of local governments to support climate change management at both the national level and local level. Nepal is transitioning from a constitutional monarchy to a federal state, as mandated in the 2015 Constitution Act. This transition will result in the devolution of power from the central government to the provincial and provincial levels. As a result, provinces and local governments (municipalities) will become the relevant authorities to oversee forest and agriculture-related activities including land use planning and management of natural resources.

26. Provincial governments have their own Ministries and technical departments and will be directly responsible for the implementation of climate change policies and strategies at provincial level. Although they are staffed by trained technicians, mostly seconded from the federal Ministries, they are yet to develop coherent planning cycles and development strategies that can be integrated into national plans. The project will provide them with appropriate strategies from the outset, serving as an example which can be replicated in other provinces and systems throughout the country.

27. The provincial government benefits therefore from a strong political commitment and the opportunity to shape the development trajectory of the new decentralized institutions. However, since Nepal is Least Developed Country (LDC) with high rural poverty levels, it will need to adopt locally-appropriate, low-cost and low-maintenance technical approaches that can be adopted/applied by local communities in order to achieve sustainable results.

28. Furthermore, Nepal is internationally recognized as a leader in Community-Based Natural Resource Management, with CBOs having long-term usufruct rights and decision-making over forests and other resources. The project will build on the

### C2 Project/Programme Objective against Baseline

The overarching objective of the proposed GCF project is to enhance the climate resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable communities in Nepal's Churia region through integrated sustainable natural resource management (SNRM) approaches.

Project activities will focus on the Central and Eastern Churia-Terai Region, where 26 vulnerable river systems are located. These rivers are situated across Provinces 1, 2, and 3, with a total population of over 3.2 million people, comprising approximately 51% women and 77% considered to have experienced intergenerational discrimination.

The project aims to work with 750 community-based organizations (CBOs), each consisting of around 864 members or 963,268 people. Women will constitute at least 50% of beneficiaries in these CBOs. The specific river systems and their characteristics are detailed in Figure BRCRN project location in vulnerable river systems in Central and Eastern Churia-Terai region.

### BASELINE SCENARIO IN THE INTERVENTION AREA

32. The 26 BRCRN river systems cover a combined area of 702,011 ha. Within each of the river systems there are four distinct biophysical zones, with shared land use characteristics, land use changes and socio-economic activities:

a) **Churia Hills** (33% of project area): Hilly terrain with steep slopes, typically located at higher altitudes where 13% of the land is cultivated and 47% covered by forests.

b) **Bhawar** (18% of project area): Transition zone between the Churia Hills and Terai consisting of 35% cultivated land and 49% forested land. It is a key zone for groundwater recharge, given its unique geological characteristics and soil type.

c) **Dun Khola** (20% of project area): A river valley located in the middle of the Churia Hills, often containing human settlements where 40% of the land is cultivated and 37% covered by forests.

d) **Terai** (46% of project area): Fertile plains in the South of the country. Often called the "rice basket" of Nepal, where 12% of the land is cultivated and 58% covered by forests. The Terai has a higher population density due to its good cover and quality as the Terai, many households in the Terai are dependent on Churia forests as distant sources.

32. Table 2 provides an overview of the land use characteristics and trends in each zone, as well as a description of how current land use patterns may be affected by climate change (as described in Section C.1) and other socio-economic factors. ### Forested Land

34. Deforestation is a major cause of land use change that further accelerates vulnerability to climate change in upstream and downstream areas of the Churia Tarai-Madhesh region. Deforestation and forest degradation not only lead to increasing GHG emissions, but also erosion and sedimentation that increase river bank rise downstream, increasing the risk of flooding. In addition, deforestation reduces the capacity of forests to store carbon and reduce greenhouse gas emissions in the Terai. In addition, the removal of vegetation on sloped areas in the Churia and Bishnepur regions can also increase soil erosion.

35. Deforestation in the project area has dramatically increased in recent years, increasing by 0.2% annually from 2000-2014. The majority of deforestation occurred in the Churia hills, and most of this occurred in the Churia hills. The direct drivers of deforestation and forest degradation can be split into four categories:

* **Unsuitable extraction of forest products:** This can include unsustainable (over-harvesting) or illegal logging, uncontrolled grazing, and other forms of land use that lead to soil erosion and sedimentation in the Churia hills, and uncontrolled grazing is problematic as it leads to the loss of shrubs and falling of small to medium sized trees.

* **Agricultural expansion and degradation:** While the area of agricultural land has remained relatively stable over a 15-year period, it is still considered a driver of deforestation and forest degradation. This is due to the use of fallow-agricultural practices that lead to soil erosion, reducing soil fertility and requiring additional clearing. Livestock also contribute to degradation given the impact of livestock grazing on shrubs and small trees.

* **Development or expansion of infrastructure:** Several national roads and transmission line corridors are in various stages of development in the Churia region, sometimes without adequate planning. Beyond this, roads can further act as an indirect driver of deforestation by improving accessibility to other previously inaccessible areas.

**C**

## Natural land use change and bio-physical conditions.

The fourth identified direct driver of land use change is not anthropogenic but natural. However, it should be noted that unsustainable human activity can further exacerbate natural drivers of land use change. While the Chera-Turi-Maheshwari region has a relatively stable climate, however, human activity or carelessness can increase the likelihood of these events. As noted above, the Chera-Turi-Maheshwari region is already fragile and susceptible to landslides, which exacerbate soil erosion and sedimentation in a region that is already highly fragile given its geological conditions.

### 36. Key underlying drivers of deforestation are briefly described in the following Table:

#### Table 3. Underlying drivers of deforestation and forest degradation

| Factors | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Economic/ Financial | * Poverty limits local peoples investments into improved land use practices * Lack of access to credit for small farmers (e.g., NPPs) |
| Policy | * Lack of opportunities available of substitute products or sustainable production systems |
| Socio-political | * Weak land tenure * Lack of incentives and motives for the adoption of sustainable practices * Low level of education in rural areas * Low level of awareness of impacts and alternative measures * Inequality |
| Demographic | * High population growth |
| Technological | * Poor awareness of improved practices and technologies * Low productivity of existing production systems |

### ENVISAGED CLIMATE-RESILIENT DEVELOPMENT PATHWAY

37. The baseline situation demonstrates the vulnerability of the Chera-Turi-Maheshwari region, and the need to support vulnerable communities in adapting to climate change impacts. Given the complex interactions among the main biophysical zones within the project area, an ecosystem-based approach should be applied that promotes integrated management, conservation and restoration.

38. Table 4 provides an overview of key practices that are needed within the project area to respond to the climate change impacts identified in Section C.2. The measures that are proposed for each biophysical zone as a response to responding to the specific climate change challenges outlined in Section C.1 – are explained in greater detail in Feasibility Study Appendix A, Figure 5 (either further below or on the next page) and the proposed adoption of these practices would be supported by the project under Component 2 of the Funding Proposal summarizes an overview of climate change impacts, baseline conditions and project-promoted measures designated for each biophysical zone.

| Chera Hills | * Ecosystem-based approaches: Soil stabilization in landscape susceptible zones, forest restoration and sustainable management, bio-engineering soil and water conservation infrastructure to stabilize river banks and reduce flows | Reduced risk of erosion, reduced exposure to flooding, particularly in downstream areas. |

### BARIERS AND CHALLENGES

39. Despite efforts by the Government of Nepal and the development community, a number of challenges and barriers continue to inhibit the transition toward a climate-resilient, sustainable and low-emissions development pathway in the Churia region. The following Table summarizes these barriers, as well as how the project will address them.

| Barrier/Challenge | Approach to Address Barrier |
| --- | --- |
| Poverty, inappropriate land management practices, the lack of alternative livelihood activities to implement SNRM | * Provision of technical support and resources to cover up-front costs of implementing SNRM in 2017-2018. * Implementation of a Community Ecosystem Restoration Plan (CERP) will ensure former fallow fields are restored as part of SNRM. * Promoted activities are designed to further address sectoral barriers, including the lack of technical and financial support for women and men farmers, limited access to market information, and weak linkages between CBOs and government institutions for forestry. Build out of best-practices and lessons learned. CBOs will be supported in developing business plans and marketing strategies to increase their knowledge and skills. Promoted measures are economically viable and can be scaled up with the aim of increasing revenue streams from land resident use practices from increased revenue streams. |

## C3. Project / Programme Description

### 40. Through the Building a Resilient Church Region in Nepal (BRCRIN) project, GCF grant financing and co-financing will be used to support the Government of Nepal (GoN) in implementing its climate change adaptation and reduction emissions from deforestation and forest degradation.

- The Project will address the specific barriers inhibiting Church communities in the Churia-Terai region from adopting low-carbon development pathways, including low-emissions development pathway.
  - And will equip them to continue pursuing this pathway after project closure. This is a critical step for climate change adaptation.

### 41. The Project will focus on the Churia-Terai-Madhesh region of Nepal, in particular 26 of the most vulnerable river systems within Provinces 1, 2 and 3, where over 3.2 million people live.
- The main beneficiaries will include representatives of provincial and local government authorities, as well as at least 700 GCF Beneficiaries (comprised of approximately 200,681 households, 963,268 people).

### Activity interventions

- **Activity interventions** are targeted for different biophysical zones and contexts, with a focus on the Churia, Bhuvan and Udham Singh Tera areas.

### Table 6. Selection of geographic locations, sites and beneficiaries

| Level | Type | Criteria / process | Stage / timing |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Geographic locations** | | | Completed during project design. |
| | | Among the 7 Provinces in Nepal, Provinces 1, 2 and 3 were selected during the project design stage as the overall geographic focus for this project. The selection was based on the following criteria: (i) vulnerability to climate change; (ii) vulnerability in the Eastern Himalayas (ICIMOD, 2008), e.g., vulnerability of agriculture, water resources and biodiversity; (iii) diversity of ecosystems and relative vulnerability of biodiversity, water, ecosystems, and livelihoods. | |
| | | Due to the inter-connected (upstream and downstream) nature of climate change challenges and unregulated natural resource management practices in Nepal’s river systems, and the associated impacts on people and the environment, specific sites within each river system were selected as the most appropriate geographic unit to guide project planning and implementation. The 26 river systems that collectively comprise the Project Area(s) were selected and prioritized for interventions and support under this project. These sites were selected based on a multi-criteria analysis which included an analysis of the river systems’ vulnerability, their relative importance in terms of water resources, as well as the extent of natural resource (including forest) degradation. The selection process is described further in Annex 2: Feasibility Study—Appendix D. | |
| **River systems** | | | Completed during project design. |
| | | Specific sites for EFS within each of the 26 river systems (which collectively comprise the Project Area(s)) will be determined in part through the Critical Ecosystem Partnership Fund (CERP) process. The CERP will identify (inter alia) ecosystem restoration priorities, as well as specific sites and locations where EFS will be established for each river system through FFS. The CERP will also specify the number of FFS (and associated land area) to be established in each river system. | |
| **Sites** | | | CERPs will be developed in each of the 26 river systems during Project Year 1. Selection of sites and locations for EFS within each river system will be completed by the end of FY3. |
| | | Specific sites for FFS within each of the 26 river systems are defined in the CERPs. FAO, as the Executing Entity, will recruit a consultant to identify specific sites and establish FFS within each river system. After selection of sites and locations is completed by the end of FY3, the FAO will approve the final selection of sites for FFS based on the following criteria: |
| | | * Suitability of each site’s agro-ecological conditions to run Farmer Field Schools (FFS) |
| | | * Suitability of the river system and practices present at relevant CERP (note this tentative assessment will be made by FAO technical experts). |

- **The farmer/landowner must consent to the use of their land as an FFS site.** (See page 73.)

### Sites for tree nurseries

- The Project will support the establishment of a network of decentralized small-scale tree nurseries which offer ready access to tree seedlings at a low cost and are located in close proximity to the relevant CBOs (and other CBOs where there is presence of FFS sites) or other CBOs, where there is presence of FFS sites. The CBOs must be assessed as possessing the potential capacity to sustain the nursery operation.
- **The Project will support the establishment of nurseries in PTZ-2 and PTZ-3.**
- **The priority locations and types of structures to be built in each river system will be determined by MoTFTE, Koshi River basin conservation office, respective municipalities, and other relevant stakeholders.** The project will provide technical assistance for the design and construction of nurseries.
- **MoTFTE will approve the final selection of sites based on the following criteria:**
  - Suitability of each structure and its selected site to maximize resilience benefits in response to the climate change impacts/challenges identified by the CBOs (and other CBOs where there is presence of FFS sites) or other stakeholders.
  - The type of structure (e.g., check dams, gully stabilization structures).
  - Whether privately owned, community owned or government owned (consent to use of land must be obtained from relevant owners).

### Criteria for nursery sites

- **Nurseries will preferably be hosted by CBOs or other CBOs where there is presence of FFS sites.** In the absence and/or other CBOs, where there is no presence of FFS sites, nurseries can also be established in PTZ-2 and PTZ-3.
- **The Project will support the establishment of nurseries subject to a consultation process with relevant stakeholders (MoTFTE, Koshi River basin conservation office, etc.).** The CBOs or other CBOs where there is presence of FFS sites can host the premises of the Divisional Forest Office (DFO) under MoTFTE as host premises.

**with local municipalities and Private Forest Owners Associations, validated by MoFTE and FAO experts, and finalized through dialogue with CBOs.**

* **Area-based Natural Resources Management (ANRM) project:** will support tools/equipment and logistical needs for the implementation of ANRM in forest land. Preliminary areas for ANR sites will be identified through the GCF process. The ANR sites will focus on forest areas of any management category (CF, LF, EFA, etc.) that are not under active logging or other activities defined within the past 20 years; were not converted to agricultural use in the last five years; and are not subject to flooding and landslides. Sites will be proposed by MoFTE, validated through a dialogue with CBOs, and finalized through a dialogue with CBOs in accordance with the principles of FPR.

**Community-Based Organizations (CBOs):**

* **All 750 CBO Beneficiaries:** will be identified and selected by MoFTE (after consulting with MoFTE) under Sub-Component 2. This includes all 557 Community Forest Management Groups (CFMGs) that fall within the Chulu and Bhairave zones (upstream areas of the 10 river basins). It also includes all 11 CFMs that are in Project Area. It also includes all 40 Leasehold Forest Management Plans (LFMPs) that are held by the Indigenous Peoples’ Organization (IPO) in each of the three provinces; a comprehensive list will be provided to MoFTE. The remaining CBO Beneficiaries out of the above will be identified and selected from among the Community Forest Users Groups (CFUGs), Public Land Forest Users Groups (PLFUGs). The total number of FIDs and CFMs that are in Project Area is at least 50; the total number will be determined by MoFTE. The CBO Beneficiaries will be identified based on their location in the upstream areas of the 20 river basins. PLFUGs are not yet included as they have not been finalized.

* **All 750 CBO Beneficiaries:** will benefit from activities under Component 1 of the Project. The degree to which each CBO Benefit will be based on the initiatives and/or projects that are already in place within the communities, and whether or not near vulnerable and degraded landscapes may be involved in multiple components of the Project.

* **All 750 CBO Beneficiaries:** will also identify the research CBO Beneficiaries to be involved in the development of a project-specific forest management plan. The CBOs will provide strategic strategies to the planning activities in the relevant areas.

* **All CFUGs, Community Forest Users Groups (CFUGs):** will be supported by existing forest management operational plans and improve sustainable forest management practices through training and capacity building.

* **Approximately 250 CBO Beneficiaries:** (out of the total 750) will receive in-depth support for their forest management activities under Sub-Component 1. The CBO Beneficiaries to be selected for this support will be those that are identified as having a high potential for success within the framework of Sub-Activity 1 (2.1), and will also include the three (3) CFM groups. A list of these CBOs will be provided by MoFTE, acting through MoFTE, after consultation with MoFTE and FAO experts.

**CERPs:** will be developed in each of the forest management systems in PY2:

* **Formalization of existing CERPs:** will take place in PY1.
* **Support for developing climate resilient activities:** will also take place in PY-3.

Professional staff (from MFTE, MAQLM and other ministries) who will benefit from training and capacity building support under this Project (i.e. under the CERPS) will be identified by the Executing Entities according to the following criteria [Professional Beneficiaries].

The selection of beneficiaries will be made by the Executing Entities responsible for the respective CERPS and will involve individuals who are familiar with the activity and its participants’ responsibilities and expertise.

CERPs will identify critically vulnerable locations within the 26 river systems, where climate change is expected to result in a loss or degradation of livelihoods and/or loss of land. The technical advisory team of MFTE and FAO will provide technical assistance to CERPs in identifying these areas for training.

The selection process for these areas will be undertaken by the CERPs on a total of 260 training events in the Project Area (approx. 10 per river system), provided that the CERPs have sufficient resources and capacity to do so. The area will be asked to propose training participants (“Farmer Beneficiaries”) for each training event, which selection will be made by the Executing Entities.

- Experience of the individuals’ current livelihood strategies and farming practices to the project-promoted practices.
- Individuals’ access to land on which they apply the CRFPs; and
- Individuals living in additional households within their community on the project-promoted CRFPs.

For each training event, the pool of nominated participants will include at least 10% individuals from vulnerable communities and associated CBRMs directly affected by these issues. The selection process for these individuals will be undertaken with a relevant CBRM.

The MFTE/MAQLM review team (on PPS) and GPPs (on PPS) will review nominees for each training to confirm their nominations are consistent with the project’s objectives, including the identification of vulnerable communities and associated CBRMs. Final selection of Farmer Beneficiaries based on MAQLM reviews.

CERPs will identify (i) locations within 26 river systems where scope exists for CRFPs to be implemented; (ii) individuals who are familiar with the livelihood strategies and practices applied by their communities and associated CBRMs most directly affected by these issues, and (iii) individuals living in additional households within their community on the project-promoted CRFPs.

For the establishment of household-level fodder tree and grass nurseries, on-farm agroforestry plantation, and for training and establishment of small-scale livestock enterprises, relevant CBO Beneficiaries will be selected based on the following criteria: • They have at least one livestock unit (i.e. own, cattle, buffalo) that can be used to establish a fodder tree or grass nursery; • They have sufficient income to cover the operations and maintenance costs for the establishment of the fodder tree or grass nursery over a period (this assessment will be made by FAO and MoFTE experts); • Higher dependence on livelihood from forest areas; and • Current lack of access to modern forms of energy or electricity.

Nominations will be made by Forest-related CBO Beneficiaries in consultation with MoFTE and MoCLM, and confirmed by the Forest Training Committee. Nominations will be reviewed according to the above criteria and the principles of FPIC.


## Forest Training Beneficiaries

The Project will deliver 180 trainings on Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) for members from forest-related CBO Beneficiaries and other forest sector stakeholders in the project area. These trainings will include representatives from all forest-related CBO Beneficiaries. Trainees will be instructed to nominate equal numbers of male and female participants, with a minimum of 10% female participation. The training will start at the beginning of each calendar year.

The Project will also deliver 122 trainings on sustainable management of forest resources for members from forest-related CBO Beneficiaries, with trainees nominated by the participating CBOs. Trainees will be instructed to nominate equal numbers of male and female participants, and ensure that at least one trainee from each CBO is a woman. The training will start in January 2019.

Forest restoration and plantation Activities, as well as other tree planting activities, will be supported by the Project through the hiring of local labourers to support tree planting. To recruit labourers for such Activities (under Sub-Component 3), the Project will engage with CBO Beneficiaries to ensure that members act as labourers in (and benefit from) these Activities. CBO Beneficiaries will be instructed to nominate equal numbers of male and female participants, and ensure that at least one trainee from each CBO is a woman. The training will start in January 2019.

## Project Objective

The project objective will be achieved through the following Components. Figure 6 presents the sequencing of the Components and Activities, how the Components are complementary and collectively contribute to achieving tangible impacts in terms of climate-resilient SNRM practices (e.g., improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers and support more widespread adoption) of project-promoted farming, land use and forestry practices after project closure.

### Project Components

a) **Component 1:** Scaling up climate-resilient Sustainable Natural Resource Management (SNRM).

b) **Component 2:** Strengthening institutions and planning for climate-resilient SNRM.

c) **Component 3:** Improving knowledge, awareness and local capacity for climate-resilient SNRM.

## Figure 6: Project components, impact streams and post-project sustainability

| Component 1 | Scaling up climate-resilient Sustainable Natural Resource Management (SNRM) |
| --- | --- |
| **Component 2** | Strengthening institutions and planning for climate-resilient SNRM. |
| **Component 3** | Improving knowledge, awareness and local capacity for climate-resilient SNRM. |

### Project Implementation Period

Under Component 1, the project will support activities focused on scaling up proven climate-resilient SNRM approaches to strengthen ecosystem stability and resilience in vulnerable river systems. These interventions will focus on climate-resilient land use practices (e.g., agroforestry, tree planting, and improved livestock management) that protect critical ecosystems and tree planting on non-forest land to build resilience and reduce pressure on natural forests for fodder collection.

The project will support the development of a SNRM action plan in each targeted river system. The action plans will be developed with input from government and community stakeholders during the process of developing the project proposal, and will include specific details about the type of interventions that will be implemented in each of the 26 targeted river systems, with the specific constellation of support (upstream and downstream dynamics) to be defined in consultation with government and community stakeholders during the process of developing the action plans.

### Post-project period

Beneficiary CBOs will continue to apply project-promoted practices after project closure, drawing on support from the SNRM Action Plan. Local extension and advisory services continue to provide technical assistance to beneficiaries who have benefited from the project (eg) subject to other project funding.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal | Page 29 of 117

### Critical Ecosystem Restoration Plans (CERPs) under Activity 2.1.2 - An activity that will precede and guide field-level interventions under Component 1.

To expand the coverage, scale-up, and scaling up of climate-resilient SNRM activities within Component 2 focus on strengthening local and provincial capacities, supporting operational planning at the local level within each project's 20-25 targeted river systems. At the sub-provincial level, this Component will support the development of CERPs in all but one of the 28 targeted river systems (the remaining one is the Limpopo River). This Component will balance top-down integrated spatial planning with participatory land use planning at the local level and at the CERP level. To achieve these goals, this Component will support the development of CERPs in each of the 28 targeted river systems. These CERPs (and the associated SNRM activities) will be developed, tested, refined, and validated with the relevant CBDS within each river system, thereby ensuring full community buy-in for interventions to be delivered under this Component.

### Project Activities

1. Project activities will further enhance awareness, knowledge and communication on climate risks, climate-resilient SNRM concepts, and climate change adaptation strategies at all levels of society.
2. This will include the extension of reach, extension services, and trainings for trainers to implement climate-resilient SNRM approaches. This will ensure that communities are equipped with the necessary skills and knowledge to adapt their land use practices in a way that provides users understand climate change threats, low-carbon and climate-resilient land use practices.
3. The establishment of an innovative monitoring system for climate change monitoring, knowledge management and dissemination, and improve climate-informed decision making planning.

### Components: Scaling up Climate-Resilient Sustainable Natural Resource Management (SRM)

#### Component 1: Focus on implementing and scaling up of climate-resilient SNRM practices to increase the resilience of ecosystems and local households within 26 river systems that are vulnerable to climate change. CBDS beneficiaries and their members will be supported in the implementation of climate-resilient SNRM practices, including climate-resilient agricultural practices on 60,963 ha, restoration of 202,237 ha of critical forest, wetland and protected areas, and the establishment of 15,840 ha of new forests. This will also include the establishment of 23,800 ha as new forest systems (including enrichment planting on shrub land, conservation and commercial plantations and other forms of afforestation). These measures are expected to enhance the resilience of ecosystems and the provision of key ecosystem services.

#### Component 2: Promote climate-resilient land use practices that will help address the proximate and underlying drivers of deforestation and land degradation as well as improved carbon sequestration in areas under forest and tree cover. Measures will have positive impacts on biodiversity conservation and agricultural productivity. Since poverty is a major barrier to the adoption of climate-resilient land use practices, this Component will provide training and extension services for local CBDSs so that they are equipped with knowledge and skills to provide technical support to farmers in their communities.

#### Sub-Component 1: Climate-resilient SNRM practices are adopted. This Sub-component will work with farmers and other land users in the 28 targeted river systems (the "Project Area") to scale up the adoption of farming and other land use practices that are climate-resilient, including agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and improved livestock management. The Sub-component will directly train and support farmers from relevant CBDSs, equip them to return to their communities to teach and train other farming households on project-promoted practices. This Sub-component will also establish crucial local

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 30 OF 117

### Infrastructure in each river system to enhance resilience against climate change-induced erosion, sedimentation and flooding risks.

#### Activity 1.1.1 - Establish Farmer Field Schools (FFS) in the Project Area to promote climate-resilient land use.
This Activity will establish 120 FFSs in the Project Area to encourage the use of climate-resilient farming practices. The FFSs will be established by government field staff and will be used to deliver trainings on climate-resilient farming practices to Farmer Beneficiaries under Activity 1.1.2, as well as to other interested farmers and communities in the Project Area.

The FFS technical expert recruited by FAO will design the overall approach to this Activity, including the development and supervision of agreements with national service providers to identify sites, establish FFSs and organize the procurement and delivery of equipment and training materials. The FFS technical expert will also supervise the FFS technical expert to deliver Training-of-Trainers (ToT) for MiTFTE and MACML field staff, as well as to other interested farmers and communities in the Project Area.

The FFS technical expert will identify the equipment needs for all 120 FFSs and organize the procurement and delivery of this equipment. The FFS technical expert will also identify the training materials needed by each FFS and organize their procurement and delivery, including the development of agreements. The Provincial Project Management Units (PPMUs) will cover the travel and logistical costs for MiTFTE and MACML field staff to attend ToT events in Addis Ababa.

The PPMUs will also provide funding for the establishment of 120 FFSs based on the FFS training modules. This is anticipated to require a total of 120 WWI, by teams of 3 field staff, to establish each FFS and train Farmer Beneficiaries in climate-resilient farming practices. This will ensure the long-term ability of all FFS to deliver all models to local farmers and communities as required.

#### Activity 1.1.2 - Train farmers to adopt and apply climate-resilient land use practices.
This Activity will train Farmer Beneficiaries on climate-resilient land use practices (CRLPs) that are most relevant to the challenges and conditions within their communities (see Appendix A.6.4.1). The CRLP training programme will be delivered by government field staff, and Farmer Beneficiaries will not only be equipped to apply relevant CRLPs on their own land, but also be able to teach and train other farmers in their communities.

The CRLP training programme will focus on building resilience of farmer households against many of the climate change impacts outlined in Section 1.3 (above). The CRLP training programme will be delivered by government field staff in each of the Project Area communities. Based on the results of the Critical Ecosystem Restoration Plan (CERP) developed under Activity 1.2, Farmer Beneficiaries who are identified as being most vulnerable to climate change impacts and identify the CERB beneficiaries to be targeted for capacity building efforts and the training approaches to be employed will be selected.

The CRLP training programme will also focus on identifying the specific equipment needs for support to the CRFP training programme will be identified, and the procurement and delivery of this equipment organized. The PPMUs will cover the travel and logistical costs for MiTFTE and MACML field staff to attend ToT events (approximately 10 per river system), each of which will be supported by 3 government field staff and train approximately 30 Farmer Beneficiaries in total. Activity 1.1.2 is expected to train about 7,500 Farming Beneficiaries.

d) Activity 1.1.4 – Construct check dams, gully stabilization measures and other local infrastructure to enhance water retention and reduce erosion in the Churun River Basin. This activity will construct local structures (described in more detail in Annex 2: Feasibility Study—Appendix A2.2). It will play a key role in improving the overall resilience of the river system, which is one of the top limiting dynamics in each of the 25 river systems that collectively compose the Project Area. ### a) Activity 1.3.1 – Establish and support the operation of 52 multi-purpose tree nurseries to serve the project’s immediate needs of CLCC and other forest restoration activities

The Project will establish and operate 52 multi-purpose tree nurseries throughout the Project Area (specifications for project-supported nurseries are available in Annex 3.2). The nurseries will produce seedlings to support continued planting and restoration activities beyond project closure, i.e., in line with CERPS and other forest restoration activities. The Project Area would struggle to procure the necessary seedlings in the future, which would risk undermining local buy-in and long-term sustainability of the project’s forest restoration efforts.

To accomplish this, the project staff will procure the equipment and materials needed to establish two nurseries in each of the 26 river systems (see Table 5.1). The Project will follow a procurement process outlined in Table 6.4 above. Project staff will also acquire material inputs (e.g., poly bags, soil, sand) and labor for nursery establishment and maintenance as needed.

The project will establish nurseries to produce at least 50,000 seedlings per year. Nurseries will be operated by local forest offices, and project staff will provide technical support and training on a regular basis. The Project will also ensure that nurseries meet their annual budgets by project closure. Nurseries will be designed in line with GESI principles, promoting equal participation of women and men as well as other marginalized groups.

### b) Activity 1.3.2 – Establish and maintain 1,300 ha of forest plantations to enhance resilience

The Activity will establish and support maintenance of several different forest plantations in the Project Area that will contribute to building resilience and mitigating climate change, while also equipping local stakeholders to promote sustainable land use practices.

i. Demonstration plantations: Project staff will establish demonstration plantations to expand tree cover and improve ecosystem services within the Project Area. The project will work with communities and vulnerable forest users in the Project Area to promote sustainable plantation management beyond project closure. To accomplish this, the project staff will train 20 MAFTEF staff at each of the three provinces (160 Total Professional Beneficiaries). These MAFTEF staff will then provide technical support and training on a regular basis.

The project will procure materials and seed stock needed to establish 1,300 ha of demonstration plantations of native species. The Project will also ensure that demonstration plantations meet their annual budgets by project closure. Demonstration plantations will contribute to enabling trained MAFTEF staff to promote and support sustainable land use practices.

ii. Public land forests on river bank sites: Building on the priorities identified in the CERD developed under Activity 2.1.2, project staff will work with vulnerable and marginalized communities to plant grassland trees along river banks within the Project Area. This will contribute to stabilizing these river banks, which in turn will build resilience for within the Project Area.

The project will procure materials and seed stock needed to establish 30 ha of public land forests on river bank sites. The CBO Beneficiaries with whom project staff work will establish these plantations under a formal agreement with their respective Municipality offices. Project staff will further support these CBO Beneficiaries in the establishment, maintenance, and monitoring of these plantations using local resources.

iii. Public land forests on community land: Building on the priorities identified in the CERD developed under Activity 2.1.2, project staff will work with vulnerable and marginalized communities to establish multipurpose trees in open community land, primarily in the upstream Chura hills and Bhawne zone in each of the 25 river systems. This expanded forest cover will enhance resilience against climate

### c) Activity 1.3.3 - Train government field staff and CBOs on Forest Landscape Restoration, and support CBOs to implement Assisted Natural Regeneration on 15,990 ha.

This activity will assist CBO Beneficiaries in implementing ANR on 15,990 ha of degraded forest land within the targeted river systems, in line with the priorities identified in the CERPs developed under Activity 2.1.2. This will enable CBOs to establish and maintain a diverse range of tree species that are adapted to local conditions while also generating significant mitigation benefits. Under Activity 1.3.3, the project will not only support CBO Beneficiaries (Forest Trainees) in implementing ANR on their own land but will also provide technical assistance to government to continue scaling up such approaches after project closure, in line with their CERPs and drawing on the lessons learned from this project.

The project will approach the activities outlined below, technical specifications for which are included in Annex 2 - Feasibility Study Report (FSR) and Project Implementation Plan (PIP).

1. Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) Project staff will prepare training materials on FLR, which they will then use to train 24 provincial forestry field staff (Professional Beneficiaries) through 12 training events in the targeted river systems. The project will also provide technical assistance and mentoring to FLR Project staff as well as organize 180 training events for members from relevant CBO Beneficiaries (Forest Trainees) on FLR, which they will be trained at least once a year by their respective communities. Approximately 30 Forest Training Beneficiaries will be trained at each training event.

2. Assisted Natural Regeneration (ANR): Building on the priorities and areas identified in the CERPs developed under Activity 2.1.2, project staff will work with relevant CBO Beneficiaries (SST CFBGs and SST CBOs) to implement ANR on 15,990 ha of degraded forest land within the targeted river systems, in particular on the Churun Hills and Bravo zones in the 25 targeted river systems. Project staff will participate in the development of ANR methodologies and implementation plans that are subject to local context. Project staff will also provide technical assistance and mentoring to CBO Beneficiaries as well as collaborate with relevant CBO Beneficiaries, which may include (depending on the needs of specific sites):

   1. Plantation for protection and stabilization: To reduce degradation and promote the ecosystem services and sustainable use of forests, communities will be supported to establish plantations in degraded forest land using appropriate tree species. The project will ensure that measures to control grazing or forest product extraction do not disproportionately affect these areas.
   
   2. Measures to accelerate natural forest recovery (e.g., direct seeding, planting seedlings) in degraded forest land: Communities will be supported with the establishment of enrichment plantations to restore over-exploited forest-dominated ecosystems, especially along river banks and other degraded sites.

   3. Measures to support natural regeneration will involve promoting tree seedlings and favoring species within a forest landscape, supporting multi-use forest management plans, with equal consideration or social, ecological, and economic functions of forests.

**Activity 1.3.4 – Project Component 5: Strengthening Institutions and Planning for Climate-resilient SNRM**

This Activity aims to support the establishment of tree cover on over 16,500 ha of woodlands to enhance ecosystem resilience, improve fuelwood and timber availability in downstream communities, and provide additional benefits such as improved water quality and flood control. The project will work with local communities, government departments, and broader ecosystem services that are essential to the overall resilience of river systems.

This Activity will also provide additional sources of fuelwood and timber for downstream communities, thereby reducing pressure on remaining woodlands in the catchment area. The project will prioritize areas identified by the CERPS to guide the identification of landowners with whom to collaborate (in line with the selection criteria outlined in Table 1). The project will work closely with local communities, including scale landowners and other successful forest owners in neighboring districts to enhance their interest in engaging in SNRM. The project will also provide training for community members on how to manage woodlands on their land, while project staff will procure the necessary seedlings and government field staff will provide technical support.

The project will also work with local communities in 26 river systems (to be selected according to the criteria and process outlined in Table 1) to establish tree cover on over 16,500 ha of woodlands. The project will ensure that households in each of the 26 river systems (to be selected according to the criteria and process outlined in Table 1) are provided with seedlings for their own landholdings.

The government remains committed to continue providing extension support for such planting, further contributing to the potential to scale up tree planting on woodlands beyond project closure.


### COMPONENT 2: STRENGTHENING INSTITUTIONS AND PLANNING FOR CLIMATE-RESILIENT SNRM

5. Component 2 is designed as a precursor for the investments in Component 1 and will support the necessary planning for interventions to be implemented under Component 1. The project will work with provincial, local government, and community stakeholders to build their capacities to understand and apply climate-resilient land use. Given Nepal’s ongoing political transition, Provincial and Local governments have new responsibilities over natural resource management and there is a need to build awareness on climate change impacts and the importance of applying climate-resilient land use planning into local strategies and plans.

Local CSOs also play an important role in managing local resources and will be supported by this Component. The project will work with these stakeholders to develop sustainable natural resource management approaches that can strengthen their adaptive capacities and overall resilience to climate change.


6. Ultimately, this Component will address climate change risks by improving awareness of threats and building capacities to cope with them. This will be achieved through a series of activities aimed at raising awareness on climate change impacts and the importance of applying climate-resilient land use planning into local strategies and plans.

This Component will also support the implementation of the SNRM Strategy, which is outlined in Component 2, which focuses on improving knowledge and understanding of climate change risks. This will be achieved through strengthening extension services and training and improving access to information on climate change impacts and the enhancement of adaptive capacities.


#### Sub-Component 1: Strengthening Institutions for Climate-resilient SNRM

This Sub-component will support government stakeholders and CSOs beneficiaries to identify and assess climate change risks and resource degradation with a view to developing appropriate strategies and plans. This Sub-component will also build capacity in the use of SNRM tools, including the SNRM Strategy.

In this Sub-component will also build the capacity of these actors to further monitor local risks and resources in the future. The project will work closely with government stakeholders and CSOs beneficiaries to identify and assess climate change risks and resource degradation with a view to developing appropriate strategies and plans.

#### a) Activity 2.1.2 - Develop and validate Critical Ecosystem Restoration Plans (CERPs) for all of the 26 targeted river systems in Province 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6

Project staff will work with provincial governments and key stakeholders to develop and validate CERPs, which will enable evidence-based planning for interventions under climate change. The CERP process is a participatory, multi-stakeholder approach that enables coordinated investments in climate-resilient SNRM in the river systems. The CERP planning process will draw on insights from research from relevant national entities, including the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM), as well as other relevant stakeholders. Project staff will conduct a vulnerability analysis for each of the 26 targeted river systems to help identify climate change risks within each river system, critical biodiversity hotspots, and key social groups that are vulnerable to these risks. The CERP process will also build on existing knowledge about the importance of maintaining intact forest ecosystems in each river basin; priority interventions to be delivered (under Component 1) to enable short-term resilience building for all targeted river systems. Project staff will also conduct a vulnerability analysis for inclusion/enclusion in prioritized interventions in each river system. Building on this analysis, project staff will prioritize key biodiversity hotspots and social groups that are vulnerable to climate change risks within the identified priorities with key stakeholders and develop this into a Critical Ecosystem Restoration Plan (CERP).

Once the CERPs are validated, project staff will work with provincial governments (through district FFPs) and relevant national entities (e.g., DHM) as well as local communities. The CERPs are locally-relevant and appropriate for each river system. Each CERP is developed in close collaboration with key stakeholders at the community level, including CBOS and other local stakeholders in each river system are supportive of the priorities and plans outlined in their respective CERPs. Project staff will also work closely with provincial governments to ensure that, where appropriate, including by addressing locally-specific social and environmental risks and capacity gaps. In no doing, project staff will work with provincial governments to develop a provincial-level SNRM plan for each river system. This plan will include the priorities outlined in the CERPs as well as other priority interventions (e.g., community forest, livelihood forestry, collaborative forestry, public land forests, private forest, primary forest, among others). Project staff will also work with provincial governments to ensure that the provincial-level SNRM plan includes the priorities and needs of vulnerable groups (e.g. women, Dalits and indigenous peoples). In total, project staff will work with provincial governments to develop a provincial-level SNRM plan for each river system based on the CERPs and the specific interventions prioritized therein. Project staff will then discuss the community-empowerment aspects of these plans with communities in each river basin (through 6 provincial workshops) to secure feedback on the planning processes and government validation of the plans.

#### b) Activity 2.1.3 - Support provincial governments in Provinces 1, 2 and 3 and plan increase resilience to projected climate change-related extreme events

Due to an increasing risk of climate change-induced extreme weather events (e.g., floods, droughts), project staff will work with provincial governments to assess these risks and how best to support enhanced preparedness – both within the government and in community level. Project staff will organize a series of workshops for provincial-level disaster management staff ("Professional Beneficiaries") and support them to develop corresponding risk management plans. Project staff will also provide training on climate change adaptation planning, including vulnerability analysis and local government staff, during which participants will learn about river-system specific risks (drawing on analysis from the CERPs) as well as how to identify and prioritize interventions that can reduce these risks in the future. These trainings are also expected to draw on insights from DHM staff (among others), both to inform the training content and to provide technical support for the provincial-level disaster management level authorities responsible for planning and delivering support in this area. Participants will also be trained on how to access information from the Chitwa Knowledge Centre in their respective provinces (established under Activity 2.3.3), and how this important resource can facilitate more analysis of climate change risks in

### Sub-Component 2 - Capacity Building for Community-Based Organizations (CBOs) to develop scale-up climate-resilient SNRM

This sub-component will work with local stakeholders and government agencies to provide support for climate-resilient SNRM in their river systems, and address other legal and institutional barriers that otherwise prevent the effective management of natural resources. The project will also help communities shift their resource use toward more climate-resilient and low-emission pathways.

#### Activity 2.2.1 - Train CBO Beneficiaries on land use planning and assist them to mainstream gender equality in CBO management

Under this activity, project staff will strengthen the technical capacities of CBO Beneficiaries to better understand climate change risks and how these can be managed through improved SNRM practices. Training sessions will include discussions on insights gained during the CERP process under Activity 2.1.2). Training will also include sessions on GES principles and gender equality, with a focus on how these can be mainstreamed into CBO management to promote greater understanding among males CBO members of women’s roles. In so doing, throughout the Project Area, project staff will work closely with CBOs to ensure that they are well positioned for the project closure. To accomplish this, project staff will start by recruiting a service provider to organize a training workshop on land use planning and gender equality in CBO management. The training will be effectively organized. Once LRPCs have been trained, project staff will organize at least 250 community-level workshops for the 750 CBO Beneficiaries (with ≥3 CBOs per workshop), at which LRPCs will train CBO members on land use planning and gender equality in CBO management. The training will focus on adaptation needs and strategies to build resilience at the local level. Such an approach is based on multi-stakeholder engagement with communities, including women’s groups, youth organizations, indigenous peoples, DAs and members from other marginalized groups. After the initial workshops and trainings, project staff will organize a series of follow-up meetings and training sessions (as outlined in Table 26) and recruit a qualified service provider, trained on gender quality and social inclusion, to support these CBOs to develop CBO management plans that effectively mainstream support for climate-resilient SNRM.

Component 3 will improve awareness of climate change threats and build capacities to continuously monitor climate change and vulnerability at the provincial (local rural municipality) and CBO level. 61.
