### Activity 3.2.1 - Raise awareness on climate-resilient SNRM through local schools, media and intra-regional exchange.

Under this Activity, project staff will conduct awareness raising campaigns to promote climate-resilient SNRM practices in the Project Area. The campaign will be conducted throughout the Project Area. Project staff will start by recruiting a qualified service provider to develop a knowledge compendium of local and indigenous knowledge based on these local-level consultations, and subsequently organize training workshops for teachers and school administrators. The service provider will also collect information from civil society organizations (CSOs), development cooperation partners and government officials. Based on the collected information, project staff will recruit another qualified service provider to develop curricula on climate-resilient SNRM for high school students. Project staff will then use these curricula in a series of training sessions for the eco-club members. To further raise awareness within the Project Area, project staff will recruit service providers to organize awareness raising campaigns and trainings on climate-resilient SNRM through local schools. These radio programmes will be adapted to the local contexts in which they will be broadcasted by the service provider. The service provider will also collect information from CSOs, development cooperation partners and government officials about indigenous knowledge gathered under Activity 3.1.1. Finally, to enhance awareness of climate-resilient SNRM among young people, whose buy-in and support are critical for the long-term sustainability of transforming land-use practices into natural resource management in the Project Area, project staff will organize exchange visits within Nepal for local government authorities (‘Professional Beneficiaries’) to demonstrate good practices on climate-resilient SNRM. The service provider will also develop a series of awareness materials which promote such practices.

### Sub-Component 3.1.2 - Ensure that all extension services are equipped to promote climate-resilient SNRM

This Sub-Component will ensure that recent projections on climate change impacts and appropriate response measures (including local and indigenous knowledge) are integrated into the training modules for extension workers, and that trainers and trainers’ trainers are equipped to promote climate-resilient SNRM, and can scale up the provision of climate change-informed extension services to ensure local communities, farmers and other land users are able to benefit from such services during and beyond the project.

#### Activity 3.2.1 - Develop 10 modules on climate-resilient SNRM to be used by extension workers.

Project staff will develop a series of locally-adapted training modules to be used when training farmers and other land users to adopt project-proposed practices, including local and indigenous knowledge. The service provider will also recruit government personnel and extension workers during and beyond the project, including for training on farmer field school techniques, climate change adaptation strategies and communication in multiple languages to ensure they cater to local needs. Project staff will ensure these modules draw on the feasibility work done under Sub-Component 3.1.1 (see Activity 2.2) and the collection of local and indigenous knowledge (under Activity 3.1.1). The modules will outline the training and extension processes for rural communities and beneficiaries including implementation planning, self-learning, quality assurance and incentive structures, and will support the facilitation of all key interventions under Component

b) Activity 3.2 – Enhance and deliver quality extension services on climate-resilient SNRM to households.

The extension system plays a crucial role in shaping how farmers, forest users and communities use land and natural resources. As climate change continues to impact livelihoods and ecosystems, it is therefore essential to scale up the delivery of high-quality, climate-informed extension services in all 28 provinces. The Project Area will provide training for approximately 100 Master Trainers (MTTs), which they will train government extension workers and LURPs (“Professional Beneficiaries”) to understand and apply the climate-informed SNRM modules. These MTs will be trained by the end of 2023, with a total of approximately 2,340 extension workers and LURPs (about 30 at each of the 78 training events) under this Activity.

Activity 3.2.1 – Establish a provincial climate-informed extension service delivery system in the Project Area: MoTE’s co-financing will be used to engage the services of provincial-level extension workers (on secondment from the Ministry of Agriculture and Natural Resources) to deliver the climate-informed modules and support under the BGRCN project. These extension workers will directly support delivery of project activities, including training MTTs, providing technical assistance on climate change adaptation, ongoing advice, guidance and support to CBGs throughout the project implementation period to enable the continuation of climate-informed extension services beyond closure of the Project Area. This will ensure households who were not selected by their CBGs to directly participate in formal trainings organized by the project will have access to climate-informed extension services through these extension workers.

The project expects to generate high adoption rates among CBGs. This approach will also ensure that the provincial governments are well equipped to continue providing climate-informed extension services in the Project Area and beyond, which is critical for scaling up and mainstreaming climate change adaptation so important to the broader process of sector transformation that this project aims to set in motion.

Activity 3.2.2 – Enhance and deliver quality extension services on climate-resilient SNRM to households: To enable continuous delivery of climate-informed extension services and planning, and to monitor implementation progress, it is essential for the government to provide a user-friendly platform that can support change-informed extension and advisory services beyond project closure; the government requires a user-friendly platform that can support change-informed extension and advisory services. Given the evolving climate change impacts and the state of natural resources within the Project Area, Project staff will therefore require additional service provision and design capacity. To this end, the Project will establish a database of the existing provincial service providers (including schools, NGOs, etc.) as well as provide a centralized and easily-accessible repository for project-generated information, knowledge, guidelines and materials that can be used by government extension workers to support climate-informed SNRM delivery which the project will actively consult during the delivery of this Activity (and others). In addition, the CKC will be used as an online platform to disseminate climate change-related information and resources.

The service providers will also establish regional CKC hubs in the MoTE offices in provinces 1, 2, and 3, specifically to provide technical support for extension workers on climate-informed SNRM delivery. The delivery of climate-informed extension services, but will also enable government and other stakeholders to continuously access information and resources from the CKC platform to support their own planning and support programmes/services accordingly.

Once the CKC is established; project staff will organize one national training and three provincial trainings to build the capacity of provincial-level extension workers on using the CKC online platform and regional hubs. The three provincial trainings will focus in particular on building the capacity of MTTs and each of the provincial CKC-hubs. As part of this training, guidelines will be developed to guide provincial staff on CKC knowledge generation, gathering and management, as well as the development of Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs), which will be agreed with MoTE to ensure the provincial CKC hubs are well operated and maintained in the future. The provincial CKC-hubs will also provide technical support for extension workers and guidelines and SOPs in PYT to build their capacity to continue operating the CKC, and ensure it fulfils its role as a repository to support climate-informed extension services and planning into the future.

### C4. Background Information on Project / Programme Sponsor(Executing Entity)

**64. The Ministry of Forests and Environment (MoFE)** will be an Executing Entity for the BRCR project (legally one of two “co-EEIs” for this project along with the FAO TA Team). MoFE is responsible for the conservation, development, use, and sustainable management of forests and other natural resources in Nepal. It has a long history of experience in REDD+ in Nepal, and hosts the REDD+ Implementation Centre (RIC). In addition, MoFE has experience and expertise in implementing forest-based projects such as community forestry, afforestation/reforestation, conservation of SNRM and ecosystem restoration in the Churia region. MoFE is thus well placed to play a central role in the delivery of the project. The MoFE will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including the implementation of the USD 24 million World Bank Forest Investment Programme (FIP) in Nepal. In so doing it will also be implementing the USD 10 million Global Environment Facility (GEF) Small Grants Programme (SGP) in Nepal. MoFE has considerable experience managing projects of this nature and scale, including the implementation of the USD 24 million World Bank Forest Investment Programme (FIP) in Nepal. In so doing it will also be implementing the USD 10 million Global Environment Facility (GEF) Small Grants Programme (SGP) in Nepal.

The MoFE has a strong track record in forest-based projects, including the implementation of the USD 24 million World Bank Forest Investment Programme (FIP) in Nepal. ### Multi-Stakeholder Coordination Units

#### Provincial Coordination Units (PCUs)
Each PCU will establish in each of the three provinces, and will be chaired by the Secretary of the relevant provincial MoFTE. Each PCU will include representative members from relevant provincial ministries, departments, CSOs including women's organizations, indigenous people's federations and representatives from local communities. The PCU will serve as a multi-sectoral and multi-stakeholder committee to develop proposals for provincial-level projects and activities that are aligned with the provincial development plan and the GCF Strategic Plan. The PCU will convene on a quarterly basis to review project progress against provincial targets, ensure coordination of project activities within those provinces, and provide feedback from provincial stakeholders to the PMU. The PCUs are key parts of the project's exit strategy.

During the second half of the project period, the PCUs will transition their roles to provincial-level institutions and will work with provincial stakeholders towards handing over project technical and operational support structures to the PCUs.

### Other key actors and entities involved in the project

- The Ministry of Finance (MoF) is the National Designated Authority (NDA) for the GCF in Nepal. - As part of the PSC, MoF will also contribute to developing project management capacity as the NDA. The Ministry as long-standing experience managing project finance from international development partners.
- The Ministry of Agriculture, Land Management and Cooperatives (MoALaC) will play an important role supporting and implementing the Project. This includes providing technical support for the establishment of cooperatives and agricultural extension, which will have an important role in planning processes for disaster risk reduction and adaptation of climate-resilient land use practices in the agriculture sector.
- MoALaC will also provide support to the project underComponent1.

- The Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation (MEWRI) will support procurement related to sustainable water resources management and irrigation infrastructure development as well as capacity building for the Project.
- The Ministry of Federal Affairs and General Administration (MoFaGaA) will contribute to improving coordination between national ministries and local and provincial governments, especially ensuring alignment with the BCRRN and provincial disaster risk reduction plans.
- The Ministry of Home Affairs (MoHaA) will support interventions related to disaster risk reduction planning under Component 1. MoHaA will also provide security for project activities in Province 2.

- Provincial Ministries of Industry, Tourism, Forests and Environment (MoITFe) in Provinces 1, 2 and 3 act as heads of the PIMAs and chairs of the PCUs (as described above), and will be the recipients of institutional capacity building interventions under Component 1. They will also provide technical support for project implementation and oversee impact and output.
- MoITFe will also use a portion of its co-financing to enlist the services of MoITFe extension workers to support project activities in Provinces 1, 2 and 3.

- including developing their competence in planning and implementation of climate-resilient NRM and DRR. Local governments will also support PIMAs and PCUs with technical and financial resources to implement the project, as well as provide feedback on plans and budgets.

CBOs are the key actors at the local level of the region and CBOs and their members are the main beneficiaries of the project.

- CSOs will work as actors that strengthen and empower communities and CBOs on livelihood diversification, providing extension services to local communities, supporting institutions strengthening and acting as key actors in support knowledge dissemination. Examples of CSOs include being INEFIN, forest user groups, indigenous peoples organizations (e.g., HEMAVIRAT, NIFW) and organizations representing ethnic minorities and marginalized groups (e.g., DANAR, FEEDO, etc.). Other CSOs may also be engaged as service providers through Letters of Agreement (LoA), as appropriate.

### Private Sector Actors

- Private sector actors will aim to project aims to further strengthen cooperation with the private sector in the promotion of sustainable forest management and DRR in the Churia Region.

### Research and Academic Entities

- To be engaged in various project activities as appropriate, through LoAs, to help generate and disseminate relevant knowledge and research outcomes on climate change, climate-resilient land use and DRR in the Churia Region.

### D.1.Value Added for GCF Involvement

#### 94. GCF support will be instrumental in enhancing the climate resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable communities in Nepal by supporting integrated climate-resilient (C-RE) approaches. Nepal’s NDC discloses the country's strategy and commitments to climate change, yet the need for additional technical and financial support is evident.

#### 95. Nepal's adaptation needs cannot be addressed through government expenditure alone. The county is still recovering from a devastating earthquake in 2015 that led to substantial loss of life and damage to productive assets. Additionally, the country has been hit by frequent floods and landslides, which have limited its ability to address interventions to address climate change in the Churia region.** The country's economy is also still recovering from a five-year civil conflict ending in 2015. Nepal’s NDC identifies climate resilience as one of its development priorities in the medium term.

#### 96. Nepal's social and economic development challenges further exacerbate the vulnerability of its population. The majority of the population (purchase power parity) is below $2,500), and 23% of the country’s lives below the poverty line**. Over 70% of its population lives in rural areas and approximately 46% are unemployed. Nepal's economy is heavily dependent on agriculture, which is highly vulnerable to climate change, including the Churia. Vulnerability situation is further compounded by the dependence of rural communities on a highland-based water supply system.

#### 97. GCF financial support would allow Nepal to invest in climate action at an opportune time and avoid predicted higher costs associated with future investments. Given its unique geographical location and reliance on natural resources, the proposed project will support local people and governments to adopt climate-resilient land use practices, strengthening their resilience to climate change through improved adaptive capacities.

#### 98. GCF support will also be instrumental in promoting integrated natural resource management as the central paradigm for promoting climate resilience in broader national, provincial and local policymaking and planning processes. Given Nepal’s political context, the project will add further momentum towards a more transformative community-based natural resource management modes across the Churia. The project will add further momentum to the country's efforts to mainstream climate change adaptation into its development plans. Given Nepal’s political transition, the project further represents an opportunity moment to establish capacities on comprehensive land use planning (CLUP) in the Churia region, as well as mainstreamed climate change mitigation and adaptation into provincial and local strategies and plans.

#### 99. The GCF support is also noteworthy in terms of the catalytic impact it will have in bringing together cross-sectoral stakeholders to address climate change at a landscape level. Nepal’s population is over 32 million people (of which 51% of inhabitants are women; 31% indigenous peoples and 13% Dalits). Daily livelihoods depend on agriculture, livestock production, forestry, and fishing. The proposed project will provide a model for integrated, cross-sectoral climate change adaptation for adoption in the other regions as well improving the resilience of communities to climate change impacts through improved adaptive capacities at the downstream communities, ensuring that a holistic management approach is promoted that takes into consideration the needs of all stakeholders.

#### 100. BRCRN project activities will crowd-in public financing and mobilize community-level investments for climate-resilient land use. Government co-financing demonstrates the government’s commitment to invest in climate resilience. The proposed project will also provide a model for integrated, cross-sectoral climate change activities will encourage CBOs and farmers to invest in improved practices. Public-private CSO platform will support opportunities for private sector investment in climate-resilient land use. The project will also promote better coordination with the public sector, CSOs and CBOs. It is highly complementary to the Forest Investment Program (FIP) of the World Bank and other ongoing initiatives.

#### 101. The BRCRN project will have a trans-boundary impact, providing positive environmental, social and economic benefits to downstream communities, including in Northern India where many of the Churia’s rivers flow. Particularly in the northern plains, where water from the Churia hills floods and swells in river flows can be controlled by SNRM—ultimately reducing the impact of floods.

### 0.2 Exit Strategy

The proposed project has been developed through ongoing consultations with government representatives from local and provincial governments, ensuring alignment over the project and demonstrating a close working relationship with government policies and plans, and that project activities support medium- and long-term planning to ensure the project's sustainability and continued progress towards mainstreaming climate-resilience in Nepal.

By aligning the project’s objectives with those of the Government of Nepal (GoN) and the 20-year Churia Master Plan, the chances of its continuity after GCF investment are very high. Improving the institutional capacity of provincial governments to mainstream climate change considerations into local land use planning and management is a key objective of the project. The provincial local communities are explicit goals of Nepal’s political leadership. As such, the Government of Nepal is a strong proponent of this project as it aligns with its national priorities for improving livelihoods in rural areas while mainstreaming climate change considerations and support for climate-resilient SNRM into the implementation of the Churia Master Plan. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals by transforming natural resource management in the Churia region beyond project closure. The successful implementation of this project will require a significant amount of additional resources, including additional external funds needed to bridge the financial gap towards the full implementation of the Chura Master Plan.

The long-term sustainability of project interventions is further enhanced by the project’s focus on individual and institutional capacity building with key stakeholders at all levels. Measures focused on institutional strengthening include training provincial government officials in climate change mainstreaming, increasing awareness and influence of such organizations over land use planning and management. It represents an opportunity time to establish a strong foundation for long-term sustainability through improved governance structures that reduce risks, facilitate the use of climate information in planning and decision making, and mainstream climate-resilience into local land use plans. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals by transforming natural resource management in the Churia region beyond project closure.

The project will further enhance the sense of ownership of provincial and local governments during the project life-cycle as well as the ongoing implementation of such activities after project closure.

### Economic Viability

Economic viability is a key criterion for viable, cost-effective and climate-resilient measures that provide tangible benefits. It is particularly important that the project cover such up-front costs associated with the adoption of new technologies and practices to ensure that farmers are not exposed to undue risk or average to “adopt” project promoted practices during the project implementation period. As shown in Table 1, the cost of adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices and improved irrigation and engineering structures are financially attractive from a land user perspective. The recurrent costs associated with continued adoption of such technologies and practices will be covered by increased farm income for participating households through increased cash-flow due to the project interventions. The demonstration plantation and protection of trees, as well as the establishment of community nurseries, will also ensure that farmers can recover the investment costs. FAO and MoEF expect that additional CBAs and member households interested in adopting such practices beyond the scope of the project could cover such costs through a combination of own resources and loans from local banks. The project’s institutional strengthening activities, including the establishment of training institutions or training, and improved knowledge management will also support sustained adoption of climate-resilient technologies.

### Water and Soil Conservation Investments

The water and soil conservation infrastructure investments will be operated and maintained by CBAs when they are built. These investments will be constructed using locally available materials, and thus operations and maintenance (OAM) costs are expected to be modest. The project’s institutional strengthening activities, including the establishment of training institutions or training, and improved knowledge management will also support sustained adoption of climate-resilient technologies.

The O&M costs for CBAs remain sufficiently modest that they can cover such costs beyond project closure, particularly given the anticipated increases in revenue among GCF members due to project-supported practices, as well as the anticipated reductions in climate change-related losses and damages due to the below-

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 61 OF 117

Engineering structures, as well as other resilience-building practices, measures and structures promoted through the MRCC initiative. Selected measures are often combined with revenue-generating activities to contribute to the financial sustainability of such investments (e.g., river bank stabilization with grasses and bamboo/tree planting for erosion control).

Investments on public land will be maintained by the government institutions responsible for the infrastructure. Investments such as farmer field schools and nurseries will be operated under the responsibility of the CBOs after the project closure.

Improvements in extension services (e.g., through strengthening extension modules/ materials, training trainers and improving access to extension and demonstration activities) will improve access to knowledge on climate change adaptation and resilience-building practices. This will also increase the demand for such services and information in the broader project area. The capacity of local extension workers will be strengthened so that they can continue providing similar services beyond project closure. By building the capacity of local extension workers, it is expected that the demand for such services will be met by the CBOs and other service providers at a lower cost rate among CBOs. This will be further added by ensuring the alignment of FFS with CEFRS, ensuring that FFS are strategically aligned to the needs of the communities and that they provide information on climate-resilient extension services based on priority interventions and local conditions.

In addition, the Government of Nepal is providing considerable co-financing to improve the service system through the development of a knowledge management system (KMS) for the identification of this pressing need and common issues it – will be encouraged and actively supported to mobilize the funds needed to continue this enhanced function beyond project closure. The KMS will also provide a platform for sharing information on FFS year 3 to the end of the project period. Knowledge management systems (KMS), institutionalization of trainings, development of extension modules, and other activities will be carried out in close collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives. The capacity building of government staff will facilitate Provincial and Central Government to mobilize the funds needed and continue investments in climate change adaptation and resilience-building practices beyond project closure. Staff will also be equipped to continue providing climate-informed extension services in the Project Area after project closure – thereby ensuring that the lessons learned from this project are as important to the ongoing process of sector transformation that this project aims to set in motion.

Ultimately, investments in FFS will address the main issues faced by the current system (highlighted in the FS in Field) and will contribute significantly towards higher productivity, and increased income. The provision of trainings (Local Resource Persons, promoted good climate-resilient land use practices), and providing targeted extension services to farmers will help them improve their skills and knowledge on climate change adaptation measures. Farmers participating in FFS will continuously improve and consolidate their knowledge and skills through the provision of training and extension services provided by the CBOs. The results from this project are economically sustainable. Participation in FFS is voluntary and will employ locally available resources materials, and technologies to ensure that the costs incurred for such activities are kept at a minimum level. The practices promoted improve farmers’ income through increased productivity, cost savings, or other means. FFS also promotes the adoption of climate-resilient land use practices and sustainable agricultural technologies/practices through FFS, including among non-participant who observe the benefits of such practices.

### E.1. Impact Potential

#### Contribution of the BCRIN programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas

**E.1.1. Mitigation / adaptation impact potential**

The project has been designed to generate positive adaptation and mitigation impacts, in line with Nepal's National Climate Policy and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) criteria.

#### Mitigation:

- **Mitigation: The BCRIN project will significantly improve the resilience of ecosystems and communities in the Churia region. Through support for the adoption of climate-resilient land use practices at the local level, the project will contribute to reducing GHG emissions from the project area (the 26 targeted river systems) by making them more resilient to short- and medium-term climatic variability. This will also reduce vulnerability to climate change-related risks on forest land, which will also indirectly reduce vulnerability to climate change-related risks for countless other livelihoods in the region. The project will support the adoption of climate-resilient land use planning and management practices into key plans at the sub-national level: strengthening the capacity of government and local communities to provide ecosystem services that are more responsive to climate change risks and appropriate response measures. In so doing, the BCRIN project will contribute to reducing GHG emissions from forest land and enhancing carbon sequestration by people.

- **Mitigation: The BCRIN project will have an impact on GHG emissions from the OFAC sector in Nepal. Importantly, the project is expected to significantly reduce deforestation and forest degradation and enhance forest regeneration through the adoption of climate-resilient land use practices at the local level. These practices include sustainable management of degraded forests, wetlands and grassland ecosystems; support tree planting activities for reforestation and afforestation; and improved agricultural and land management practices that (inter alia) enhance carbon sequestration in soils. Adopting an 80 percent adoption rate, the project will therefore deliver annual average emissions reductions of 574,180CO2eq/yr over a period of 30 years.

#### The projects’ components and Activities are complementary and collectively will achieve impacts on the ground beyond project lifetime:

- In the first two years of the project, activities within Component 2 will focus on strengthening the capacity of government and local communities to provide ecosystem services that are more responsive to climate change risks and appropriate response measures. These include: (i) supporting the development of a national forest policy; (ii) developing a national forest action plan for the country’s 26 targeted river systems and mainstreaming climate-resilient land use planning at the provincial, local (e.g., rural municipality), and community levels; and (iii) strengthening the capacity of government and local communities to provide ecosystem services that are more responsive to climate change risks. The Component will be based top-down integrated spatial planning with participatory land use planning for climate-resilience at the local level, culminating in the development of CEMPs in each of the 26 targeted river systems. These plans will identify and prioritize key forest conservation areas (FCAs) within the project’s relevant CBXNs within vulnerable river systems, thereby ensuring full community buy-in for intentions to be delivered under Component 1.

- The delivery of support under Component 1 generally involves a combination of: (i) training for member households from each CBXN on climate-resilient land use practices; and (ii) providing the necessary inputs, tools, and equipment to households to adopt the specific project-promoted practices (note: the specific inputs, tools and equipment vary by practice). The training will be provided in a participatory manner with the support of local communities throughout the project implementation period to support beneficiaries to sustain the project-promoted practices. As a result of these training sessions, TAFs are expected to have a relatively high adoption rate under Component 1 activities. Households and CBXNs are generally expected to be able to cover the costs when adopting and applying project-promoted practices, and where costs are involved (e.g., basic equipment and tools for farm households), the GCF grant funds will be used to provide these inputs, tools and equipment; otherwise, the cost will cover these costs. As a result of this, the project allocates approximately 55% of GCF grant finance to cover the costs of inputs (e.g., seedlings), materials (e.g. for community structures), tools and equipment that would otherwise serve as barriers to adoption. In the case of community structures (e.g. check dams), plantation establishment and forest restoration

### Methodology for Calculating the Mitigation Impact

1. The methodology for calculating the mitigation impact indices follows IPCC/CGP (2008) principles for land-based accounting.

### Methodology for Calculating Beneficiaries

The number of direct beneficiaries were calculated as follows:

- Within the project districts, the average household contains 4.8 people (slightly higher than the national average of 4.5). The project aims to reach 750 CBOs. On this average will imply ~29–20 user groups per river system, which is within the range of what has been observed in other projects and best practice.

The following assumptions were made in terms of the composition of groups and number of beneficiaries:

| Type of Conservation Body (CBO) | Average membership of CBOs per CHG | Number of CBOs to Target | No. of Households engaged in BCRN project |
|---|---|---|---|
| CFGRM | 105 | 750 | 26,834 |
| CoRM | 32,227 | 90 | 2,886.1 |
| Farmer and Soil Conservation User Groups | 40 | 90 | 3,666.1 |
| Public Land Management User Groups | 50 | 90 | 4,500 |
| Pro-Poor Watershed Forest Groups | 70 | 90 | 6,300 |
| Total | * | * | **29,881** |

In total, the 750 CBOs are expected to include at least 20,661 households within the project. Based average household size of 4.8 people in the project area, the total number of beneficiaries would be around 98,328 people.

At least 1 Indigenous Peoples Organization (IPO) in each province will also be among the beneficiary CBOs. It is expected that this will not add to the total number of beneficiary households; the members of these IPOs will also be included within the target population as beneficiaries CBOs for particular project activities, in consultation with IP representative bodies e.g. NEFIN.

### E.2 Paradigm Shift Potential

#### Degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project/programme investment

**E.2.1 Project Overview**

The project will be the first ecosystem-wide initiative in the Chiru region to move beyond pilot and institutionalize climate change mitigation and adaptation options into multi-sectoral local development planning processes. It will encompass 3,054 km² of land area across 6 districts, with a population of approximately 128,000 people.

**E.2.2 Project Design**

The project will support the implementation of climate-resilient SNRM at scale to address critical climate change challenges and demonstrate (for communities, governments and other stakeholders) the substantial benefits to be accrued from adopting such practices. The project will also provide a platform for scaling up the lessons learned in this pilot project case and drive a transformation process.

**E.2.3 Project Implementation**

At the policy and institutional level, the project will ensure that climate-resilient SNRM becomes a central feature of all land use planning processes, including management and rural development. This will benefit from a strong political commitment and the opportunity to shape the development trajectory of the new decentralized institutions in the Chiru region. The project will also provide a platform for scaling up lessons learned within high rural poverty and relatively low potential to attract private investment for climate resilience.

The proposed project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach, with the SNRM model being adapted/adopted by local communities largely through existing resources, sustained by robust institutions and support from the government. The project will also provide a platform for scaling up lessons learned within high rural poverty and relatively low potential to attract private investment for climate resilience.

At field level, the project will work directly with 750 CBOS to support their member households to adopt and invest in climate-resilient SNRM practices on their land. The project will do so by strengthening CBO management plans, training for CBOs on climate-resilient SNRM and strengthening forest management planning processes (e.g., on climate-resilient farming practices, forest management and other approaches) for nominated CBO members in the project area.

The project will also provide a platform for scaling up lessons learned within high rural poverty and relatively low potential to attract private investment for climate resilience. **129** The project will also organize awareness raising campaigns to demystify broader impacts in climate-resilient SNRM practices, linked with the demonstrable benefits accrued by communities and households who adopt such practices during the project (as outlined above), this is expected to reinforce demand for climate-informed extension services beyond the project period. **130** Creating an enabling environment, collaboration with national and sub-national (e.g., local and provincial) entities further ensures local ownership and sustainability, and thus the potential for scaling up and replication after project closure. For example, the project has significant elements that respond to the latest development in climate change policy, national policy, national REDD+ draft policy and recent amendments to the Forest Act of 1993.

**131** The planning approaches mainstreamed in the Climate (e.g., provincial and CBD planning) can be refined through a series of workshops and training sessions on a cost-effective and demand-driven, and evidenced-based net-in-savings through targeted, efficient, results-oriented investment of national resources. This will be aided by the establishment of a knowledge management system that will facilitate the sharing of lessons learned from the development and implementation of measures for increasing climate resilience. Scaling-up efforts will be facilitated by the establishment of a network of provincial and local government officials, as well as other key stakeholders at sub-national institutions on best practices for climate-resilient SNRM. The project will thus promote the active exchange of knowledge and lessons learned and best practices to guide implementation of climate-resilient SNRM and DRR measures across Nepal.

**132** As the Churia region extends into India to Pakistan, the experiences, knowledge generated and lessons learnt from this development will be shared with other countries in South Asia. This will also facilitate the replication of similar measures within these countries. The project will have substantial impacts downstream, not only in the downstream river basins but also in the communities that experience high exposure to flooding and that is greatly impacted by upstream land use in the Churia hills of Nepal.

#### 2.2.2 Portal for knowledge and learning

**133** The project will encourage innovative knowledge management to improve awareness, build capacities, and support decision-making on water and land use information into decision making processes at the national, provincial and local level. Within Component2 and 3, 6 activities focus on institutional capacity building and learning. Provincial MoTFTEs will be supported in developing a provincial-level knowledge portal for climate change including the job support to identify critical hotspots and conduct a mapping activity within each river system. Such knowledge portals will also provide information on climate change vulnerability assessments, and how local governments will have with regards to natural resource management. The development of CERPs will contribute to this by providing an opportunity for communities to share their experiences in disaster risk reduction and disaster risk integration into planning and decision making processes. This will ultimately guide the adoption of appropriate adaptation measures.

**134** At least 750 local CBAs will participate in workshops to raise awareness of climate change risks and climate-resilient land use practices that are adapted and appropriate to their local contexts and priorities. A participatory approach will be used for developing CERPs, which will include the development of a provincial-level knowledge portal using and allowing communities to develop ownership and apply their knowledge based on their own experiences in disaster risk reduction and adaptation measures. This will also provide an opportunity for climate change risks in their localities’ region, risk-reduction measures, as well as all how where they can access relevant information and services.

**135** Activity 3.1.1 will support the consolidation of local and indigenous knowledge on adaptation related to water, agriculture and forests; where innovative and locally adapted approaches will be identified, disseminated and integrated into CERPs. In addition, communities in the Churia hills have expressed that they are concerned about communities, have noted the importance of existing traditional and indigenous knowledge for climate change adaptation measures. This will also provide an opportunity to share their experiences with other stakeholders.

and farmer field schools. This represents a major innovation for supporting the mainstreaming of this knowledge into extension modules, combined with other good practices that build on key lessons and experiences.

### Activity3.2.2 focuses specifically on strengthening extension services in the project area, with a focus on knowledge and information. The project will finance the elaboration of 10 extension modules for climate-resilient land use planning, based on the results from the baseline study (Activity 3.1) as well as the findings from the feasibility work done for this project, as well as the insights gained from the CERP process (under Activity 2.1-2). and the lessons learned from other projects in the region. The modules will be designed to address the specificities of the project area and the different bio-geophysical zones and aim to support the transfer of knowledge on climate change adaptation, including the use of participatory approaches that are sensitive to gender, age, ethnicity, socio-cultural backgrounds, poor households, women, and people who are illiterate, among others. Diverse communities will be involved in the development of these modules (including indigenous peoples, pastoralists, farmers, women, youth, children, older persons, etc.), as well as in their testing and validation (including women, indigenous peoples, Dalits, and Mathwah people, among others) will be trained on the modules and equipped with the necessary tools to support extension services. The modules will also include information (through official translators or through local resource persons) to support extension services and knowledge dissemination at the community level. These modules will be used in training of trainers workshops, individual learning, improved knowledge management (through the CCK), and will support the replication of training and adoption of climate-resilient land use in other parts of the country.

### Activity3.2.1 focuses on strengthening extension services for the entire Churia region, as well as the Churia landscape that extends from India to Bangladesh and Bhutan, passing through Nepal. Thus, the discussion will be at a regional level rather than just focusing on the project area. The CCK and CIC knowledge hub, would support learning processes and the potential scaling up and replication of project activities.

#### E2.3.2 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

138. The BRCRN project contributes to the creation of an enabling environment through a step-wise approach. Through Continuous Risk Reduction Network (CRRN) workshops, stakeholders will be brought together to implement the project activities. Ultimately, the capacity of government stakeholders and local CBIs will be built by the project to understand and assess climate risks in their respective areas, including the identification of vulnerable communities, degraded land use, and further monitor local risks and resources in the future. This will equip them to continue investing in climate-resilient land use planning at both upstream and downstream interventions to maximize adaptation and mitigation benefits—beyond project closure.

139. Given the government’s ongoing political transition and the associated devolution of power to the local and provincial levels, it is important that the CRRN workshops are held early on. This will ensure that DRCs and SNMIF at the local and provincial level. Building institutional capacities at an early phase will facilitate institution building in the future. Thus, climate-resilient land use planning becomes a cross-cutting consideration for government institutions after project closure.

140. Provinces and CERPs will further create an enabling environment for climate-resilient land use, ensuring a wide range of stakeholders are involved in the process. This will include local CBIs, provincial governments, climate-resilient land use planning, ensuring an integrated approach considering upstream and downstream management. Such an approach will also ensure that the project’s activities are sustainable over time, given the longevity and scaling-up of such practices. Specifically, they will enable provincial governments to understand climate risks in their respective areas, including the identification of vulnerable communities, degraded land use. Such an approach will demonstrate a new framework for low GHG and climate-resilient planning in Nepal. This will also ensure that the project’s activities are sustainable over time given the region’s needs, given the high vulnerability of the region and the political context of the country, where there is a need to quickly adapt in the opinions of provincial and local policy makers to enable the adoption of necessary adaptation measures.

141. Capacities of CSOs, CICs and other stakeholders will be strengthened as well, ensuring that there is a clear feedback loop between the project and scaling up activities. The continuous risk reduction network planning approaches through workshops will help communities become aware of climate risks and improved practices in land use management. This will also ensure that the project’s activities are sustainable over time to implement based on their local context and priorities. Such an approach will increase awareness of climate

### Social Co-benefits

The project will deliver numerous health-related co-benefits. For example, appropriately managed water conservation systems can reduce waterborne diseases in communities; in addition, pilot-implementation of improved resource quality and production can reduce collection distances for fuelwood, water and fodder (where women previously had to walk several hours carrying heavy loads – activities that were increasing due to climate change and urbanization). The project will also support the development of a gender-sensitive approach to land use planning (which plays an important role in collecting water) to allocate more time to their education.

The project will contribute to the achievement of 100% of direct beneficiaries being indigenous peoples. An indigenous peoples planning framework has been developed, based on diverse consultations with indigenous communities and other stakeholders. The project will also support the development of targeted measures to empower indigenous peoples and ensure that a responsive M&E framework can be applied to the project. This will contribute to the project’s impact in strengthening social inclusion.

The project will support SDG 10 in reducing poverty, and among countries, as socio-inclusion is mainstreamed as a cross-cutting consideration within the project with the aim to enhance social inclusion and empower indigenous peoples. The project will also support the development of targeted measures to protect river banks or areas that are susceptible to landslides; and the project will further help reduce their vulnerability to climate change and strengthen their adaptive capacities by targeting the most vulnerable communities.

### Economic Co-benefits

The BCRON project will deliver crucial economic co-benefits through its support for agricultural development. Investment in sustainable agriculture will improve soil quality, water availability and reduce land degradation, which can help to strengthen agricultural production and productivity that can allow households to generate more

### E.4. Vulnerability of country and beneficiary groups (Adaptation only)

As described in the previous sections, Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change. The vulnerability of already fragile ecosystems is further exacerbated by human activities such as deforestation, overgrazing interventions such as settlement through the clearing of forests for cultivation, over exploitation of timber and other forest products, and conversion of wetlands into agricultural land. These activities have led to a decline in the number of livestock - thereby altering ecosystem function and the overall resilience of the region. Local people’s resilience is negatively impacted through the loss of key ecosystem services and resilience that increase their exposure to climate change.

Based on historical trends and future climate change predictions, Nepal will likely become increasingly exposed to climatic extremes such as droughts, floods, and landslides. The economic costs associated with the impacts of climate variability and extreme events are estimated at US$ 270-360 million/year, representing 1.5 to

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### E4.2 Financial, economic and social institutional needs

**Financial and Economic Needs**

161. Nepal's daunting social, environmental and economic development challenges exacerbate the vulnerability risks of its population as described in Section D.1 above. Given these challenges, Nepal requires consistent grant financing to fund development priorities, including those described in its NDC, in the medium term. If such support does not materialize, the costs for adaptation will increase, as will the costs for local households and communities as climate change impacts increase in intensity and frequency.

**Social and Institutional Needs**

162. The government is undergoing a transition toward a federal state. Nepal's political transition is a landmark event accompanied by the devolution of power over forest and agricultural resources to the provincial and local level. Capacities on climate change and climate-resilient development remain limited at the provincial and local levels, and are further hampered by weak institutional capacities in the areas of governance, planning, and financial and institutional capacities. At the same time, this transition represents an opportunity moment to strengthen capacities and improve coordination among different stakeholders for effective implementation of climate change adaptation initiatives. Trainings are needed on various topics, especially climate change risks and climate-resilient land use practices, as well as on how to integrate these into existing development plans and programs. Similarly, tools and approaches for disaster risk reduction planning.

163. Many CSOs have been active in the field of climate change, but changing political structure. Before the transition, CSOs often had national representation with district-level chapters. Many CSOs will now have to adapt to provincial level chapters, as well as increasing local level representation to match the new roles that local and provincial level governments are taking on. This will require increased coordination between CSOs at different levels of government in cooperation with central and provincial government authorities and other stakeholders.

164. While many communities are aware of the availability of forest and agricultural resources, many are not aware of climate change threats and locally-vascular vulnerabilities (critical ecosystems, differentiated vulnerabilities, risks). Capacity building is needed to increase awareness on these issues at the local level, as well as low carbon and climate-resilient land use management practices and DRR measures.

165. As mentioned in Section C.2, a range of barriers have limited the effectiveness of extension services in Nepal, and many communities are not aware of their existence. To overcome such barriers, and support enhanced capacity building to strengthen adaptive capacities and overall resilience

#### E.2. Country Ownership
**E.2.1. Country Ownership of, and capacity to implement, a funded project or programme**

**E.5.1. Existence of a national climate strategy and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAMAs, NAPASs and NAPs**

The project is closely aligned with Nepal's NDC, and is congruent with existing laws and policies.

- **a. The Fourteenth Development Plan (2017) of Nepal** recognises the role of forests in contributing to both climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts, and calls for the conservation, sustainable management and use of forest resources.
- **b. President Chure-Tantri Madhesis Conservation and Management Master Plan (2017)** provides strategic direction on how to address the challenges posed by climate change through integrated land-use planning considering the interconnectedness of upstream and downstream natural resource management.
- **c. The Climate Change Policy (2011)** aims to improve livelihoods by mitigating climate change impacts, adopting a low-carbon development path and endeavours to reduce greenhouse gas emissions through clean energy and energy efficiency.
- **d. Nepal's NDC(2016)** describes the impacts of climate change on a number of sectors to which the proposed GCF project is relevant. These include: increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; water availability as well as increased frequency of droughts and floods; agriculture, both crops and livestock; food security; and (re) renewable energy. The NDC also describes adaptation priorities with which the proposed project aligns including: climate-resilient agricultural practices; improved access to clean drinking water; rehabilitation and restoration; strengthened community-based natural resources management; and iv) improved disaster risk reduction.
- **e. The National Adaptation Programme of Action and draft National Adaptation Plan (2015)** identifies a number of adaptation priorities, including: climate-resilient agricultural practices; improved access to clean drinking water; rehabilitation and restoration; strengthened community-based natural resources management; and iv) improved disaster risk reduction.
- **f. Forest Sector policy (2015)** replaces the former forest policy (2003) and envisions contributing to local and national development through sustainable management of forests, biodiversity and watersheds. It aims to improve livelihoods by providing a range of ecosystem services and income opportunities through conservation, management and utilization of forest resources.
- **g. Nepal's Forestry Sector Strategy 2016-2025** aims to: support adaptive capacity of communities and forest ecosystems; increase the contribution of forests towards national development goals; and contribute to REDD+ BCRCN contributes to the Strategy’s goal of enhancing Nepal’s forest carbon stock by at least 5% compared to 2013.
- **h. Nepal's Agriculture Development Strategy (2015-2035)** sets forth priorities to move towards a self-reliant, sustainable, competitive and inclusive agriculture sector that drives economic growth and contributes to improving livelihoods, food and nutrition security.

**Note:** M&E 2016

#### E.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

169. FAO will serve as the project’s Accredited Entity. FAO is a specialized technical agency of the United Nations whose main goals are the eradication of hunger, food insecurity and malnutrition; the elimination of poverty and the reduction of human suffering; and the achievement of sustainable development and management of natural resources, including forest, land, water, air, climate and genetic resources for the benefit of present and future generations. More information on FAO’s work in Nepal can be found in Annex 2 – Feasibility Study Section 4.

170. FAO is well placed to play a central role in the BRCRN project as it has been involved with the country since inception in Nepal in 1951. FAO is in the process of negotiating the country programming framework (CPF) with the Government of Nepal, which will be signed by mid-2017. The focus areas under the CPF for the next five years are: natural resource conservation and utilization; climate change adaptation and mitigation. More information on the types of projects that require adequate, sustainable forest and watershed management, improve institutional and technical capacities for climate change adaptation in the land use sector, and strengthen climate-resilient value chains, among others.

171. MoFE is well placed to play a central role in the delivery of BRCRN. To assess the capacity of MoFE to implement the BRCRN project, FAO contracted an independent assessment of the Ministry to gauge the level and types of institutional capacity required for implementation of the project. The assessment was conducted largely on MoFE’s experience of managing and implementing the projects mentioned above. Arrangements between MoFE and FAO will be finalized in the next few months.

### E.8 Efficiency and Effectiveness

#### E.8.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

180. The total project costs are estimated at USD 47.34 million, comprised of a USD 39.3 million grant from the GCF and non-reimbursable cost-sharing contributions by Nepal amounting to USD 8.04 million.

181. Project activities are cost-effective in terms of their ability to deliver results for the intended beneficiaries. The project is targeting vulnerable groups, poor communities, and predominantly (semi)-subsistence households, and uses grant financing primarily for barrier-addressing and non-reimbursable cost-sharing contributions. The total project costs amount to USD 47.34 million, which is equivalent to USD 953,268 per person. This corresponds to a cost of about USD 49 per direct beneficiary.

182. MuFE will provide USD 0.04 million in co-financing for the implementation of the BRCRIN project, which is equivalent to about 17% of the total project costs.

#### E.8.3 Financial viability

183. Project financing will leverage additional private investments during and after the project, mainly in form of the In-kind contributions from land users to manage agricultural and forestry interventions. GCF financing will cover only the initial investment costs of transitioning to climate resilient agriculture and forestry models. The operational management and maintenance of the proposed models will be financed by the project participants.

184. After 7 years, the economic rate of return for this project is negative in absolute terms (NPV at 10% discount rate is USD -25 million). However, the internal rate of return (IRR) on discounted cash flows (DCF) is positive with an ERR of 97% (NPV at 10% discount: +18.6 million). In the long-run, incremental benefits of the project are expected to be realized in terms of a positive ERR of 114% (NPV: USD -25.3 million) after 10 years and ERR of 115% (NPV: USD 207.16 million) after 20 years.

185. In short, GCF grant financing will cover up-front costs of adopting climate-resilient models and practices promoted by the project. The project is expected to generate positive financial returns in terms of ERRs for both DCF and NPV over a longer period (after and during) the project life cycle.

186. In addition, the project will significantly increase benefits to the society at large through the adoption of these models and practices during (and after) the project, all of which will significantly increase benefits to the society at large.


---
**Page 69**
---

E.6.4. Application of best practices

188. All of the technologies and practices promoted within the framework of the BRCRN project are demonstrated approaches that have a clear component based on good practices. Complexity is largely built up of the projects’ components, such as REDD+, Action Plans, Community Forests, or local development within the framework of REDD+. It further builds off of decisions of lessons learned in supporting decentralized community-based forest management and other climate change adaptation and mitigation activities and initiatives.

189. For Component 2.1.3 various pilot programs have been implemented that have supported communities to strengthen their resilience and aid risk reduction measures. FAO has supported the Ministry of Agriculture, Land Management, Environment and Forestry (MALEF) in developing a National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for Upland Districts where climate change adaptation and risk reduction are major components in the plans.

190. The promotion of biogas (within Activity 1.1.3) builds off of years of experience of the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC), which has been working with communities to promote the use of biogas at local communities. The activity is designed based on AEPC guidelines and common practices applied.

191. The measures promoted in Component 1 have all been piloted in various regions of the country within the context of other projects, such as the National Forest Programme (NFP), which has worked with provinces, district-level governments, especially related to water management, forestry, agriculture and soil conservation. While a number of these measures are being considered for inclusion in the national post of concept for the investment and many communities have expressed interest in the proposed measures.

192. Research and development activities include the following: (a) conducting studies on women extension staff; (b) providing leadership skills and training, increasing the number of women extension staff, providing targeted trainings for women); (c) The gender assessment and GAP were presented and discussed in a workshop with implementing partners and stakeholders to identify gaps and opportunities. The results will be reflected their own lessons learned and experiences, as well as best practices based on the project’s context.

193. Thus, it is evident that project will support the application of best practices. However despite so many successful experiences, a major barrier to scaling up these practices has been the lack of finance given the country’s financial situation. The project will provide the necessary resources for scaling up and replication of good practices. The project will help to create an enabling environment to support the uptake of proven climate-resilient land use practices, and will contribute to the development of a national framework on gender equality in agriculture, including land use planning and management, while also improving extension services and training and information dissemination. Annexes provide a detailed list per activity of key best practices and experiences for each activity.

| --- | --- |

| (c) Expected lifetime emission reductions overtime | 11.48 million tCO2eq |
| (d) Estimated cost per tCO2eq (a + c) | US$ 4.10 tCO2eq |
| (e) Estimated GCF cost per tCO2eq(requested = a / b + c) | US$ 3.43 tCO2eq |

## Methodology used for calculating indicators:

The cost and emission reduction mitigation measures are supported by the project many of the activities are on-going in nature and it is challenging to break the budget down into mitigation and adaptation-exclusive activities. Thus, the total budget was used for the calculation, which will result in a conservative estimate of the cost per tCO2eq. The actual cost per tCO2eq that would be incurred may be lower as not all funds will be used for mitigation activities.

## Comparison with appropriate benchmarks:

The cost per tCO2eq for the overall project and for the GCF are very competitive. Many carbon projects/programs, such as the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility’s Carbon Fund, have set a price of US$ 5 per tCO2eq. The fact that the proposed project is able to achieve a lower cost per tCO2eq than the mitigation and adaptation activities, this represents a cost-effective approach to reducing GHG emissions.

# F.1 Economic and Financial Analysis

## Economic Analysis

### Results of economic analysis

From the perspective of the Government of Nepal (i.e., without social value of carbon), the economic analysis demonstrates that the project is profitable over its 20-year lifetime, with an NPV of USD 49.0 million and an ERR of 20%. The project is considerably more profitable from a global perspective, resulting in a positive NPV of USD 181.6 million and an ERR of 35% for the period over 20 years (ERR: 15%). This clearly justifies the GCF investment into the project from an economic point of view.

It suggests to use price ranges between USD 40 – 80 RCOI for the year 2020. To be conservative, the lower carbon price was used in the economic analysis for the BROMA project.

## F.2. Technical Evaluation

The project has been designed building on the longstanding experience of FAO Nepal, MuFE, MoALMD, MoEWRD and MoHA, among other key actors, on topics related to climate change adaptation and mitigation. A summary of the technical evaluation considerations is included here below. Detailed technical specifications for projects supported by Component 1 are provided in Annex B – Technical Specifications (specifically document 4.2 – Appendix A), specifically:

a) Climate-resilient agriculture (nutrient management, soil and water conservation, agronomic practices, tillage and residue management, agroforestry) in Appendix A.1.

b) Ecosystem restoration (soil and water conservation bio-engineering, restoration and revitalization of degraded ecosystems, reforestation and afforestation (trees/shrubs/groundcovers)) in Appendix A.2.

c) Tree planting on private and public lands (fruit orchards/horticulture, smallholder woodlots, contour free/freestanding shelterbelts, holder banks, commercial tree plantations, protective plantations, nurseries and seed production) in Appendix A.3.

Various proven technologies and best practices will be promoted within the context of the BRONN project. These include:

- Improved soil fertility management (e.g., composting, organic manure application)
- Soil conservation techniques (e.g., contour plowing, terracing, mulching)
- Water harvesting and storage methods
- Agroforestry systems
- Climate-resilient crop varieties

The new approach of Component 2 builds off of government experiences, especially from REDD+ ER-PD and DRAP development, in combining top-down and bottom-up planning, while also addressing existing capacity gaps. The project will support the Government to develop a National Forest Strategy and Action Plan (NFSAP) that will strengthen government capacities while supporting operational plans for project implementation, while also building on existing forest management practices and policies. The NFSAP will be developed in close collaboration with local contexts and priorities aligned with CERF. This approach will ensure that integrated planning at the river basin level is informed by national-level strategies and will build on existing institutional arrangements to strengthen capacities and support them to plan and implement climate-resilient land use tailored to their priorities and different environmental conditions.

Given the new roles of newly formed institutions, EGP and other local-level plans are important to raise awareness of climate change risks and resilient land use practices and to build capacities on such planning processes. The project will provide technical assistance in developing these plans. These plans will be developed with support from experts on topics ranging from integrated watershed management, biodiversity, agriculture, etc.

Technologies promoted under Component 1 (described in Section C.3 and E.6.4) have been selected due to their relevance and effectiveness for climate change adaptation and mitigation based on the context of the project area. Technologies will be tested at pilot sites before being scaled up. The project will also provide a strong proof-of-concept, where the project will build on experiences and lessons learned from piloted projects in Nepal as

well as international best practices. A detailed assessment of these measures is available within the feasibility study.

### Measures related to extension services have been designed in close consideration of key barriers facing extension services (e.g., youth engagement, lack of information on climate change, weak capacities, among others) further discussed below.

#### Insufficient incentives in NTCs have been identified as a barrier for monitoring the impacts of climate change, but also for disseminating knowledge and improving information materials. Within extension services, such investments will help to build capacity within NTCs, strengthen linkages between NTCs and local government officials and improve communication and coordination. To support the longevity of this measure, it will be delivered in conjunction with other measures.

#### Before the government transition, some pilot districts elaborated DRR plans at the district level. Such processes and the resulting plans, including efforts from FAO to implement planning processes in Utsaparang among others, were aimed at building capacity for disaster preparedness and risk reduction processes. Districts no longer exist under the current government structure, and thus this measure aims to build on these experiences and develop risk management plans at the Provincial level for provinces 1-3.

### F.3 Environmental, Social Assessment, including Gender Considerations

#### The FAO Project Environmental and Social Screening Checklist was prepared (ESMF - Annex 4), and the project was classified as having a B (moderate risk).

#### Environmental Impacts and Risks

While the project is expected to generally promote positive environmental impacts, there are some potential short-term negative environmental impacts that could arise from project implementation. Small-scale environmental degradation could occur due to the construction of small-scale infrastructure including check dams, stone bunds, and other structures (Annex 4). These structures may cause sedimentation in water bodies (resulting in increased erosion or runoff into local waterways). Impacts will be mitigated through the preparation of a management plan for each structure that includes measures to limit the environmental impact. The plan will be submitted to the Department of Soil Conservation. Before construction can occur, plans will need to be reviewed and approved. These plans must take into account seasonal restrictions and conditions, as well as measures to limit the environmental impact.

#### One of the project sites is located within one of the 36 Biodiversity Important Over Systems (Adhara Khola, Baraue Khola and Dura Khola River System); the Koshi Tappu Wildlife Reserve. The Wildlife Reserve was designated in 1982 to protect the habitat for a wide range of wildlife including endangered species of mammals, among other flora and fauna. While no direct project interventions are planned for the conservation area, it is possible that some indirect impacts may occur as a result of the project (e.g., soil erosion and sedimentation into rivers and bodies of water).

#### The project will also support the establishment of forest plantations and agroforestry systems on degraded lands throughout the project area. These activities are in line with national policies, including those related to relevant natural regulations, such as the country's Forest Policy (2015), as well as operational guidelines for community-based groups. Both native- and non-native species will be promoted for forest and agroforestry plantations; however no native species will only be promoted when they are locally adapted species with a long.

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 72 OF 117

### Social Impacts and Risks

As with environmental impacts, majority of the project's impacts are expected to be largely positive and enhance the resilience of communities in the project area. However, some negative social impacts may also occur. The project could lead to conflicts between community members who disagree on land use planning processes and decisions. To address this risk, the project will ensure that all stakeholders have access to information about improved livelihood practices which could discriminate against some members of society, especially poor and marginalized groups. The project will work with communities to identify and mitigate these risks. The project is voluntary, and communities' participation will be based on the principle of FPRC. The project provides a flexible approach for community engagement in land use planning processes and decision-making plans, where communities can decide, based on their specific operational protocols and decision making processes, whether or not to participate in such processes.

### Indigenous Peoples

211. Indigenous peoples and marginalized minority groups, including Dalits among others, often experience discrimination and may face additional barriers to their participation and engagement that could limit their empowerment and inclusion in the project. To address this risk, the project will engage with all relevant stakeholders, and targeted measures to strengthen social inclusion have been mainstreamed as cross-cutting elements of the project. The project will ensure that representatives from indigenous peoples and other organizations representing marginalized groups will be invited to participate in multi-stakeholder bodies within the project structure, to enhance coordination, collaboration and ongoing communication during project implementation.

221. Within the BRCRN project area, 31% of inhabitants are indigenous people. An Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) has been developed for the BRCRN project, in consultation with indigenous peoples, as well as representatives from indigenous federations and organizations in Nepal, to ensure the project causes no adverse impacts on their rights or interests. The IPPF will be used by the project to guide respect, include and promote indigenous peoples issues during project implementation, including their right to Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC). The project will also ensure that all relevant stakeholders are engaged to take into account the different contexts and vulnerabilities of indigenous peoples living in the project area.

### Gender Equality

231. As a result of project beneficiaries will be women, with proportional representation of indigenous peoples, Dalits and other marginalized groups. All members of project management structures will undergo training on GEI in the context of the BRCRN project, ensuring that all staff are aware of potential barriers, differentiated vulnerabilities and opportunities for gender equality and empowerment. The project will ensure that local communities and local resource persons supporting the project will include diverse people, including women, indigenous peoples and other marginalized groups.

234. The project will build off of best-practice approaches piloted in the Chhuka and Terai Regions to strengthen gender equity and empower women. These approaches include community-based participatory research methodologies representing indigenous women and Dalit women, among others, as well as local community members and local-level government officials. In addition, the project will develop and implement strategies for supporting gender equality and empowerment within the framework of the BRCRN project were developed and integrated into the GAP (including project-wide measures and activity-specific considerations).
