### F.4 Financial Management and Procurement

226. As the Accredited Entity for the BRCRN project, FAO will ensure that financial management and procurement of goods and services using GCF resources will adhere to international standards and good practices. This includes financial management and procurement policies and procedures in accordance with the Financial Management and Procurement assessment of MAFIE in June 2018 (which included an assessment of MAFIE’s financial management and procurement policies and practices) identified the ministry as a “low-risk” partner in this respect. This includes a towards ensuring that all procurements are conducted in compliance with the prevailing Public Procurement Law of the Government of Nepal.

To enable effective performance of financial management and procurement functions, FAO will provide (i) technical assistance to the PMU to ensure that the procurement officer and administrative officer work to in the PMU throughout the duration of the project; and (ii) provision of a dedicated FAO Technical Capacity Development Team (as co-Executing Entity) will perform a range of supervisory and support functions, including but not limited to: (i) providing technical assistance to the PMU to ensure that all procurements are conducted in compliance with the prevailing Public Procurement Law; (ii) ensuring that all procurement is done in line with agreed standards and practices; and (iii) minimizing procurement-related risks.

227. The FAO Technical Capacity Development Team (as co-Executing Entity) will perform a range of supervisory and support functions, including but not limited to: (i) providing technical assistance to the PMU to ensure that all procurements are conducted in compliance with the prevailing Public Procurement Law; (ii) ensuring that all procurement is done in line with agreed standards and practices; and (iii) minimizing procurement-related risks.

228. FAO has deployed an Oracle based Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) system entitled the Global Resources Management System (GRMS). The GRMS provides a single, integrated platform for managing financial resources, procurement and finance functionalities. Using GRMS improves the flow of financial information, supports the implementation of financial management policies and procedures, and ensures that all procurements are conducted in accordance with the prevailing Public Procurement Law.

229. FAO maintains a Chart of Accounts which is used by the whole Organization and that allows for a separation of income and expenditure by donor and project and it provides a standardized coding structure that enables data to be reported on a consistent basis across all projects.

Direct procurement by FAO is done in accordance with its Manual Section on “Procurement of Goods, Works and Services” which sets out the procedures for direct procurements. Such procurements are managed under the FAO Procurement Policy and Procedures Manual and are subject to the approval of the Director General. All procurement activities to ensure the Organization procures goods, works and services based on “Best Value for Money” principles. In accordance with its policy on indirect implementation of FAD agreements, FAO operates in accordance with its policy that governs the indirect implementation of FAD agreements and programs.

230. Financial management and procurement by MAFIE (the Executing Entity) will also be overseen and supervised by the PMU. The PMU will ensure that all procurements are conducted in compliance with the prevailing Public Procurement Law, including the Operational Modality (OPM), the GCF project supervision team will undertake regular supervisory missions; and will recall to the attention of MAFIE any non-compliance with the prevailing Public Procurement Law. The PMU will ensure that financial management and procurement by the PMU and PPMAs are being performed in line with agreed standards and practices, and that the agreement is being signed between FAO and the Government of Nepal before the project becomes operational.

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

## 231. Risk factors associated with the implementation of the BRORN project include technical, institutional and operational risks. Prominent risks that may compromise the success of the BRORN project include:

a) There is a lack of coordination among key institutions involved in the project, which makes it difficult to coordinate and collaborate, which is essential to the success of the project and its efforts to promote a sustainable land-use pattern.

b) Weak enforcement capacity along key entities, and path dependency among communities, that may make it difficult to sustainably achieve changes in land-use patterns and decision-making.

c) Institutional risks associated with the ongoing political transition in Nepal, which may complicate project implementation. The government is currently deciding to further adjust the specific distribution of mandates, roles and/or responsibilities.

## 232. These risks, as well as the project's risk mitigation strategies, are briefly presented below:

# G.2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Insufficient cooperation and collaboration among government institutions and community-based natural resource management committees. | Technical and operational | Low (<5% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- (i) sensitization and awareness raising of the importance of strong collaboration;
- (ii) alignment of institutional mandates, including the development of a common understanding on the delivery of similar projects (e.g., REDD+);
- (iii) involving key stakeholders from the beginning through diverse consultation mechanisms to gain clear roles and responsibilities for all; including representation on project management structures such as the PSC and TCU;
- (iv) ensuring that the project is designed in a manner that allows communities to understand, accept and give sense of purpose; and
- (v) maintaining effective communication channels. A results-based project framework based on shared goals and responsibilities will be developed with the aim of highlighting the importance of the project in contributing towards their own respective development agendas and providing co-benefits.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Weak enforcement capacity that may make it challenging to change the status quo of illegal or unsustainable land-use practices. | Technical and operational | Low (<5% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Incentives for local communities to engage in better resource management will act as a motivator for the adoption of sustainable land practices and improved environmental governance. Training sessions at both national, provincial and local levels will be conducted to understand threats from climate change and suitable climate-resilient land use practices to improve resilience against climate change impacts. The project will also work with communities to identify their needs and vulnerabilities of local people. Furthermore, the project is closely aligned with key policies of Nepal, and have strong synergy with existing and proposed ER-PR program that aim to reduce deforestation and unsustainable land use.

Site-specific environmental impacts due to the construction of small-scale infrastructure (retention ponds, check dams, etc.)

### Description
Conflicts within CBOS about land use decisions, including decisions that could have disproportionately impacts on women and children, especially poor households and marginalized groups

#### Risk category: Technical and operational
#### Level of impact: Low (<5% of project value)

## SELECTED RISK FACTOR 7

### Description
Local level land use planning based on participatory local-level planning processes based on consultations with members of local CBIOs/CEOs groups, promoting proportional representation women, Indigenous peoples and people from marginalized communities. The project will ensure that the process is inclusive and transparent (stable) and land use planning is integrated based on community priorities and local context. Decision-making processes will be based on a participatory approach to ensure that all stakeholders are involved in decision making. Pre-project measures have been integrated operational guidelines and laws, which will help ensure the integration and participation of women and marginalized groups.

### Mitigation Measures
Training and project management will be trained in GESI Awareness raising and capacity building on climate-resilient land use planning and gender equality. Training will also include awareness-raising about the importance of ensuring that any household person feels discriminated against or has concerns related to the project, they are able to access the project’s grievance mechanism (described greater detail within Annex 4).

## SELECTED RISK FACTOR 8

### Description
Long-term sustainability of investments in land use practices (e.g. forestry) or soil and water conservation measures may require future investments to maintain investments.

#### Risk category: Technical and operational
#### Level of impact: Low (<10% of project value)
#### Probability of risk occurring: Low

### Mitigation Measures
Investments will be made on land use practices (e.g. forestry) or soil and water conservation measures may require future investments to maintain investments. For all investments that will require ongoing management costs, such costs will be clearly communicated in the project document. The project will ensure that all CBIOs who adopt these practices, which will clearly communicate management needs and maintenance requirements, and establish a clear understanding of the responsibilities for maintenance with the respective CBIOs and the respective community-based organization. Local resource persons and local government officials will be trained on monitoring and evaluation of land use practices. Training will also include emphasis on capacity building and trainings will further build capacities for appropriate management and maintenance.

### Government transition could lead to delays or adjustments in anticipated government structures

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

The Government of Nepal is currently undergoing a transition from a constitutional monarchy to a federal democratic republic. While the Constitution and Local Government Operation Act (2017) both provide detailed information on the roles, responsibilities, and powers of local governments, these are still under development as the new structure has not yet been established.

This is seen as a low-risk since the project was closely developed with a cross-sectoral committee from various government agencies. While highly engaged in the project design phase, if any significant changes occur, there may need to be additional fine-tuning upon project approval to ensure that institutional structures proposed for the project are still appropriate during the project inception phase.

The PSC will further support close communication with government counterparts to mitigate potential delays or adjustments.

### Selected Risk Factor 8

**Description:** Large volume of procurement to be managed by MoFE could result in compliance risks associated with financial management and procurement under the proposed GCF project. MoFE policies, practices and track record on financial management and procurement were reviewed by a qualified audit and accounting firm during project design and deemed (by the firm and FAO) to be satisfactory.

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

The Ministry of Finance and Economics (MoFE) will manage a large volume of contracts and procurement under the proposed GCF project. MoFE policies, practices and track record on financial management and procurement were reviewed by a qualified audit and accounting firm during project design and deemed (by the firm and FAO) to be satisfactory.

The PSC will further mitigate the above-mentioned risks by working closely with MoFE to deliver activities under the proposed project in line with MoFE policies, practices and track record. The PSC will also ensure that all procurement actions adhere to international standards are adhered to. In addition, the FAO-GCF project supervision team will recruit a qualified independent auditor to perform regular audits and spot checks to further reinforce compliance with international standards.

# H.1 Logic Framework

## H.1.1 Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund Level

### Shift to low emission sustainable development pathways

The BRCIN project will contribute to reducing deforestation, supporting reforestation, sustainably managing forests resources, conserving and enhancing forest carbon stocks and adopting land management practices that enhance resilience of ecosystems and communities. The BRCIN project will work with CBs to adopt climate-resilient land use practices in 26 vulnerable river systems by working with CBs to scale up adoption of climate-resilient land use practices and apply such practices in their communities, while also building the capacity of these CBs and their governments to sustain and scale up investments beyond project closure.

### Increased climate resilience through sustainable development

The BRCIN project will contribute to increasing the resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems in 26 vulnerable river systems by working with CBs to scale up adoption of climate-resilient land use practices and apply such practices in their communities, while also building the capacity of these CBs and their governments to sustain and scale up investments beyond project closure.

### Component 1: Scaling up climate-resilient land and practices

#### Volume of administration
To ensure that project staff put fewer gallons.

### 3.2.4. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

#### 233. In this role as Accredited Entity, FAO (specifically the FAO-GCF project supervision team) will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between BRCGIN and FAO on 15 May 2018. The AMA sets out the terms and conditions under which the GCF will provide funding to BRCGIN for the implementation of this project.

The AMA provides that the FAO Office of Evaluation will be responsible for the independent interim and final evaluations for BRCGIN. The evaluations will use a question-driven approach, and may include desk reviews, interviews, focus group discussions, and/or field visits. The results of these evaluation will be instrumental in contributing – through operational and strategic recommendations – to improve governance and management practices at BRCGIN. The independent interim and final evaluations for this project will assess the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, as well as sustainability and scalability of results, differential impacts and lessons learned. The evaluation should also assess the extent to which

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 52 OF 117

The intervention has contributed to the Fund's higher-good goal of achieving a paradigm shift towards emissions sustainable development pathways and climate-resilient sustainable development. To measure attributes changes, the evaluation will draw on mixed-methods, using qualitative methods (e.g., participatory rural appraisal) in combination with quantitative methods (e.g., remote sensing). The baseline data will be collected from the control group data from the project's baseline and completion surveys, which will be confirmed during project inception. Baseline data will also be collected through remote sensing and other monitoring systems. The evaluations will draw on the baseline and completion surveys financed by the project, as well as monitoring reports and other data generated by projects prepared by staff, including remote sensing data on changes in forest and land use patterns. Careful attention will be paid to the disaggregation of data, results and outcomes by gender.

## Project monitoring and reporting

### 235. Project-level monitoring will be the responsibility of the PMU: The PMU will establish an effective monitoring system to track implementation and continuously assess efficacy, targeting and project assumptions and targets. The project management team (PMT) will be responsible for ensuring that lessons learned are captured in project management as needed, and to generate key lessons learned.

### 236. Within the PMU, the National Project Director (NPD) will oversee project implementation and monitoring, and ensure coordination with other relevant stakeholders and government partners and civil society. The NPD will be supported by a gender focal point and monitoring & reporting specialists, all of whom will be members of the PMU. FAO will support the NPD in the development of the project monitoring plan and provide technical assistance for project monitoring & reporting. All members of project management bodies will be trained on project monitoring & reporting protocols.

### 237. Project progress will be assessed in part against the detailed project implementation plan, gender action plan, indigenous peoples plan and social inclusion plan, while results will be measured against the targets outlined in Section 10. Monitoring of project progress will be conducted through a combination of regular meetings, quarterly reporting, and submission of the project monitoring report to the GCF as part of the Project Impact Report. Monitoring of project progress will also be supported by remote sensing data on changes in forest and land use patterns. The NPD (or other person who will in turn provide progress updates to the PSC and FAO or AEI) Project progress will be communicated to the GCF Secretariat as part of the quarterly reporting.

### 238. The basic parameters of the anticipated BCRN monitoring system are briefly summarized here, and elaborated in more detail in Annex 2 – Feasibility Study, Section 7.2: The project monitoring system will feature a combination of activities to collect data on project performance against key indicators. Data collection methods will be based on a COMBINATION of field-based surveys and user group self-monitoring and digital data collection, complemented by remote sensing (e.g., Sentinel-1) and other data sources (e.g., the Community Group Leader Census (Component 3)). In terms of monitoring project impact performance in a quantitative manner, the key approach is that identified in Section 7.2. The BCRN will be monitored using a combination of quantitative and qualitative indicators. For every 5-10 registered CBGs, one community member is selected as the Community Group Leader (CGL) to provide feedback on project performance against key indicators. Data collected through surveys (developed by individual group members) into a group record. Summarized data of each group will be collected and entered in an Excel spreadsheet. The CGLs will also be responsible for monitoring the project using the tools used to administer baseline and completion surveys in the project area, providing robust data that will contribute to providing evidence on the impact of the project. Data from the BCRN monitoring system will be integrated into systems over the course of the project implementation period. This project-generated information will also be harmonized with other relevant datasets (e.g., the Community Group Leader Census and the Community Group Leader Census (which is expected to be administered in 2021)). In addition, the project will use remote sensing to monitor some key quantitative and qualitative indicators as outlined in Section H.11 as well as transacting data from other indicators to ensure accuracy.

### Barriers Inhibiting Response to Climate Change Challenges

- **Physical and Socio-Economic Vulnerabilities**
  - Weak institutional capacity
  - Limited access to information and knowledge
  - Limited access to resources
  - Limited access to technology
  - Limited access to markets
  - Limited access to finance
  - Limited access to land tenure
  - Limited access to education

- **Climate Change Impacts**
  - Increased frequency of extreme weather events
  - Increased vulnerability and exposure to climate change impacts
  - Increased risk of natural disasters
  - Increased risk of food insecurity
  - Increased risk of water scarcity
  - Increased risk of land degradation
  - Increased risk of biodiversity loss

- **Vulnerability Factors**
  - Poverty, livelihoods, and income generation
  - Lack of access to resources
  - Lack of access to information and knowledge
  - Lack of access to technology
  - Lack of access to markets
  - Lack of access to finance
  - Lack of access to land tenure
  - Lack of access to education

# ANNEXES

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 99 OF 157

### Attachment 2 - Simplified overview of climate change impacts, baseline conditions and project promoted measures, disaggregated by biophysical zone

| CHURA HILLS |
| --- |

#### Climate change challenges
- Increasing summer and monsoon rainfall coupled with decreasing numbers of moderate rainfalls days and prolonged dry spells
- The number of hot days is expected to (i) increase in the hills, with a shift from mid-day to afternoon heatwaves and (ii) increase risks of flash floods in the hills

#### Description of baseline land use trends
- Households can fuelwood for cooking and heating. Forests also provide fodder, timber, and non-timber products.
- Majority of terrain unsuitable for agriculture due to steep slopes and poor soil quality.
- Most households have small farms within the hills. Agricultural practices are rain-fed, with yield following rainfall patterns. Soil degradation poses major barriers to productivity.
- Livestock provides livelihoods only for 6–8 months per year.

#### Land use being vulnerable to climate change
- 75% of deforestation in the Chura Hills: Deforestation increases vulnerability to erosion and landslides, reduces soil fertility, and increases exposure to floods (e.g., through reduced vegetation cover).
- Unsuitable agricultural practices on steep slopes through integrated watershed management.
- Reduced rate of erosion, sedimentation, and acidification.

#### Relevant climate-resilient land use practices to be promoted
- Climate-resilient agricultural practices that stabilize soil through terrace, plough strips, alley cropping, contour farming, and increasing tree cover (e.g., feeding, fodder banks).
- Soil conservation through integrated watershed management.
- Improved agricultural practices on steep slopes through integrated watershed management.
- Reduced rate of erosion, sedimentation, and acidification.

#### Project-promoted measures contributing to climate change
- Reduced rate of erosion, sedimentation, and acidification.
- Exposure to flooding, particularly in the hills due to deforestation and forest degradation.
- Improved agricultural practices on steep slopes through integrated watershed management.
- Soil conservation infrastructure to reduce soil runoff and reduce flows.

# ANNEXES

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 100 OF 517

### BHAVAR REGION

#### Climate change challenges
- Increasing summer and monsoon precipitation, resulting in increasing numbers of moderate rainfall events (e.g., several days of very wet days), as projected by climate models.
- Flooding in the hills, with significant downstream impacts.

#### Description of baseline land use
- **Tamura:** Primarily unsuitable for rain-fed agriculture due to irregular supply of water. Suitable only for small livestock and poultry rearing (e.g., free-ranging chickens). Majority of households use this land for their energy needs.
- **Forests:** Also used as fuelwood, timber and non-timber forest products.

#### Land use showing vulnerability to climate change
- Deforestation and degradation contribute to increased soil erosion and reduced water retention. Such land use trends further exacerbate the problem by reducing floodplains through deforestation and erosion.
- Reduced vegetation cover (from deforestation and degradation) reduces groundwater recharge, increasing the risk of drought for communities exposed to extreme temperatures associated with water deficit.

#### Relevant climate-resilient land use
- Climate-resilient agricultural practices to reduce erosion and improve soil fertility. Improved forest management, including agroforestry.
- Improved grazing practices (e.g., rotational grazing, feeder banks).
- Improved tree cover (e.g., afforestation) for groundwater stabilization in sustainable water sources on vulnerable river banks.

#### Role of project promoted by the Green Climate Fund to address climate change
- Water harvesting and storage to alleviate heat and water stress.
- River bank stabilization and soil conservation.
- Reduced emissions from deforestation and degradation.
- Improved forest quality and productivity.
- Improved groundwater recharge, reducing administrative erosion.
- Improved water security downstream.


---
**Page 105**
---

# ANNEXES

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 101 OF 517

### DUN VALLEY

#### Climate change challenges
- Decrease in average precipitation in the winter season and increase in temperature.
- Increase in the number of consecutive dry days, which is particularly large during summer months.
- Increase in frequency of extreme weather events such as heat waves.

#### Description of baseline land use
- Most households are fuelled for household consumption (woodfuel) and provide forest fodder, forage, timber, non-timber forest products.
- The majority of the most fertile and productive areas are used with steady groundwater irrigation systems to increase area for staple foods such as maize, sorghum, pulses and oilseeds. Many households also grow vegetables in raised in primary low-yield agricultural production systems.

#### Land use showing vulnerability to climate change
- Degradation within the Dun Valley, as well as in the surrounding areas, increases the vulnerability of forest ecosystems to extreme heat.
- Most land management practices do not apply soil conservation and water harvesting practices that focus areas with high rainfall and reduce extreme heat, water deficit and drought. This leads to forest degradation, as well as increased soil erosion from practices that fail to address soil moisture retention, soil degradation and water conservation in areas with dry season crop production.

#### Relevant climate-related land use change
- Climate-resilient agricultural practices to reduce erosion and improve soil fertility.
- Improved water harvesting and overall soil and fertility stabilization in sustainable forest ecosystems.
- Reduced emissions from deforestation and forest degradation on vulnerable river banks, through the establishment of sustainable forest management systems.
- Improved forest quality and resilience by applying engineering soil and water conservation techniques to address sedimentation erosion, improved groundwater recharge, increased stability and resilience.

#### Role of project promoted adaptation practices to climate change
- Water harvesting to alleviate heat and water stress in agricultural areas.
- River bank stabilization through the establishment of sustainable forest ecosystems.
- Reduced emissions from deforestation and forest degradation on vulnerable river banks, through the establishment of sustainable forest management systems.
- Improved forest quality and resilience by applying engineering soil and water conservation techniques to address sedimentation erosion, improved groundwater recharge, increased stability and resilience.


---
**Page 106**
---

# ANNEXES

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 102 OF 517

### TERAI

#### Climate change challenges
- Decrease in average precipitation in the winter season and increase in the number of convective storms, as well as particularly large increases in the frequency of heavy rainfall events.
- Increase in summer and monsoon precipitation, coupled with a decrease in the number of rainy days (as opposed to an increase in the number of dry days).
- Increased pressure on natural resources by communities in the Terai region.

#### Description of baseline land use
- Farmers in the Terai produce most of their food crops (maize, rice, among others) as well as particularly large quantities of pulses and oilseeds.
- The region is also an important area for livestock production relied upon to meet subsistence needs on the hill and mountain areas.

#### Land uses showing vulnerability to climate change
- Decreased land use from forest degradation, with trees above 10m in height.
- Increased agricultural land use due to supply soil erosion or water erosion.
- Increased pressure on natural resources by communities in the Terai region.

#### Relevant climate-related land uses
- Erosion-related agricultural practices to reduce erosion and sedimentation.
- River bank stabilization for flood protection.
- Planted forests are able to provide upland farmers with fodder, firewood, and other non-timber forest products.
- Forest management, biodiversity conservation (conservation areas, wildlife corridors, water walls).
- Erosion control in woodlots and horticulture production to reduce exposure to sedimentation.

#### Role of project promoted practices in addressing climate change
- Practices related to alleviate heat and water stress.
- River bank stabilization for flood protection.
- Upland farmers with fodder, firewood, and other non-timber forest products.

## Sustainability Considerations

- **Adoption of the practices including risk-taking in agroforestry and livestock management:** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **Financing of equipment/tools for climate-smart agriculture and livestock management:** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **Impact on increased revenue streams from these practices:** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **Promoted activities (described in greater detail in the annexes) are expected to have a positive NPV of ≥US$290 on average per farm unit.** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **CBAs and local farmers are relevant in climate-smart agriculture and livestock management:** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **The pilot demonstration has demonstrated that climate-resilient activities like agroforestry and livestock management practices can improve the livelihood of poor farmers, while reducing their vulnerability to climate change.** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **The pilot demonstration has demonstrated that climate-resilient activities like agroforestry and livestock management practices can improve the livelihood of poor farmers, while reducing their vulnerability to climate change.** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **Improved awareness of climate change, climate-smart agriculture and livestock management encourages CBOs to adopt agroforestry and livestock management practices on their own land, while reducing the costs, and additional benefits.** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.
- **Establishment of community nurseries will provide local people to grow low carbon forest trees and legumes for food security and income generation.** The project activities can be financially sustainable and generate additional benefits for CBOs - single users with an NPV ≥ US$175 or equivalent.

**Deliverables:**

- 94 local-level climate change and climate variability risk assessments completed as part of a planning engagement.
- Training in GIS tools for provincial and local government staff.
- A provincial level CEEP developed for critical ecosystem services.

**Impact:** This is particularly relevant given the increasing need to devolve decision-making power to the provincial and local levels. Materials, guidelines, manuals, protocols, and training will be provided to assist with institutionalized knowledge management and capacity building in order to ensure the successful application of this approach in other new systems.


**Sustainability Consideration:** The capacity of governments and stakeholders to understand and assess climate risks, utilize climate information and data, and develop and implement better policies, programs, projects, and practices at local levels is crucial for the future. This will also contribute towards ensuring that national SDMPs are a means for coordinating investments in adaptation and mitigation.

**Priority measures will be implemented through:**

- A provincial level CEEP for each new system aligned with best practice (Level 3) of the Green Climate Fund’s Adaptation Framework.
- Additional funding for further measures (e.g. as per Level 4 of the Adaptation Framework) at Provincial or Provincial Government and/or the President’s discretion.

Both, both have been working on forests, soil and water resources in the same geographical areas.

Engagement of diverse stakeholders, including government officials, local communities, NGOs, CBOs and other relevant actors is critical to ensure commitments and ownership over climate

Outcomes that are better aligned with the needs of communities and ecosystems. Supported by the activities in Component 1, this activity will help provinces to adopt climate-resilient practices, with ongoing communication and capacity building on adaptation. Based on the demonstration of this process, this project will support the government of Nepal to scale up its efforts at the national level. This is due to strengthened capacities, improved coordination and better planning outcomes. The project will also provide a notable benefit for the government in Nepal in terms of increased resilience of communities, strengthened livelihoods and ecosystems. |

| Land use planning processes are strengthened through participatory land use planning workshops with participating provincial land use planning officials and representatives from provincial governments and CBOs. Combined with other activities under this component, this will result in more resilient land use practices, and implement a holistic approach for the management of climate change related and non-climate related land use. Such an approach will also help to address the needs of communities facing climate resilient planning in Nepal, which will be reflected in their provincial plans. This is necessary to support the country's needs, goals and objectives as outlined in its NDCs. The project will also provide a notable benefit for the government in Nepal in terms of increased capacity to quickly switch into the capacities of a participatory approach for land use planning and adaptation of necessary adaptation measures. |

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project Building a Resilient Churia Region in Nepal as included in the funding proposal submitted by Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) to us on 18 September 2018.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of Finance, International Economic Cooperation Coordination Division, Government of Nepal.

Pursuant to GCF decision 6.04/(b), in the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the project (or programme) as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Nepal has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project is being implemented in conformity with Nepal's national priorities, strategies and plans;
(c) In accordance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations;

We also confirm that our national process for accertaining no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of this project.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP118

Proposal name: Building a Resilient Churia Region in Nepal (BRCRN)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country(ies): Nepal

Project/programme size: Small


I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The level of recipients' needs is high as the project targets one of the least developed countries and one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change. The project addresses the impacts of increasing temperatures and changing precipitation patterns caused by climate change. | Weak enforcement capacity may cause it to be challenging to change the status quo of land degradation, especially after the project's completion |
| The project takes an integrated sustainable natural resource management and cross-sectoral approach that takes into consideration the interlinkages of ecosystems and land-use systems. |  |
| There is strong stakeholder engagement, including with government, development and community-based organizations. |  |

2. Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions as per the respective term sheet and addendum XIII titled "List of proposed conditions and requirements".

II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

2.1 Project background

The Churia region of Nepal is characterized by the critical nature of its geographical location, its socio-economic marginality, ecological sensitivity, and its sensitivity to climate change. The region supports a large number of vulnerable populations in plains downstream, including groundwater regulation, prevention of siltation, flood control and the supply of water for irrigation purposes. For decades the natural resources of the region have been managed in an unsustainable manner. This created a vicious cycle of land degradation, causing decreased productivity which in turn


---
**Page 126**
---

caused further land degradation and the unsustainable extraction of natural resources, resulting in sedimentation, landslides and erosion in downstream farmlands. This situation is further exacerbated by the effects of climate change - increasing temperatures, changing precipitation patterns, more frequent and intense droughts, and increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events.

The proposed project will take an integrated approach to enhancing the resilience of ecosystems and communities against these impacts through a combination of measures: climate-resilient land-use practices, restoration of ecosystems and forests, confronting the challenges posed by climate change, and improving livelihoods. The proposed interventions are categorized by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the accredited entity (AE), as a category B project with medium risk.

The total project cost is USD 473 million with a request to GCF for finance USD 39.3 million (10 per cent). The remaining 90 per cent will be covered by the Government of Nepal through the Ministry of Forests and Environment (MoFE).

### Component-by-component analysis

**Component 1: Scaling up climate-resilient natural resource management (SNRM) (total cost USD 34.5 million with a request to GCF USD 2.837 million, or 8.29 per cent)**

The project will target 26 of the most vulnerable river systems within the selected three provinces and two municipalities. The project will be delivered by community-based organizations (CBOs) and their members, as well as local government, with technical and financial support to scale up sustainable practices, including:

- Conservation agriculture
- The restoration of 302,973 ha of critical forest, wetland and grassland ecosystem; and
- Tree planting on 238,800 ha as new forest systems.

This support will be delivered in each of the targeted river systems, guided by river system-specific plans. This is a component-by-component approach, where specific measures are selected considering differentiated vulnerabilities within each river system.

Activities in this component will absorb the majority of GCF finance (84 per cent). The project will focus on addressing the underlying drivers of land degradation in the project area, leading to further emission reductions through reduced deforestation and forest degradation as well as improved carbon sequestration.

**Component 2: Strengthening institutions and financing for climate-resilient SNRM (total cost USD 31.8 million with a request to GCF USD 4.697 million or 14.7 per cent)**

As a precursor to guide investments in component 1, activities in component 2 will support provincial and local government stakeholders and CBOs in identifying and assessing climate-related risks and vulnerabilities, as well as developing locally relevant plans to respond to the identified risks.

The proposed interventions in this component are critical not only to ensuring investment readiness but also to ensure that the project is able to also equip government stakeholders and CBOs to be able to continue investing in climate-resilient SNRM beyond project completion. This will be achieved by strengthening institutions, capacity building, and delegating the responsibilities for natural resource management to provincial and local governments.


---
**Page 127**
---

### Component 3: Improving knowledge, awareness and local capacity for climate-resilient SNRM (total cost USD 1 million; GCF cost: USD 0.45 million or 26 per cent)

#### 11. Component 3 will improve the awareness of climate change threats and build the capacities necessary to continuously monitor climate-change risks and vulnerabilities at the project level. This component will include training for extension workers, development of materials and guidelines for farmer-field schools, scaling up the reach of extension services, and training of trainers in climate-resilient SNRM practices. A climate change awareness and education centre will also be established to enhance climate monitoring, knowledge management and dissemination.

#### 12. The proposed activities under this component are complementary to components 2 and 3 in setting a strong enabling environment to sustain and scale up the SNRM interventions following their adoption as national policy.

### Component 4: Project management (total cost USD 0.94 million; GCF cost: USD 0.158 million or 77 per cent)

#### 13. Project management costs will be covered by GCF and the Government of Nepal proportional to the overall contribution to the project. The total project management cost is around 4.2 per cent of the total project cost, which is compliant with the GCF policy on fees.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

14. The project addresses environmental degradation through multiple angles, leading to significantly improved climate-resilient communities and environmental resilience in the region. Climate change is affecting the agricultural production potential of the region and increasing vulnerability among the poor. The proposed interventions will positively impact both issues, leading to direct benefits for nearly 1 million people in the project area, and over 3.2 million indirect beneficiaries in the downstream areas of the project.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
**Scale: High**

15. The project takes a systems-based approach that promotes integrated land management. The integration of the areas of SNRM in new to Nepal, thus this project has significant scalability potential for other regions in the country and worldwide with regard to its successful practices.

16. Following the adoption of Nepal’s New Constitution in 2010, governance in the country has developed to reach sub-national levels of government. Through its institutional capacity-building approach, this project will contribute to lower level governments, ensuring the continued consideration of SNRM beyond the lifetime of the project.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
**Scale: High**

17. Sustainable Development Goal (SDG): The project will deliver crucial economic benefits through its support for agricultural development. Its direct beneficiaries, which are mostly poor and landless farmers, will benefit from increased soil fertility, improved agricultural productivity, the greater availability of fuelwood and timber, and reduced exposure to disasters.


---
**Page 128**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

1. Nepal is a least developed country and is ranked as the world’s fourth most vulnerable country to climate change. The project area lies on the Churia hills, which provide vital ecosystem functions to the heavily populated plains downstream in Nepal and India.
2. Nepal is also susceptible to natural disasters such as earthquakes. The most recent major earthquake of 2015 caused an estimated economic damage of USD 10 billion, approximately 50 per cent of original gross domestic product. Other natural disasters include landslides and extreme rainfall floods. As a result, susceptibility to natural disasters, its fiscal space is very restricted.

3. Country ownership

Scale: High

22. The project is aligned with key policy frameworks and co-financing is being provided by the Nepal Government.
23. The project responds directly to Nepal’s first nationally determined contribution, with specific reference to the following of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): “i) Building climate resilience of watersheds in mountain eco-regions; ii) Building resilience to climate change impacts on agriculture and food security; iii) Promoting sustainable livelihoods.” Apart from that, the project references national policies of forests, biodiversity, ecosystem-based adaptation and pro-poor strategies.
24. The Ministry of Forests is currently engaged in the development of the funding proposal, which is one of the key programmes of the MoFE.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: High

15. The mitigation impact of the project is estimated at USD 4.24 per tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent, with an external rate of return of 81 per cent over 20 years, inclusive of ecosystem benefits, and 20 per cent excluding them. The project invests USD 5M in direct biodiversity conservation activities.
16. Project co-financing is relatively limited at 17 per cent, being provided by the Government of Nepal. FAO or the AE will not provide any co-financing.

IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

25. The project aims to enhance the climate resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable communities in the Churia region of Nepal through integrated SNMIR approaches. The interventions will be undertaken in 26 river systems in the region and will provide benefits to women, particularly those living in remote areas and those who are members of indigenous GCBs.
To achieve its objective, the project will undertake activities organized into the following


---
**Page 129**
---

components: (1) scaling-up of climate-resilient SNRM; (2) strengthening institutions and planning for climate-resilient SNRM; and (3) improving knowledge, awareness, and local capacity for climate-resilient SNRM.

The AE considered the project as having a potential moderate environmental and social risk due to its scale and scope. The AE has given consideration the likely adverse environmental and social risks and impacts of the project, which it finds to be less adverse and fewer in number than those associated with other projects of similar size and scope. Environmental and social risks and impacts are considered site-specific, and mitigation measures have been readily designed and implemented for each risk and impact identified.

The AE has submitted an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) since the project is expected to have a moderate level of environmental and social risk. The ESMF describes the processes for integrating environmental and social considerations in the activities. The ESMF describes how the project will be managed, including the identification of risks and impacts and planning of measures to avoid, minimize and mitigate the identified risks and impacts.

Incorporated in the ESMF are an indigenous peoples planning framework, biodiversity management plan, gender equality action plan, land use and tenure management plan, and grievance redress systems that will be established for the project.


Key partners and stakeholders involved in the project include:

1. Indigenous peoples: These individuals form approximately 31% of the population in the project area, of which 50% are women, and according to the project logic will both form 31% of the direct beneficiaries and 31% of the indirect beneficiaries.

2. Indigenous peoples' organizations: The AE has provided for their inclusion under the Environmental and Social Management Guidelines. The AE has provided an Indigenous Peoples and Social Inclusion (IPSI) Framework, which is a key component of the ESMF. Including Dalits along with indigenous peoples in their consideration of vulnerable groups, as well as ensuring that they are included in decision-making processes, will enhance positive benefits. Further participation could be enhanced by identifying links, if any, between the project and other initiatives or programs being implemented by government institutions within the AE such as the Interdepartmental Working Group, FPC Task Force and the internal caucus of indigenous peoples' local points.

3. Indigenous peoples' organizations: The AE has signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the National Association of Indigenous and Tribal Peoples Convention, 1989 (No. 169), that was ratified in 2007. Both national and AE commitments expressed under this MoU are included within the IPSI Framework. As part of the project framework, outcomes are explicitly conditional on whether FPC agreements have been signed prior to implementation.

4. Indigenous peoples' organizations: Including indigenous organizations, and national indigenous women's organizations, were included in the consultations for the development of the funding proposal. The AE has provided for their inclusion under the Environmental and Social Management Guidelines. Indigenous peoples is explicitly mentioned at the level of the Provincial Coordination Units, as well as representation 'engaged' in the PCC where they will be invited to discuss thematic issues, and project planning. A larger time is dedicated to ensuring the participation of indigenous peoples in the PMC and FPC.

5. The IPSI Framework ensures the creation of an Indigenous Peoples and Social Inclusion Plan as a separate IP SMP for each of the four provinces involved. The IPSI Framework describes impact and mitigation measures including that the project will not be

### Biodiversity and natural habitats

The project is expected to improve the ecosystem services provided by the area through SNRM. However, it is likely that some of the project activities will be implemented within an established protected area (Koshu Tappu Wildlife Reserve). It should be noted that the activities will not reduce biodiversity value of the area and impact its ecosystem services. The project will also comply with the requirements of the AE and the GCF. While the project will support forest plantations andrews proposed by local communities, it is important to note that this approach does not protect biodiversity resources and manage and mitigate potential impacts on them.

### Involuntary resettlement

The result of the environmental and social screening conducted for the project indicated that the project would not likely lead to any involuntary resettlement. The project will not have a significant impact on local communities' access to forest resources on private land. Investments in public lands, either non-forested or degraded, will provide opportunities for local communities to benefit from sustainable management of their forest resources. The participation of public land users and the local communities in the project activities is expected to be voluntary.

### Construction-related environmental and social impacts

The project will not support activities that result in a negative change to existing legitimate rights and interests of indigenous peoples, including access to forests and other natural resources.


---
**Page 131**
---

scale of such infrastructure, the risks and impacts from construction are expected to be limited and temporary. Construction environmental management plans will be prepared prior to any construction works to ensure that appropriate mitigation measures are prepared and implemented.

The ESMP includes a list of activities that are excluded from the project's support. These excluded activities are considered to have likely environmental and social risk factors that may raise the likelihood of negative impacts on the environment or communities. The excluded activities will not be supported by the project include those that would: (1) result in negative change to existing ecosystems; (2) result in significant loss of biodiversity; (3) result in the construction of medium to large infrastructure; (4) increase greenhouse gas emissions substantially; (5) implement in protected areas; (6) involve direct supply of agrochemicals; and (7) support clearing of natural vegetation.

The ESMP provided the AE's screening checklist which will be used as the main tool for identifying potential environmental and social risks. The ESMP also includes a list of follow-up assessments, follow-ups and developing mitigation measures are provided in the ESMP including the timeframe for undertaking these due diligence activities.

The ESMP provides detailed information on the project's approach to ongoing stakeholder engagement during the implementation of the project, building on the extensive consultations that were undertaken as part of the project preparation phase. The ESMP also includes details from the stakeholder workshops and consultation undertaken since 2017. Stakeholder engagement measures are integrated into each activity planned for the BRCKN project and include continuous monitoring and evaluation to ensure that the project is meeting its commitments, information dissemination, and communication and feedback. Annexes A of the ESMP includes detailed overview of the stakeholder engagement plan.

MoPF will serve as the main executing entity (EE), supported by FAO. A project steering committee, chaired by MoPF, will be formed to provide overall guidance to project design and implementation. The Provincial Multi-Partner Implementation Mechanism (PMIM) within MoPF will be formed and composed of provincial level supporting units. The PMIMs are responsible for the coordination between MoPF and the provinces, Ministry of Health and Environment. Within the provinces, provincial coordination units will be created that would include representatives from the provincial government, local communities, NGOs and civil society organizations, including women's groups, indigenous peoples organizations and federations, and others.

A grievance redress mechanism has been developed to receive and address complaints that may arise from the project. This project level grievance redress mechanism compliments the AE's grievance redress mechanism as outlined in the IFC Performance Standards and Principles for Business and Human Rights. The detailed steps and procedures are outlined in the ESMP and will be implemented by MoPF, with support from FAO and other partners. Traditional or customary dispute management practices are also integrated into the project level grievance redress mechanism.

The ESMP includes a detailed ESMP workplan that includes well-defined tasks, progress indicators, schedules, responsibility assignments, and costs, including budget dedicated to both the ESMP and the project activities.


### Gender policy

The AE has submitted a comprehensive gender assessment; therefore, it complies with the operational guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. The AE has also provided a gender action plan for the project.


---
**Page 132**
---

### Gender Assessment

The gender assessment provides a comprehensive overview of the gender situation in Nepal, particularly within the Churia region. The assessment was completed through a desk review and extensive consultations with women's representatives, CBOs, and communities themselves.

The assessment highlights several key challenges faced by women in this region, including:

- **Political Representation**: Women face significant barriers to participation in politics and decision-making bodies due to a minimum 33% female representation requirement.
- **Economic Factors**: Women are disproportionately affected by persistent poverty, lack of capacity, and limited access to resources such as land titles, credit, and agricultural inputs. This is exacerbated by the fact that women often have lower levels of education compared to men.
- **Workload and Livelihoods**: Women in rural areas spend approximately 10.5 to 13 hours a day maintaining their families' livelihoods, particularly in agriculture. This heavy workload has added to women's challenges as they face labor shortages when working on agricultural land, forcing them into early retirement or even abandonment of their households.
- **Agricultural Practices**: Women often lack access to information and training about extension services or access to labor-saving practices and technologies.

### Gender Action Plan

The gender assessment findings are integrated into the gender action plan, which includes activities designed to help women overcome barriers they face in participating fully and benefiting from interventions planned through this project. The action plan focuses on empowering women by addressing their empowerment through activities, mainstreaming gender and inclusion issues in policies and regulations, and ensuring that women's concerns are addressed in all aspects of the project.

The action plan also includes:

- **Empowerment Activities**: These aim to enhance women's participation in decision-making processes.
- **Policy and Regulation Mainstreaming**: Ensuring that gender equality is integrated into all levels of policy and regulation.
- **Monitoring and Evaluation Framework**: Regularly assessing progress towards achieving the project's objectives.

The proposed action plan has been vetted by relevant stakeholders, including representatives from women's organizations and civil society groups. It aims to ensure that women's concerns are incorporated based on the results of the gender assessment and the logical framework of the funding proposal. The action plan also includes a budget for activities (10% of the total project budget) and timelines for all activities.

The gender action plan will establish baseline conditions, conduct monitoring and evaluation, and ensure that women's voices are heard throughout the process.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall proposal assessment (medium risk):

- GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 39.39 million for promoting climate-resilient land-use practices, addressing the challenges of deforestation and enhancing the resilience of ecosystems in Nepal.
- The project will be implemented by the AE and FAO, with an initial contribution from the GCF of USD 8.04 million; co-financing will be provided by the AE.

#### 4.3.2 AE/EE capability to execute the current programme (low risk):

- The AE, FAO, has worked in Nepal since 1951, completing over 400 projects.
- FAO will establish a Technical Capacity Development Team in the FAO Nepal office, which will serve as a secondary capacity development unit for the project.

#### 3.2.2 The funding proposal mentioned that FAO will ensure the segregation of the functions of the AE and EE.

#### 4.3.3 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk):

- The project involves interventions on priority owned lands as well as community lands.
- Requires active engagement by government institutions/EEs with the communities involved financially and technically, which may lead to implementation challenges.
- Previous pilot projects indicated that issues related to tenure insecurity and lack of awareness among stakeholders may hinder progress.

#### 4.3.4 Political transitioning:

- The Government of Nepal is currently decentralizing and establishing a new governance structure.
- As per the constitution, the transition is well under way, but it presents challenges for the AE and EE in building institutional capacity of the new provincial government ministries that will be significant to project implementation.

#### 4.3.5 Finalization of the locations of interventions:

- The funding proposal aims to cover large geographical areas in 26 different river systems, including 86 upstream locations, dams, and conservation sites.
- Interventions are planned for each river system following a detailed assessment of the needs of each river system and the development of critical ecosystem restoration plans. Based on these plans, the project will identify priority interventions.

## Economic and financial viability as per financial and economic analysis

It results in negative net present value during the project implementation period (seven years) due to its non-revenue-generating aspects and long-term impacts, such as tree planting and long-term land management. However, the project is expected to be economically viable over its 20-year lifetime with an external rate of return of 20 per cent. An analysis considering the social value of carbon decrease at a global scale (e.g., increased biodiversity), the social value of carbon decrease at a national scale were performed. The project is expected to maintain a positive net present value over a 20-year period.

### Compliance risk (low risk)

The proposed project does not raise major concerns in relation to integrity risks – particularly those related to money-laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) vulnerabilities. However, certain elements of the proposed activities may entail a heightened exposure to the risk of ML/TF. These include the potential for some private forest land users to be included as forest user group subset of CRBs. Similarly, the provision of benefits to private land user groups could also elevate the likelihood of GCF resources being applied to support illicitly acquired property.

In response to the key risks identified above, the AE has proposed a mitigation strategy which includes: (i) ensuring that all project activities are carried out by entities whose operations are not accepted by CRBs. In addition, all donations to CRBs above USD 100 will be made through the formal banking system, which is subject to anti-money-laundering/countering financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) regulations. As part of this strategy, the AE has confirmed that although the project will work with some private forest owners under activity L.3 & L.4, these activities are not expected to be carried out on private land. In case of an extension of private woodlots, the AE will ensure that sites are proposed by MoEF after consultation with CRBs and other relevant stakeholders, in accordance with guidelines by MoEF and FAO experts, and finalized through dialogue with private land owners, according to the principles outlined above.

Overall, the AE confirms that GCF proceeds will be managed solely by MoEF and FAO (as primary and secondary EEs, respectively). The project will also engage service providers through formal contracts. As such, the AE has confirmed that all procurement activities follow necessary procurement procedures and controls to ensure compliance with international AML/CFT regulations. In addition, as part of the tendering process for the woodlot upgrade, the AE commissioned a capacity assessment of MoEF by a qualified firm, which identified MoEF as an appropriate entity in accordance with its procurement processes. Nevertheless, the FAO technical assistance team has also received a qualified assurance that the support service to directly support MoEF with its procurement actions under this project to ensure international AML/CFT compliance is provided.

These mitigating factors are expected to significantly decrease the risk of ML/TF and other integrity risks to a low probability.

### GCF portfolio composition risk (low risk)

Should the Board approve this funding proposal, its impact on the GCF portfolio composition in terms of results area and single proposals is minimal.

## Recommendations

It is recommended that the Board take the above factors into account when considering this proposal.

# Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting project, the adaptation interventions of the investment expect to benefit directly approximately 252,000 beneficiaries. The project will deliver benefits directly to 30 per cent of the targeted population and indirectly to 100 per cent of the targeted population. Of the targeted population, women comprise 481,634 direct and 1.6 million indirect beneficiaries.

The project has a baseline carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) coverage of approximately 167,830 t CO2e under improved management; the mitigation intervention will reduce this to an estimated 59,000 t CO2e with a decrease in the cost per t CO2e from US$ 17.31 to $10.00.

Overall, the funding proposal and logical framework sufficiently apply GCF Fund-level (impact) indicators and project-level (output) indicators. The project has built in baseline data collection that can inform progress monitoring and evaluation of the project’s outputs. In addition, the project includes a combination of assistance/training-based grant projects, measurements for behavioral change have been integrated into the project design.

Regarding section H1.3 of the funding proposal, overall the logical framework complies with GCF standards and has been cleared through Secretariat review.

The funding proposal theory of change would benefit from further details reflecting on causal pathways and intermediate outcomes. The proposed rationale (which ideally are tested in implementation either with project performance management indicators or impact data/evaluation) is not sufficiently detailed to support the theory of change.

Under section H2.7 of the funding proposal, the Secretariat notes the intention for FAG to conduct formative/process evaluations to the interim and final evaluation requirements to assess the effectiveness of the project’s outputs and outcomes. The Secretariat also notes application of the intention to conduct mixed-methods data collection within the project activities and reporting framework.

Additionally, in the detailed budget, the AE needs to ensure that sufficient budget is allocated to conduct formative process steps and collect essential project-level data for reports resulting to enable adaptive management.


## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 8 June 2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Nepal, country in which GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. In the event that the Republic of Nepal is a party to any international agreement or treaty, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks needs to be further assessed by the Secretariat. The AE should ensure that the privileges and immunities agreement to the government of Nepal on 23 May 2019.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been informed about the project. The IRM and IIU will monitor and address activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Any approval by the Board for the implementation of GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

The Secretariat recommends that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:
