# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP118

## Proposal name: Building a Resilient Churia Region in Nepal (BRCRN)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium/high

#### 1. Nepal is a landlocked country in South Asia with an estimated population of 30 million.

The country lies in the central part of the Great Himalayas, with high mountainous plains. The highland regions embrace the Himalayan range with elevations between 4,877 m and 8,849 m (Mount Everest). The midland region accounts for about 64 per cent of the total land area of the country.

It is formed by the Terai plain in the south and the Mahabharat hills in the north. The Terai range: The Terai is a lowland range in the southern part of the country. It is the flat river plains of the Ganges, Brahmaputra, and Kosi rivers, which are covered with forests. The Terai range occupies about 17 per cent of the total land area of the country.

Despite recent economic progress, the country’s per capita income remains low as an average USD 764 per year (2015). Agriculture is the mainstay of Nepal’s economy and contributes largely to the country’s economy. Agriculture (including livestock) and forestry are key economic activities in Nepal. The agricultural sector directly or indirectly employs over 70 per cent of the population. This makes the country's economy dependent on natural resources as well as stable and predictable climate conditions.

The Terai region is a major food basket for the people living in North India. It provides vital ecosystem functions to the heavily populated Terai plains downstream, including groundwater recharge, flood regulation, and soil conservation, which are essential for agriculture, including fuel and construction materials. In addition, the Terai-Mahabharat area of Nepal is responsible for the majority of the country’s agricultural produce. Together, the Churia and Terai areas are home to 70 per cent of the population.

Nepal has been experiencing significant impacts from climate change over the past 40 years. The average temperature in Nepal has increased by about 1.3°C (2.3°F) since 1965, with modelled average annual temperatures could increase by 3.8°C (7°F) and 1.8–5.8°C (3.2–10°F), compared with 1986–2005. Monsoon precipitation could increase by up to 40 per cent over the next four decades, while average annual rainfall and precipitation could decrease by around 10 per cent. The average number of dry days and consecutive dry days is projected to increase further in the coming decades.


---
**Page 139**
---

6. Communities in the Churia region are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Many depend on rainfed subsistence rate-of-agriculture, so their predominant livelihood requires stable climatic conditions and a healthy natural resource base. The agricultural practices broadly follow traditional methods that are not resilient against extreme weather events or physical terrain, particularly if climate conditions change as anticipated.

7. The Building a Resilient Churia Region in Nepal (BRCRN) project promotes integrated sustainable land management approaches to enhance the resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable communities in the Churia region. The project advances climate-resilient land use practices that are adapted to local environmental conditions, including those related to climate-induced hazards. The capacity of local government, local communities and other stakeholders to understand and manage these risks will be enhanced through a participatory approach. The benefits delivered by the project can be sustained and enhanced even after the project's completion.

8. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) is the accredited entity (AE) for the Ministry of Forests and Environment (MoFE) of Nepal as its executing entity (EE).

9. The total project cost is estimated to be USD 67.34 million, and the project requires USD 59.1 million in core funding from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and co-financing with USD 8.04 million in co-financing from the MoFE.

10. The project focuses on the central/eastern Churia Terai region where 26 vulnerable river systems drain into the Ganges River Basin. These river systems span 121 rural municipalities in provinces 1, 2 and 3, where there are over 3.2 million people, of which 51 per cent are women and children under five years old. The project will focus on improving food security and socioeconomic inclusion. The project will work with 750 community-based organizations (CBOs).

11. The project consists of three components. Component 1 (USD 34.1 million) is to support activities to scale up proven climate-resilient SNRM to strengthen ecosystem stability and resilience in the Churia Terai region. These activities include: (i) establishment of agroforestry; restoration and sustainable management in ecosystems, and tree planting on non-forested land to build soil fertility and improve water retention capacity, especially in the upstream Terai. This support will be delivered to each of the 26 target river systems.

12. Activities in component 1 include (1) the establishment of 120 farmer field schools (FFS) to train farmers on climate-resilient SNRM practices; (2) the promotion and sustainable forest management in all forest land managed by forestry-related CBOs to ensure silvicultural practices are adapted to local environmental conditions, including those related to climate resilience and forest carbon stock enhancement; and (3) creation of 52 multipurpose tree nurseries to serve the planting and restoration needs of CBOs and other forest sector stakeholders.

13. Component 2 (USD 2.6 million) supports learning and preparation for the interventions proposed in component 3. This includes: (a) capacity building for provincial, district, provincial and local governments and CBOs to understand and apply climate-resilient SNRM; (b) development of a knowledge management system.

14. Component 3 (USD 8.6 million) will improve knowledge, awareness, and local capacity for climate-resilient SNRM in the Churia Terai region through: (a) the establishment of an information dissemination to support awareness-raising and the enhancement of adaptive capacity; and (b) the establishment of the Churia Knowledge Centre (CKC) will be used to promote systematic knowledge and learning sharing on improving climate-resilient SNRM.

15. The project aims to improve the resilience of ecosystems and communities in the Churia region. Direct beneficiaries include at least 200,681 households or 93,248 people equivalent.


---
**Page 140**
---

to about 30 per cent of the inhabitants in the project area is in the 24 target river systems. The project will support the enhanced resilience of ecosystems on 270,502 ha of land. This indirectly further reduce vulnerability to climate change related risks for other inhabitants in the Churun River Basin and the Beni River Basin. Indigenous peoples are among those who live in the project area, of which 31 per cent are indigenous peoples and 13 per cent are Dalits.

In addition to its adaptation impacts, the project has an impact on greenhouse gas emissions. The project is expected to reduce deforestation and forest degradation and improve forest management in the project area. This will lead to a reduction in carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions of about 50 million annual emission reductions of 574,180 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) or 11.40 million tonnes of CO2 over the lifetime of the project. The project also meets the climate change mitigation impact indicators following the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories principles for land-based accounting.

The World Bank's Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) assesses the impact potential of the project as "medium/High".

### Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium

#### Potential for scaling up and replication
- The project is the first ecosystem-wide initiative in the Churun region to move beyond pilot and demonstration projects. It integrates adaptation planning into multi-local sectoral development planning processes.
- It enhances the planning and capacity of CMNs to develop climate change adaptation plans, including the establishment of a Climate Change Network (CCN).
- CMNs will receive support for the development of "critical ecosystem restoration plans", the enhancement of forest management operations, and the strengthening of forest management operational plans. The extension system will be enhanced in order to provide technical assistance to CMNs.
- The project is expected to continue applying climate-resilient SNMR measures. The establishment of the CCN also plays a key role in ensuring that the extension system has access to the latest data and information about climate change and natural resources.

The project integrates a range of aspects of climate vulnerability and adaptive capacity, rural livelihoods and poverty reduction, and institutional strengthening into its planning for the Churun region. The project proposal describes the financial, social and environmental benefits to be accrued by provincial and local governments in the Churun region that adopt climate-resilient SNMR at scale to address ecological climate change challenges.

The project is aligned with existing government strategies and is complementary to other ongoing initiatives. It will provide technical assistance for CMNs to support provincial and local government entities to strengthen their planning and capacity to continue to support and promote climate-resilient SNMR beyond project closure. Close coordination and collaboration with authorities at the provincial level (provincial) entity is promoted so that local ownership and sustainability are ensured.

#### Potential for knowledge and learning
- Under the project, provincial and local government authorities will be trained in climate change adaptation planning, which includes support to identify critical hotspots and conduct a mapping activity in each river system. Such knowledge and learning are essential and will be effective given the new roles that provincial and local governments assume in natural resource management.


---
**Page 141**
---

10. The project aims to organize workshops where at least 750 local CBOs will be able to raise awareness about climate change risks and climate-resilient land use practices appropriate to their local contexts and priorities. By participating in the workshops, communities will be trained on how to integrate climate resilience into their land use planning processes and continue to update knowledge and seek technical support, such as CRC, local extension agents and radio programs. The project will also provide training for 100 CBOs on adaptation in water, agriculture and forests, which will consolidate local and indigenous knowledge and experience. Trainers (including women, indigenous peoples, Dalits and Madhesis) will be selected from the communities to participate in the field.

### 1.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

13. The project will establish an institutional and planning framework conducive to implementing its activities. Given the Government's decision to hand over forest and agriculture-related responsibilities to local governments, the project will support the capacity-building of local and provincial authorities on climate-resilient planning, disaster risk reduction and land use planning. This will ensure that climate-resilient land use will enhance the Government’s commitment and promote climate-resilient land use planning with an integrated approach for upstream and downstream management. The capacity building activities will be conducted in collaboration with the concerned local government authorities to ensure the adoption and scaling up of climate-resilient practices at the household level.

14. The project will also facilitate awareness creation on knowledge-generation and dissemination activities, which can further enable the enabling environment after project completion.

### 1.2.4 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

25. The project will promote the integration of climate-resilient SNRM into provincial strategic plans and action plans. This will enable the Government to orient investments and priorities toward climate-resilient land use practices and to encourage local governments to implement climate-resilient land use planning in their respective districts as part of the system action plan. The project's efforts to build the capacity of local and provincial authorities with community participation will ensure that the necessary institutional arrangements, policies and measures to be promoted will contribute to the implementation of Nepal’s nationally determined contributions (NDCs) as well as various national policies and plans, including the President’s Climate Change Action Plan, the National Forest Policy, the Water Sector Strategy and the Agriculture Sector Strategy.

26. The project will promote the application of an integrated climate-resilient SNRM approach to all areas, from upstream, hill terrain to the downstream, upper Terai region in Nepal. The ITAP considers that the approach can be considered innovative.

17. The project will establish 300 DPFs and 20 CRCs as planned and is expected to be implemented after project closure. The project will establish 120 DFS and the CRCs, and will enhance their capacity for climate-resilient land use planning in order to reduce adverse impacts after project closure. As per the AE analysis, additional funding is not required, because of the incremental costs required to implement the measures promoted by the project are modest and therefore can be covered within the existing budget. The ITAP considers that it will be economically and socially if they adopt those approaches. However, the ITAP still views the sustainability of these projects after project closure as uncertain in the absence of a dedicated funding arrangement.

28. Accordingly, the ITAP regards the paradigm shift potential of the project as "medium."

# Sustainable development potential

## Economic co-benefits

The project will realize economic co-benefits by increasing agricultural production and income for smallholder farmers through support for climate-resilient agricultural practices that improve soil quality and water availability, and reduce land degradation. The project-supported practices strengthen the natural resource base on which productive agriculture depends. The benefits of these practices are expected to be felt across the Churua region, where agriculture is the backbone of economic activity.

The project will also deliver co-benefits that can contribute significantly on climate change development in the Churua region. The adoption of climate resilient land use practices, as well as measures for ecosystem restoration, water conservation, gully stabilization and afforestation, will reduce the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts. These actions will yield savings for governments, communities and households that can be invested in local economic development.

## Social co-benefits

The project aims to deliver social co-benefits, such as the reduction of waterborne diseases as a result of better managed water conservation ponds and the reduction of collection distances for firewood, water and food. The frequency of soil erosion, problems associated with flooding and landslides will be reduced.

The project aims to achieve proportional representation of indigenous peoples (at least 31 per cent of direct beneficiaries are indigenous peoples) and to ensure that they are empowered and that their rights are safeguarded. Further, the project aims to enhance social inclusion by ensuring that women and other vulnerable groups have equal access to climate change adaptation measures.

The project will empower women and promote their equality through targeted measures (eg training sessions on business literacy for women's groups, ensuring representation of women trainers). At least 50 per cent of direct beneficiaries will be women, of which there is proportional representation of indigenous women, Dalit women, and other vulnerable groups will be ensured.

The TAP considers the sustainable development potential of the project to be 'High'.

## Needs of the recipient

### Vulnerability of the country

Communities in the Churua region are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Many of them depend on subsistence rain-fed agriculture and have little capacity to manage risks and mitigate adverse effects of climate change. The communities where the project will be implemented are home to more than 3,2 million inhabitants, of which 51 per cent are women and 77 per cent are Dalits. At least 90 per cent of the population is rural.

The project will work with 750 CHS comprising approximately 208,681 member households or WLS. Eighty-five per cent of these households are headed by women. At least 31 per cent of the project's beneficiaries are indigenous peoples and 13 per cent Dalits.

### Vulnerable groups and gender aspects

The population in the project area is over 3.2 million, representing 11 percent of the national population. Diverse cultural, ethnic and caste groups inhabit the project area. At least 75 percent of the population lives below the poverty line and areas considered to have experienced intergenerational economic exclusion.

### Absence of alternative sources of financing

#### The total project cost of USD 9.24 million is to be financed by a USD 39.3 million grant from GCF and OSM 0.14 million in co-financing from the FMO.

#### GCF funding will not be available if there are no alternative sources of financing. In the absence of alternative sources of financing, Nepal is a low-income country, and the Government, with its limited financial budget, cannot provide sufficient resources to support the project activities. Most households in communities in the target region are poor and predominantly subsistence households. As the project and its activities are not revenue generating, private sector funding can be expected.

### The need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

#### Following the Government's decision to hand over forest and agricultural resources to the provincial and local levels, there is a need to enhance the capacity of the provincial and district development needs to be enhanced at the provincial and local levels. Additional technical and financial support will be required to support the project activities and strengthen institutional capacity.

#### CRBs have demonstrated their ability to responsibly manage natural resources. However, they are not yet equipped with climate change through and local level vulnerability. Thus, capacity-building and technical support for CRBs is needed for them to understand climate risks at the community level and implement carbon and climate-resilient land use management practices and disaster risk reduction measures.

#### The IFAP considers the project "high" against this criterion.


### Country ownership

#### Alignment with national climate strategy

1. The NDC of 2014 highlighted the country's vulnerability to climate change, water-induced disasters and extreme hydro-meteorological events such as droughts, storms, floods, landslides, and glacial lake outburst floods. The Government has identified nine priority areas for adaptation, which include (1) promoting community-based adaptation through the integrated management of the agriculture, water, forest and biodiversity sectors; (2) building and enhancing the resilience of communities to climate change impacts on ecosystems and access to services related to agriculture development; and (3) forest and ecosystem management in support of sustainable livelihoods and consumption innovations.

#### Coherence with existing policies

14. The President Chure-Tara Madhesh Conservation and Management Master Plan (2017) provides the strategic direction for conservation of the Chura area. Its key objectives include mitigation of damage likely to be caused by climate change and natural disasters through the sustainable use of forest resources, water management, and land-use planning in the vicinity of the Chure hills and Bhuvan region.

### The Fourteenth Development Plan (2017) of Nepal recognizes the role of forests in complementing climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts and calls for the conservation and sustainable management and use of forests. It further notes the challenges faced by forests due to deforestation, degradation, over-exploitation, unsustainable land use, and the fragility of the ecosystem. It promotes the implementation of integrated forest resource management (IFRM) as a means of addressing these challenges, considering the interconnectedness of upstream and downstream natural resources.

15. The project is closely aligned with the country's national policies and existing plans applicable for the region and sectors.

Nepal was natural resource conservation and utilization, which includes measures to enhance sustainable forest management, water resources development, and integrated land use for sustainable forest and watershed management, institutional capacity-building for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

It has supported the implementation of INRM in the Chura and Tara Madhesh Conservation and Management Master Plan (2017) within the land-use sector. The process included the participation of diverse groups including women, indigenous peoples, Dalits and other marginalized minority groups.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
#### Scale: Medium/high

##### 1.6.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency regarding financial and non-financial aspects
1. The total project cost is estimated at USD 473.34 million to be financed with a US$ 39.3 million contribution from GCF and USD 380 million co-financing.
2. The GCF contribution in the form of a grant can be justified given the fact that Nepal is a least developed country and the project’s major beneficiaries are poor communities in the region most vulnerable to climate change. The activities that will be implemented by the project are not revenue generating.
3. The AE estimates that the project will directly benefit at least 200,681 households or 965,268 individuals. This is a cost per beneficiary of USD 473.34 million divided by 200,681 which considers a competitive cost-per-beneficiary ratio. In addition to the adaptation benefits, the project will also generate co-benefits.
4. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions from the agriculture, forestry and other land use sector in Nepal. Based on the total project costs, the estimated avoided CO2 is USD 413.1 million, which equates to USD 1.03 per tCO2eq for the GCF financial contribution.

##### 1.6.2 Amount of co-financing
5. The EE will provide USD 80.4 million in co-financing for the project, which represents about 17 percent of the total project cost. GCF will therefore achieve a co-financing ratio of 0.2 against its contribution.
6. During and after the implementation, the project expects additional in-kind contributions from farmers and project participants to manage agricultural and forestry interventions.

##### 1.6.3 Programme/project financial viability and other financial indicators
7. As per the AE analysis, the financial viability of the climate-resilient agriculture and forestry models implemented by the project is assessed as “higher” and therefore more profitable than the baseline scenario (i.e., the adoption of the practices currently adopted by the communities).
8. Although the economic return of the project is negative in absolute terms after seven years with a negative internal rate of return (ERR) (i.e., absolute cash flows minus baseline project cash flows) is positive, with an ERR of 97 per cent. The project’s incremental benefits are expected to be significant, including an incremental ERR of 104 per cent after ten years and 115 per cent after twenty years.

The measures in component 1 have all been already piloted in the Chari region within the context of diverse development projects, including those related to climate change adaptation, water management, forestry, agriculture and soil conservation.
* The project creates an enabling environment to support the uptake of proven climate-resilient land use practices. It helps fill information gaps and improve the consolidation of relevant knowledge on climate change adaptation in the downstream upper Terai region while also improving extension services and training and information dissemination.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP recommends the Board approve the proposed project as presented.

* The project strengthens the ability of local communities and ecosystems to adapt to climate change and reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation through an integrated approach that promotes sustainable land use practices in the downstream upper Terai region. The project is congruent with existing government strategies and contributes towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) at national level.
* The project will further promote the generation and dissemination of knowledge that can be adapted at the local level to assist the communities and governments to promote and mainstream climate-resilient SNRM approaches in development plans and strategies.

# GENDER ASSESSMENT

## Vulnerability of communities in Churia

### Background

1. The Churia hills are located between the mid hill areas to the north and the plains in the south of Nepal. In this region, the Churia hills perform critical ecological and socio-cultural functions both upstream and downstream. With more than 50% of its area under forests and riverine areas, the Churia hills render hydrological services such as regulating surface water flows and recharging groundwater resources. The Churia hills are also home to the Terai population, which is one of the most densely populated alluvial plains in the Terai and northern India.
2. Men and women living in the Churia are highly dependent on natural resources to maintain their livelihoods. Forests cover 70% of the Churia hills area, with 74% of the economically active population involved in the sector.1 Forests are an essential resource and safety net for communities. In addition to providing key ecosystem and cultural services, forests provide diverse products such as timber, fuelwood, non-timber forest products (NTFP), and other goods including plants for fodder, subsistence and medicine plants. The Churia hills and Bhairav regions are among the most vulnerable in Nepal due to their high dependence on natural resources, directly dependent on the health of Churia ecosystems to ensure their food, water and energy security.
3. The region is naturally in a more vulnerable situation with friable soils, high exposure to flash flooding, landslides, and other natural disasters (see Table 1). Climate change coupled with unsustainable natural resource management (NRM) in the region increases the vulnerability of communities and ecosystems to landslides, flooding and erosion, extensive forest degradation, and loss of biodiversity. The Churia hills were hit by a major earthquake on 25 April 2015. In Nepal from January to September resulted in the destruction of 43,400 houses, 191,700 partially damaged houses, the displacement of 20,900 families, and the death of at least 86 people.2 Furthermore, the loss of rice production due to floods in 2015 was estimated at over US$5.74 million, which has a major impact on rural livelihoods and food security.3

4. Without significant landscape-level investments in restoring natural capital and promoting climate-resilient land use practices, livelihoods and adaptive capacities, the impacts of climate change will further exacerbate environmental degradation, and thereby aggravate rural poverty, as well as food, water and energy insecurity in the Churia and in downstream regions. For the Churia region in particular, a landscape-level approach to Sustainable Natural Resource Management (SNRM) is crucial for long-term environmental and social stability.

1.1.2 Project description

The overarching objective of the proposed GIZ project is to enhance the climate resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable communities in the Chira region through integrated sustainable rural development and natural resource management approaches, including the following:

- Strengthening capacities of local and provincial government officials on climate change, climate-resilient land use planning, and sustainable natural resource management practices.
- Strengthening the capacities of at least 750 local community-based organizations/user groups* on recognizing climate threats, climate-resilient land use planning and sustainable natural resource management practices, and implementing climate-resilient interventions.

Large-scale adoption and promotion of climate-resilient land and forest management practices on an area of about 112,489 ha, which specifically respond to climate-related threats, including landscape degradation and the vulnerability of critical ecosystems in vulnerable river systems.
- Reducing deforestation through sustainable land use planning, soil and water conservation, and constructing small-scale infrastructure (stone walls, contour bunds, check dams, etc.).
- Reducing deforestation and forest degradation through the implementation of sustainable natural resource management (SNRM) practices and other measures that address the direct and indirect drivers of deforestation.

Improving water recharge systems by reducing surface run-off water through the adoption of water conservation techniques such as contour bunds, check dams, and stone walls.
- Diversifying livelihoods and enhancing incomes of local communities through the strengthening and promotion of climate-resilient value chains.
- Strengthening the capacity of women to participate in SNRM (80% measures).
- Improving information consolidation, dissemination and monitoring to raise awareness on climate change and climate-resilient land use practices, and strengthen the utilization of climate information services.

6. More detailed project information on the specific activities can be found in the BCRIN project feasibility study.

7. Project implementation will occur in 26 of the most vulnerable river systems in Nepal**, with a focus on the Central and Eastern Region (Figure 3). BCRIN project activities will directly benefit 750 local communities, including 148,900 people living in these areas. The project will also indirectly benefit 963,268 people (at least 50% of which are women, of whom 77% are from groups that have experienced inter-generational socio-economic exclusion). Together with reduced deforestation

* Including community forest user groups, household forestry, private forest users and public forest user groups, water user among others.
** Some activities may be implemented on overlapping parcels of land (e.g. measures to reduce deforestation such as controlled grazing, with sustained forest management practices).

A more detailed description of the project activities can be found in the project feasibility study and the project funding proposal.

*Vulnerable people are defined as “groups of people whose disadvantage is situational rather than structural.” Their deprivations are as a result of a particular situation (in some cases, climate change) that has reduced their ability to withstand shocks. Vulnerability can be considered as a state of being exposed to risk. The term vulnerable refers to beneficiaries who are considered vulnerable to climate change (since they live in and highly or moderately vulnerable river systems), those with different socio-economic conditions (such as women, people living in high-risk areas for flooding or landslides, or due to inter-generational discrimination), or those who have experienced intergenerational discrimination. The term “vulnerable” is defined as: “those who have experienced intergenerational discrimination and have been systemically excluded due to economic, social, cultural, political, and/or environmental factors (UNFCCC 2017). This includes groups including women, poor people, Dalits, Aborigines/Indigenous, Muslim, Roma people with UNFCCC 2017).

Over 206,377 ha of critical ecosystems (forests, wetlands and grasslands) will be restored through project implementation. In total, over 3.2 million inhabitants (51% women) of the BRCRN area will indirectly benefit from improved livelihoods, carbon sequestration and strategic planning to support the scaling up of climate-resilient land use practices within the project area.

### 1.1.3 Gender assessment

8. This Gender Assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender-context in Nepal, with a focus on gender considerations for the proposed project activities under the Biodiversity, Research and Community Nutrition (BRCRN). Specifically, this assessment aims to inform project design to ensure that key gender considerations have been effectively maintained throughout the project proposal.

9. The assessment has been conducted based on an extensive review of studies undertaken by the Government of Nepal, donor agencies, social organizations and other stakeholders. Various consultations were held from November 2017 to March 2018 with diverse actors**, including women’s associations and organizations at the national and local level (e.g., Feminist Dalit Organisation [FEDO], National Indigenous Women’s Federation [NIWF], the Himalayan Grassroots

**disabilities, third-gender and people living in remote areas. Socio-excluded groups comprise over 75% of the project population and are disproportionately affected by poverty and environmental degradation.

The aims of the project's Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) contains a detailed list of the consultations that were undertaken with key stakeholders and other relevant actors, including GEF presenters, government officials consulted, along with other pertinent information.


---
**Page 157**
---

### Women’s Natural Resource Management Association (WNRMAN), and the Community-based Forestry Supporters’ Network (COFSUN), among others), provide direct feedback on the current context and barriers, as well as gender-responsive measures and related monitoring and evaluation (M&E) considerations. At the local level, 268 local people living within the project area were consulted in a participatory mapping exercise conducted by the BRCRN team at the Kathmandu on February 21, 2018 to further discuss the preliminary findings from the consultations and gender analysis, and to provide feedback and key insights into the elaboration of a gender action plan. The Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAP) is an integral part of the gender assessment and gender action plan. Detailed meeting summaries and attendance sheets can be found in Annexure 10.

The following sections will focus on the status of gender inequality in Nepal, and the identification of challenges, barriers and opportunities for empowering women and promoting their equality within the project area. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is an integral part of the BRCRN project, which aims to mainstream gender in the BRCRN project, ensuring gender-sensitive measures that overcome key barriers and a gender-responsive monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework, which will monitor the impacts of the project on both men and women, as well as inter-sectoral dimensions of inequality including age, caste, social status and inter-generational exclusion, among other factors.

#### 1.2 Gender Inequality in Nepal

The population of Nepal in 2016 was just under 30 million people, of which 52% are women. While Nepal has made substantial progress in enhancing gender equality in the last three decades, gender discrimination and inequality continue to be a major challenge for women in Nepal. The empowerment of women remains a challenge as well, where there are differences in equality based on age, position within the family, marital status, religion, caste and ethnicity, among other factors.

The following sub-sections will provide an overview of the main challenges for achieving gender equality in Nepal, focusing on where possible on the dynamics and trends experienced in the BRCRN project area.


#### Poverty & Income

Nepal is one of the world’s least developed countries (LDC), with a per capita income of $761. In 2014, Nepal achieved a score of 0.116 according to the multidimensional poverty index (MPI) [1], with an estimated 26.9% of the country's population affected by multidimensional poverty, equivalent to around 7.5 million people.[2] Focusing on income-related poverty, approximately 13% of the population is considered poor and 40% are considered vulnerable.

The country has experienced rapid development in the last two decades and the government has made substantial progress in working toward its goal to graduate from LDC to developing countries status by 2022. However, despite this progress on poverty levels, and as a result of the implementation of measures to meet the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), in addition


[1] World Bank 2016
[2] Ibid.
[3] MPI considers three core dimensions as a measure of poverty: (i) Health (nutrition, child health); (ii) Education (levels of schooling, years attended and level of learning e.g. reading, writing, math, water, electricity, food; and (iii) Additional information can be found at: http://child.unmpi.org/en/content/multidimensional-poverty-index-mpi
[4] World Bank 2015

---
**Page 158**
---

national targets. Nepal has made large strides in addressing poverty at the national level: the country’s Human Development Index (HDI)15 value has improved from 0.476 to 0.538 from 2003 to 2015.16

15 While Nepal has made substantial efforts to advance gender equality and promote women’s empowerment, it still faces significant challenges in this area. The country’s HDI values (0.538) continue to lag behind men’s (0.582). Another challenge remains to address poverty and inequality within ethnic minority and other excluded groups.17 In 2015, indigenous people were the most vulnerable group with an HDI of 0.469, followed by Dalits with lower HDI values in comparison to other high-caste groups such as Brahmins (0.538) or Chhetri (0.538). While the general trend of inequality is decreasing in the country, it remains a challenge to be addressed.

16 The Churia Terai-Madhesh region faces unique poverty-related obstacles. HDI values for groups in the Terai-Madhesh region (0.454) are lower than mountain and hill groups (0.513). Many poor households in this region depend on forest resources, which provide a critical source of income and sources for meeting their subsistence requirements, and some work for the rich timber traders who might exploit them. The Churia Terai-Madhesh region is also one of the most drought-prone areas. Thus, forests serve as a safety net for many communities in the Churia-Terai-Madhesh region.

17 Female-headed households make up around 20% of households in the project area, lower than the national average of 28%. An analysis of a 1991 Agriculture Development Survey showed that female-headed households were more likely to be poor and less likely to meet subsistence needs. The poverty rate for female-headed households was 50.7 percent to 47.2 percent, respectively. However, there is a need to disaggregate female-headed households into those headed by widowed or divorced women versus those headed by single women in employment, as which the sending of remittances prevents the household from entering poverty, versus households that are female-headed due to widowhood, divorce or unmarried. Considering the above-mentioned factors, it is difficult to determine whether female-headed households are more likely to be poor than male-headed households. The proportion of female-headed households in the project area was lower in all regions; e.g., in 1991 more than 13% of households were female-headed but only 6% of families headed by women lived below the poverty line.

The analysis also shows that female-headed households often have lower literacy and educational rates, high dependency ratios and greater lack of access to information and media. Thus, barriers

### Gender Inequality Index (GII)

18.Nepal's Gender Inequality Index (GII) for 2015 was 0.497, with an overall ranking of 115 out of 159 countries ranked."While still lagging internationally, gender empowerment has improved substantially from 2001 to 2011 when considering the improvement in the Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM) and the decrease in the GII. This is due to the implementation of policy frameworks related to gender and social inclusion."

#### Education

19.Gender gaps in the education sector continue to persist, where girls and women tend to have lower levels of education, especially in rural areas. The percentage of women participating in primary education has increased substantially in the last two decades, where the number of women completing 12 grades rose from 21% to 42% (Figure 4). Nonetheless, equality can still be promoted in terms of access to education, especially in rural areas where studies have found that girls' education often remains a lower priority.

Health

As with other sectors covered, major improvements in improving gender equality have been observed in Nepal's health sector, where it has been noted that significant improvements have


---
**Page 162**
---

been made in improving maternal and children’s health in Nepal in the last two decades. During the period from 1990 to 2013, the country's maternal mortality rate declined from 538 to 170, women’s life expectancy rose from 55.5 to 70 years, and the total fertility rate also declined from 4.6 to 2.6.*


In Nepal, rural areas continue to lag behind the progress made in urban areas. Rural communities in Nepal often have limited access to health services, largely due to poor infrastructure, limited access to qualified professionals and other socio-economic and cultural barriers. Women living in these areas are more likely to experience discrimination based on caste-based discrimination, poor education of girls, among others.*Women from poorer households and marginalized minority groups, especially Dalits and Muslims, often experience additional discrimination.


In the BCRN project area, many communities have limited access to clean water, especially in dry seasons, when water scarcity becomes a major challenge for many.*As a result, many communities rely on unsafe water sources such as ponds, rivers, and wells. The most common problems identified by focus groups included gowns, bone and joint issues associated with heavy lifting of water jugs, and the lack of clean water for cooking and washing.*The scarcity of safe water resources, increasing water and natural resource scarcity could exacerbate these health impacts through reducing the abundance of safe water resources and by increasing collection time (e.g. walking to a distant water source). Women in rural areas who are responsible for collecting water may experience additional challenges. Cases of discrimination have been reported at village wells, where Dalits were forced to wait until all other castes had filled their water collection vessels first.*


The household level is the most important level of decision making in Nepal and in many regions respectively, have major impacts on human health. Women are especially exposed to negative impacts as they are responsible for cooking for their family and inhale the harmful fumes.


At the household level, women are often in charge of domestic tasks, and in general have less power over decisions regarding these activities.*There are various factors in control over decision making exist related to regional location, education, employment, region, socio-cultural background and ethnicity, among other factors. There is a great degree of variation in the involvement of women in decisions regarding economic activities such as agriculture, livestock production, and income generation.*For example, within Gurung communities, a study found that 43% of households both men and women were involved in decision making about agricultural activities.*In contrast, within Tharu communities this percentage was higher at 60 percent.* In general, women’s autonomy in household decision making is still disadvantaged due to lack of awareness of opportunities and rights. Nevertheless, when it comes to family well-being related activities such as health and education, women are generally found to be more actively involved within many ethnic groups.

29.Since 2001 women have experienced increased access to economic resources, property and micro-credit and the percentage of households in which women own land has considerably increased.46 Women access and control over economic resources plays a critical role in their access to education and health and provide the foundation for empowerment and equality. However, although the right to property is enshrined in law, there are still significant barriers to women’s full enjoyment regarding property, several barriers still remain. Barriers of limited access to credit and support services, curtailed mobility due to threat of violence, perhaps both in the private and public sphere, and the lack of legal recognition of women’s rights to land ownership continue to restrict women's roles in decision making processes. Regional differences have been found with women in urban areas having more freedom of mobility and access to household financial resources than women in the Terai.47

30.While policies have become increasingly advanced in mandating the participation of women in political representation, there are still significant barriers to women’s full enjoyment regarding their participation. The 2015 constitution states that 33% of parliamentary seats in government must be filled by women (Article 86, B), and similar provisions are in place to ensure women's participation in village level governance bodies. However, these provisions have not been fully implemented.

31.At the village level, while Water User Associations require least 33% participation by women and CFUGs 50%, it has been highlighted that often women’s full participation is limited due to the personal responsibilities they bear at home and in the community. Women are also subject to the domination of elite men in decision making spheres.48 Other concerns raised in the consultations included when women are able to participate they are often from elite groups, and that marginalized women face more barriers than other women in accessing their rights as a water user group.

32.Time poverty in the Chure Valley is a major barrier to the participation of women in decision making, as many women spend between 10.5 to 14 hours a day on maintaining their livelihoods. The Churea experiences high migration of men for work, leading to exacerbated time-poverty, as women need to compensate for the absence of men in their households by taking on their responsibilities and working longer hours than before. This is further compounded when husbands often have greater autonomy and decision-making authority at the household level.49

33.Closely linked to time-poverty is the fact that women in the Churea has less access to quality education, which limits their ability to participate effectively in community development activities in such fora, and to make decisions on sustainable natural resource management. A study by Luma et al (2015) noted that lack of education, low self-esteem and lack of capacities were major barriers for women’s effective and full participation in forest user groups’ executive committees. It should be noted that these barriers are not only experienced by women, but also impact the participation of indigenous groups and ethnic minorities in the BRCRN project area. 34.Thus, while it has been an important first step to provide women with the legal basis to become engaged in decision making structures and committees within community-based organizations (e.g. establishing a quota for women on village development committees or other elected bodies, executive committees within community forestry user groups), additional policies, incentives and measures are required to empower women, including women from marginalized communities, and ensure that they have the skills and resources needed to participate effectively in decision making. ### Resource Use, Income & Labor Force

35.Access to and control over resources, especially in the forestry and agriculture sectors, remains a major obstacle for women’s empowerment and gender equality in Nepal.[11] This challenge is further exacerbated when looking at women from different castes/ethnicities, single women, widowed women or female-headed households.

36.Women often do not hold land titles, due to customary and statutory laws that have limited land ownership in the past. This has created major obstacles for women to access credit, technical support and other resources needed to increase their productivity.[12] Women own only 14 percent of the total agricultural landholdings in Nepal, representing a 9.7% increase since the last decade[13]. The disadvantaged position of FFRs can be seen by the fact that landholdings of FFR are considerably smaller than those of male-headed households. While more than 13% of households were female-headed but only 5% of farms were owned by women.[14]

37.Women are highly engaged in the agricultural sector, including in crop production and raising livestock. Women’s share in total agricultural labor force is estimated to be around 60%, compared with only 28% for men[15]. An ongoing trend towards the ‘feminization’ of agriculture can be observed in rural areas in Nepal, due to men obtaining off-farm work or migrating to urban areas or foreign countries. In many cases, women are left behind to perform agricultural activities that were formerly performed by men, such as ploughing or other activities associated with more strenuous physical labor, in addition to their normal activities tending to crops and livestock.[16] This trend is also observed in the forestry sector, where the number of female-headed households has more than doubled in the last decade[17].

38.Livestock is kept as an asset as well as for manure and draft usage. In rural areas, by generating bio- gas from animal waste, women are able to reduce their time spent on household chores.[18] With regards to livestock, women contribute nearly 70% of the work for livestock feeding, especially related to forage collection, cleaning stalls, and feeding animals[19]. Practices are often intense, and women spend substantial time cutting grass, collecting fodder and taking animals to

Men are more often responsible for milking animals, and commercialization aspects (e.g., selling milk and other livestock products).12) Men are more often responsible for larger animals (cows, buffaloes), whereas women have more responsibilities over smaller ruminants and animals.13) While women are considered "resource poor in terms of raising and feeding livestock," they are increasingly involved in the care and management of livestock. Women have increased women's participation in agricultural development programs, and livestock rearing. Time-saving activities, including integration of fodder with crops, agroforestry systems, and improved breeds, can increase productivity and income for both men and women, as well as improve their skills and capacities.

39.A recent study by World Bank and FAO (2018) found that women in Nepal have "high control over agricultural land," including women from households with men who have migrated for work elsewhere as well as women in households where the men have not migrated. They found that women are more likely to be involved in the management of non-agricultural income, however they are also more likely to engage in off-farm work as well as having less control over land and time less time-poor.

40.The "intensity" of agriculture such as has led to numerous environmental, social and economic challenges. Women in rural areas are often "time poor," with women working over 10 hours per week on average than men when taking unpaid labor into account. However, in agricultural communities, women spend more time on farm work (on average 5-6 hours a day) compared to men's 4 hours a day on average." Labor shortages are a major challenge for the agriculture sector, and have led women to focus on high-value and less labor intensive crops. Increasing land abandonment has also been observed in Nepal, with women being more likely than men to abandon their plots resulting challenges such as time-poverty. Such trends have led to a decline in certain agricultural activities that has contributed to food insecurity in some communities. Social impacts include increased domestic violence and child labor. Women are also more vulnerable to accidents and injuries due to hard labor and exhaustion due to their household responsibilities (paid and unpaid labor).14)

There is limited access to credit, inputs, and extension services for women farmers. Extension services in Nepal are weak, and women do not have sufficient information about and access to labor-saving practices technologies (see Feasibility Study for more detailed information on extension services). Women's groups such as women's self-help groups (WSHGs) can help support them through collective action, investments and ultimately help overcome some of the barriers faced by women (e.g. access to knowledge, credit, inputs/tools, among others). CBSs often dedicate a portion of their resources to provide training and technical assistance for women in programs that are dedicated to support the most vulnerable households within CBSs. World Bank and FAO (2018) found that over 56% of women from migrant households (i.e., households with at least one male member who has migrated) are engaged in some form of agricultural, livestock, social or religious groups, compared to 47% of women from non-migrant households.

41.Rural communities are also highly dependent on local forest resources to sustain their livelihoods. Men and women both collect firewood, fruits, honey, and other products for subsistence and forest gardening; however, this means that they do not necessarily visit the forest on a daily basis. 42. Forest user groups (FUGs) improve women’s participation in forest-related decision-making, and have emerged as a key mechanism for enhancing the socio-economic and political status of women and disadvantaged groups in rural Nepal*. Majority of research focuses on Community-forestry User Groups (CFUGs), which are formed by the government to provide access to forests. Women engage women through their institutional structures (described below) within the institutional policy chapter. The CFUGs have been found to be more inclusive than other FUGs, and they promote inclusive benefit sharing. Women-led CFUGs have also been promoted (i.e., CFUGs with all women executive committees), and in 2012 there were 1,035 women-led CFUGs*. Research has found that these groups are more likely to be financially sustainable than men-led groups due to better financial accountability. Furthermore, women-led CFUGs also create an empowering environment for women, which increases the participation and engagement of women within CFUGs. Women’s participation in decision-making is enhanced by the presence of women executive committees and positive discrimination policies. Nonetheless, CBSs still can improve terms in gender-equality and -solidarity, as well as social inclusion and benefit sharing and several organizations have developed best practices and recommendations to continue to promote GEIS within these institutions (see Chapter 15 and 2 below).


Gender Violence


43. Violence against women and girls has been identified as one of the main barriers that hampers female empowerment and substantive equality*. Approximately 20% of rural women reported having been subjected to physical violence, in comparison to 19% of women living in urban areas*.


The 'Domestic Violence and Punishment Law' was enacted in 2005. However, the actual socialization and enforcement of the law is weak*, where violent incidents are often not reported or investigated*.


44. Such trends are also evident in the BCRN project area, where interviews with women groups confirmed that violence against gender violence in the community's lives is linked to lack of access to health care, limited economic rights, lack of empowerment and gender and caste-based discrimination and all can contribute to the prevalence of violence against women in rural areas, among other factors.


45. In 2017 the Government of Nepal set up a hotline for reporting gender based violence, and established a network of service providers (including local NGOs and CSOs) to support gender-based violence victims and prevention. This has improved reporting of GBV, and has improved


---

information dissemination on legal protections, and support services. Within the project’s districts, there are a number of NGOs working in awareness raising, advocacy and support services.”14 Serious working on gender in Nepal have noted the importance of engaging both men and women in gender sensitization and social inclusion, including promoting male and female role models, leaders and trainers within projects and communities.15

### 1.3 Legal and administrative framework protecting women and promoting gender equality

46.In Nepal, women’s participation in various stages of decision making processes was not fundamentally accepted until the 20th century. However, the government of Nepal initiated the concept of women in development in national policy since the 1980s by the 6th Five Year Plan, which adopted the policy of enhancing the efficiency of women and recognizing the role of women in development.

47.The Local Self-Governance Act, 1999 is the first act which made the provision for representation of women at the local level. The Women’s Empowerment Policy, 2065 B.S., adopted in 2008 was the first step in this direction and women’s representation was formally recognized by the government. Currently, this number stands at 25% at ward levels, and at least one position (mayor and deputy-mayor) must be filled by a woman.


---
**Page 168**
---

48.The Constitution of Nepal 2015 also makes special legal provision to women in national, state and local legislatures, which ensures 33% women's representation in national and state legislatures. The irrigation policy (2001) makes the legal provision of 33% representation of women in Water User Associations (WUA). Community Forest Guidelines (2015) made mandatory proportion of 50% representation of women in community forest management committees. In addition, the constitution requires that a position (president or secretary) should be filled by a woman. While setting such targets has greatly increased the participation of women in meetings and groups, women's participation in decision-making remains low due to lack of access to land, resources, education, financial services, education and skill training, and time-poverty, among other factors.49.The Legal Code (the Mulak Act) 1863 is the main document regarding the portion of ancestral property that belongs to women. The law was amended in 2005 and again in 2017. In that year, the government passed a bill making amendments to the legal code, giving equal inheritance rights to daughters and sons. However, this amendment has not been implemented yet. This is another major advancement for women's rights on parental property, it is still not practiced in reality.50.The issue of gender equality has been greatly strengthened within Nepalese policy since the adoption of the Constitution 2015. The new constitution and its various amendments to the constitution from 2007 made several gender-friendly provisions and socially inclusive laws, including guaranteeing the rights of Nepalese women to freedom, equality and social justice. It further guarantees the right of all women to equal opportunities in education, employment, and women, amongst other major milestones that strengthened human rights in the country. In sum, there are three dimensions of social inclusion in government policies and plans: i) access to resources and benefits; ii) participation in decision-making processes at all levels of policy making and planing, implementation, monitoring and evaluation based on policies, laws, values and structures.


51.Since 2008, policies across sectors have increasingly included provisions designed to empower women and promote gender equality through improving access to sector resources and engagement in official fora at the national, provincial and local levels. For instance, in 2008 the Ministry of Forests and Soil Conservation (MoFSC) launched the Gender and Social Economic Inclusion (GESI) strategy. The MoFSC GESI strategy aims at: a) assist government, non-government, donor agencies and other stakeholders to mainstream gender equality and social inclusion within their organizations, projects and programs. Furthermore, the strategy aims to guide all organizations working in the forestry sector to be responsive and inclusive through identifying and addressing gender inequalities and promoting gender equality and social inclusion strategies; ii) good governance and inclusive institutional development; iii) GESI-sensitive budgeting, programming and monitoring; and iv) equitable access to resources and benefits. MoFSC has also been implementing a number of initiatives in the forestry sector for the implementation of gender-sensitive and -responsive policy at the national, regional and local level. Nonetheless, the implementation of GESI in community forestry at the local level in practice is still challenging due to lack of gender-sensitive policy and its implementation in the field due to institutional and socio-cultural barriers.


52.At the international level, Nepal is a signatory to various conventions that aim to support the empowerment of women and gender equality in general. Nepal is a signatory member of the

# Gender issues in building a resilient Churia region in Nepal

## Link between Climate Change and Unustainable Land Use and Gender in the project area

### 1.4 M4.6M and women living in the BCRN project area are highly dependent on natural resources to maintain their livelihoods. Agriculture is the main form of employment in the area, for both commercial and subsistence purposes. The forest provides a variety of products that are used as fuel for their houses and for energy consumption; firewood remains the main source of energy in the Churia region and also used during the Tera festival. Forests also provide local people with NTFPs including plants, fruits, mushrooms, honey, and other medicinal plants which are used to meet their daily cultural services. Forests in the Terai have been largely cleared and, thus many of the people living in the Terai source collect and NTFPs from forests in the Churia hills and Bhuvan region.

### 55% Women in the area are involved in forest resource collection and management. The combination and deforestation in the Churia hills and Bhuvan region, including: illegal harvesting, landlides and uncontrolled livestock grazing, among others. These drivers have had differing impacts on men and women. Women are more vulnerable to these changes because of their limited access to resources due to the nature of their responsibilities, including both paid and unpaid labor. For example, forest quality has deteriorated in the Churia hills region over time. The women who were interviewed in the project area reported that walking distances to collect firewood and fodder have increased, along with decline of fodder, grasses and firewood. For instance, women in the Bahakra River watershed reported that it takes them 20 minutes to collect firewood from a distance of about 1 km. In the last 10 years, but the same task takes them over than 3 hours. In the Mahuli community of Saptari, women reported that it takes about 4 hours to collect a load of ground grasses and fodder from a distance of less than 500 meters. The same task took less than 2 hours in the past. The collection of NTFPs has also been limited due to over-harvesting, degradation and changes in climatic


---
**Page 170**
---

conditions, ultimately leading to declining incomes. The degradation of agricultural soils also leads to productivity losses, which can jeopardize food security and family nutrition.

56.Deforestation and land degradation can increase the risk of natural disasters, erosion and other negative environmental impacts, which can create further challenges for local men and women to sustain livelihoods and meet their basic needs. Climate change is expected to result in increasing temperatures during the dry season and precipitation in the monsoon season, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, landslides and flooding, among other trends. Climate change is expected to result in increasing temperatures during the dry season and precipitation in the monsoon season, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, landslides and flooding, among other trends. Climate change further exacerbates the impacts and exposure to climate change (Figure 9).

![Figure 9: Overall illustration of the vulnerability of the project region with multiple climate and human induced disasters in view of climate change causing cascading impacts](image_url)

57.Climate change further poses a major risk to the livelihoods of women and men living in the BRCRN project area. Climate change will exacerbate existing environmental, social and economic patterns, and the driving up of seasonal springs, among other changes. Unsustainable natural resource management (NRM) will exacerbate climate risks and impacts, having major impacts on the environment and human well-being. Women and men living in the BRCRN project area do not have DRR plans, and thus are often ill-prepared and vulnerable to natural disasters. Often those living in the project area are women and men who live in rural areas, with high levels of flooding and landslides, are poor and marginalized communities, with low adaptive capacities and a high dependence on natural resources to maintain their livelihoods. Gender-based violence (domestic violence and sexual violence) often increases following natural disasters, especially affecting displaced and highly vulnerable women.


*UNDP 2013; International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies 2015: Gender Assessment and Action Plan BRCRN


---
**Page 171**
---

58. Ground water recharge has greatly declined in many communities in the BRCRN project area, where many communities have noticed the depletion of such resources. Such a trend has major impacts on the water, food security and income of many communities. Women are more vulnerable to such trends, as women are responsible for the collection of water. While seasonal changes in water availability are normal, the decline is happening at an alarming rate. The lack of access to clean water is making women and girls travel longer distances for its collection. For girls, this means there is a higher chance they will need to miss school to collect water, whereas for men, it leads to increased workload on their part. Women in rural areas spend 2-3 hours per day collecting water versus less than an hour a day.10 Women in migrant households (where household members have migrated elsewhere for work) are also vulnerable as they may not be able to access clean water at all times.

59. While women and men living in the BRCRN project area are vulnerable to the impacts of unsustainable natural resource use and climate change, women, especially women from marginalized or minority communities, are even more vulnerable. Women comprise 10% of the 3.2 million inhabitants of the BCRN project area, of which 77% are from marginalized/excluded groups.10

60. Four core barriers were identified that women face in adopting sustainable land use practices (Table 1):

• Gender gap in access to resources and land
• Gap in mainstreaming gender into climate change and land use planning processes and policies, and the resulting decision-making related to GESI,
• Gender and social inclusion gaps in education, skills and leadership,
• Gender gap in disproportionate roles and responsibilities in households and communities.

Gender gap in access to resources and land

61. A major gap was identified by women working in the field of women's rights and other women in the Chiria area that was the patriarchal values system as seen in rooted in a society. This is perceived as the main obstacle for women to their empowerment and restricts their access to resources and land. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes related to natural resource management, including water use.10 The lack of education and skills among women in SRM/R, climate change and in decision making processes. Some women in the community have limited knowledge on how to manage their land and other resources effectively, which limits alternative livelihood opportunities, which greatly limited the participation of women in such trainings. One participant noted that many believe that "training is not for women, but for men" and that women are not encouraged to participate in training sessions.10 Therefore, gender-sensitization is needed for both men and women. Extension services also need to be made more “gender-friendly”, including mainstreaming GESI into training modules, training extension staff on gender issues, and ensuring that the extension service is not dominated by men but largely dominated by men, with limited decision making and engagement of women. CBs are increasingly developing policies and initiatives to strengthen the engagement of women; however it is an ongoing process that it not without its challenges.

# Problem Statement: Women in the Eastern Churia and Terai region are vulnerable to natural disasters and climate change, and face food, water and energy insecurity which is further exacerbated through unsustainable natural resource use in the region.
