62 Many people residing in the Churia region do not have secure land tenure and only ~25% of women have land entitlements in the Churia region. They have been residing and cultivating alien land (non-registered) for decades. Few women in the Eastern Churia and Terai regions have access to land, especially those who are widowed or divorced. Women received land as an inheritance or gift (dājīgo) from their parents, especially in households without sons, or through the purchase of land in the name of wives by absentee husbands who are working abroad.

63.Lack of secure access to land and tenure security and the limited control over land use decisions and resources limit women’s ability to adapt to climate change, implement sustainable agricultural practices, and improve their livelihoods. Women noted that without statutory property rights they cannot access loans to support their farming activities or other income-generating activities. They also lack use practices (e.g., improved plant varieties, animal breeds, etc.). Tenure insecurity prevents women from making medium- to long-term investments in more sustainable and improved practices. Secure land tenure is a basic human right provided to both men and women to support women’s empowerment and gender equality.

64.Despite these challenges, some programs have focused on increasing incomes that empower women (e.g., microfinance, livestock, and indigenous products), provide trainings, and support women’s empowerment in decision making processes and in the formal access of resources and land. In Nepal, the government initiated tax exemption in land registration for women. The cost of land registration for women has reduced from 25-50% in the cost of land registration for women. Daughters and granddaughters now receive 50% tax discounts in land registration, which is 35% for a single woman (especially without any male family member).

65.CBOs have emerged as a key institution for promote collective action; enabling improved information sharing, promoting time saving activities and pooling resources. It increased access to credit, training, technology, and access to finance, among other benefits. On their own, many households, especially women-headed households, are not able to access these services or are not technically ready to adopt climate-resilient SRM due to the lack of capacities, awareness and finance. CBOs thus can help them to pool their resources and overcome many of these barriers. In Nepal, the government has been working with CBOs in developing GESI policies, e.g., including mandatory minimum participation quotas of women, and promoting proportional representation of marginalized groups and equitable benefit

* During consultations with local communities in Khusbandi & Dandaha of the Kankai River Watershed, Kaski district, many households have been cultivating land for decades. However, they did not have an official land certificate, although they have been living there for more than three decades.
* CBOs are a key institution in Nepal.

* Field consultations in the project area (conducted from December 21-28, 2017).

* *CBO = Community Based Organization*

* *UNSD 2016; MoF 2016.*

* *Community-based organizations often discussed the positive environmental, social and economic benefits from joint investments, including the alleviation of time-poverty. For example, several communities discussed that community water harvesting structures (e.g., check dams) have helped to reduce their time spent on collecting water for domestic use, NTFPs, etc.), and increasing participation of girls in school (before they often had to walk two hours in the dry season to collect water).


---
**Page 175**
---

sharing. CFUGs also increasingly involve and support landless and poor households, giving such groups an opportunity to benefit from the sustainable management of community-managed natural resources.125 CFUGs dedicate at least 35% of their funds for pro-poor programs.126 The increasing involvement of women in CFUGs is a positive development, as such local community-based user groups, especially CFUGs, represent an important entry-point for promoting gender equitable and inclusive climate-resilient SNRM.

68. The government has also been working on mainstreaming gender into natural resources through investments in “Public Land Forest User Groups”, which will be primarily composed of marginalized households. Management plans for all CBOs will be developed in a participatory manner with such user groups, combined with technical support.

**Gaps in mainstreaming gender in climate change policy and land use planning practices**

67.While Nepal’s legal framework related to gender has been strengthened in recent years, there are gaps in the role of policies relating to gender, and implementation and enforcement of policies tend to lag behind, especially in rural areas.128 Policies also may not be fully implemented at the local level. For instance, in CFUGs there is a legal requirement that at least 35% of funds should be allocated for pro-poor programs, but this is often not decision making committee (i.e. either president or secretary). Many people consulted in rural communities highlighted the challenges of operationalizing such policies, and noted that many women are excluded from decision-making processes.

68.Gender-mainstreaming in climate policies remains limited in the country.129 Climate change has been recognized as a priority issue by the government, but there is no specific policy on climate change at national level. The BRCRN project region. Nonetheless, many policies do not take into account differing dynamics and the impacts on men and women, and many are not designed in a gender-sensitive manner.130 Women’s voices have been largely excluded from discussions of topics that are critical for their livelihoods. This goes beyond simple policies, and also manifests in land use planning at the local level, where women are largely excluded from planning processes and decision-making on how to manage natural resources and agricultural resources, with in-depth knowledge about their utilization and status, however they have yet to be fully engaged in decision making and planning processes. In many groups, women’s voices need to be mainstreamed into land use change and DRR policies, as well as in land use planning processes at the local level, to ensure that a gender-sensitive and -responsive approach is adopted with the potential to address the specific


---
**Page 176**
---

vulnerabilities and necessities of women and men, taking into account differing dynamics and impacts.

69.Nepal is currently undergoing a political transition from a constitutional monarchy to a federal democratic republic, involving major restructuring of local level institutions – including the establishment of seven provinces and the devolution of power over natural resource management to the local level. At the local level, there are the mayor and deputy-mayor in municipalities, and the chairman and vice-chairman in rural municipalities. The head of a village development committee (VDC) is reserved for Dalits- and/others from each ward, who are each elected for five years.

70.The Constitution of Nepal (2015) and Local Government Operational Act (LGOA) (2015) state that the local level institutions have the authority to make decisions on matters related to their jurisdiction. They must operate the given responsibilities and to regulate its procedure. However, while these rules shall be under the domains of municipalities, they must be consistent with provincial and national laws.

71.Women’s participation in formal decision making bodies, but also trainings, schools and other activities is often limited or absent due to numerous reasons discussed in more detail within previous sections (e.g.time poverty, socio-cultural barriers, among others). The fact that women are disproportionately affected by climate change and DRR means that a major segment of the population, which is particularly vulnerable to climate risks, cannot access knowledge and learning opportunities. This ultimately limits their ability to participate effectively in decision-making processes on risk reduction practices. Further gaps are noticeable among women of different groups. Women from ethnic minorities, widows and other disadvantaged women experience greater inequalities than others when it comes to accessing education, training and other platforms. In some communities in the Terai it was noted that some community members, especially women and marginalized minority groups, may have limited knowledge of the Nepal Climate Change Policy.

72.Many communities consulted noted that while people may understand that the climate is changing, people are largely unaware of risk reduction measures. However, some communities were aware of the need to adapt their livelihoods in response to the changing climate. For example, the Lepach people in the Mechi River watershed noted

### Gender Assessment and Action Plan BCRN 24


---
**Page 177**
---

that they have begun to grow akhore khursani (a local variety of chili), and oranges at higher altitudes, which has only become possible due to changing climatic patterns. Such experiences with local knowledge and the piloting of local solutions are key to strengthening adaptive capacity in communities.

However there is currently no effective knowledge hub or network which allows communities to share local knowledge, nor access information about climate change, DRR, or DRM within their own region. The lack of a knowledge hub is a major gap in disseminating lessons learned, best practices and other key experiences to communities.


73.Measures are needed to encourage the full engagement of women in knowledge platforms especially at the local and provincial levels, and strengthen the capacities of women on SMMs, DRR and DRM. Women’s participation should be prioritized, meaning that many women may not have the capacity to take on formal leadership roles or to participate in technical trainings. Extension services need to be made more gender-sensitive and tailored towards women farmers. The design of training modules for DR/DRR need to ensure that they are targeted for women given their educational backgrounds and typical land use responsibilities and activities, and that they also further promote skill building and the development of new skills. Training materials should be designed in an inclusive manner, ensuring the engagement of women from different groups (e.g., age, education level). The selection of extension workers, resource persons and trainers include women and people who are familiar with the cultural and local language, to facilitate information dissemination and ensure the full engagement of women in knowledge platforms. Women’s access to training needs to be improved as women will often not travel long distances to attend a training. Gender-sensitization of men and women also should be further strengthened, noting the benefits associated with training women.


Gender gap in terms of disproportionate roles and responsibilities in households and communities


74.Women in the Churia and Terai region are often faced with time poverty, where women are working up to 13 hours a day, taking care of their household and livelihoods, considering both agricultural and non-agricultural activities. Women are more involved in crop production than men, especially in the cultivation of rice, maize, millet, pulses, grasses and water. Women are more responsible for the maintenance of the household (cleaning, cooking, caring for the elderly, among other tasks), in addition to supporting the aforementioned agricultural activities. In urban areas, women’s time poverty is less critical as patterns of men tend to urban or foreign countries; women’s time poverty is becoming more critical, as women are taking on additional responsibilities while not being fully empowered to make decisions.


75.Thus, in addition to the other barriers previously discussed, time poverty, compounded by lack of confidence and lower levels of literacy, is a major barrier to engage women in trainings

on SNRM or climate change, as many women may not have the time to participate in trainings, user group meetings, etc. Women have little awareness of time-saving land use practices, and limited access to time-saving technologies.

### 1.5 Gender Assessment Recommendations

The gender assessment has provided insight into gender challenges in Nepal, zooming in on the specific context of the BRCRN project area, beneficiaries and interventions. The assessment was conducted through desk reviews, focus group discussions (FGDs) with women as well as with stakeholder consultations on gender in Kathmandu as well as in the project area. The findings of this analysis have been described in the sections above, and have helped identify key gender challenges that need to be addressed within the context of the BRCRN project.

Summary of Recommendations from Stakeholder Consultations

Stakeholder consultations during project development were designed to engage diverse actors, where gender was viewed as a cross-cutting topic. A wide range of stakeholders, including organizations representing women and men, were engaged early on to provide insight into the gender-specific challenges faced in the BRCRN project area as well as feedback on how these challenges could be addressed through the promotion of gender equality. Meetings have been conducted both at the national level with key stakeholders in the capital (including FEDO, NWI, HIMAWANTI, Nepal Federation of Indigenous Nationalities (NFIND), and others) and at the local level within the project area (see ESFM Appendix 3 for meeting summaries and attendance sheets). Key issues highlighted from the consultations include:

- Gender inequalities are a major barrier to climate change and land use planning at the local level, where women's knowledge needs to be taken into consideration and their voices considered in decision making and land use planning processes.
- Many women, especially Dalit women, don't have access to formal property rights for land rights, despite their key role in managing the agricultural land, primarily due to

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan BRCRN

## Recommendations for Project Design and Implementation

The BRCRN project aims to enhance the climate resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable men and women in the Churia region through integrated sustainable rural development and natural resource management interventions. It is recommended that the project design takes into account the following recommendations to help overcome existing gender gaps, while promoting gender-sensitive and equitable development:

- **Access and quality of forest and water resources**: These are negatively impacted by climate change and unsustainable NRM.
- **Gender-sensitive trainings** are not only needed for women but also for men to encourage their participation in leadership roles.
- **Engagement of boys and girls in schools on topics related to climate change and SNIM, including gender-related topics**, represents an important platform to engage the youth.
- **There is a need for targeted support to help women develop skills related to livestock production and marketing**. This will help them strengthen their income and livelihoods.

### Gender-Specific Workshops

A gender-specific workshop in Kathmandu was conducted on February 21, 2018, to validate the gender assessment and receive early feedback on the proposed gender action plan (GAP). The workshop also provided an opportunity for stakeholders to review the final version of this assessment and the GAP. In general, the GAP was well received; however, the importance of providing targeted measures for women within the project design was highlighted as a key recommendation that should be taken into account.

The Gender Action Plan was presented at the BRCRN Validation Workshop on March 25, 2018, where the project was validated by diverse stakeholders and partners from the Churia region.


---
**Page 180**
---

### E1. Promote membership in CBOs and empower women to actively participate and take on leadership roles, and strengthen access to control over resources

- **Work with community-based organizations (CBOs) as a main benefit for the project**, as they are key actors in promoting gender equality, social empowerment, and sustainable natural resource management (see Feasibility Study for additional information).
- **Disseminate information on benefits for women associated with CBOs**, including:
  - A more comprehensive list of the social, environmental, and economic benefits is provided in the Feasibility Study.
    * Improve environmental conditions to reduce exposure to extreme weather events and hazards, improve agricultural productivity (reduced land degradation), and improve food security. These improvements can also contribute to increased resilience against climate change, especially for rural vulnerable and excluded women.129
    * Strengthened resilience to climate change can also reduce the vulnerability to gender-based violence, which often increases with natural hazards (taking advantage of hazard-affected women).130
    * Enhance opportunities for social empowerment and political engagement through CBO management structure (targets for membership and participation in plenary meetings, and mandatory!).
  - Allocate time poverty by supporting an improved natural resource base (e.g., tree upland restoration), fodder production, and other income-generating activities while promoting collective action and pooling of resources.131 CBOs can also help women access gender-sensitive technical assistance, knowledge and credit.
    * Improve access to technical assistance, know-how/credit, market information and knowledge
  - Support measures to increase incomes of the rural poor, women and Dalits132.
- **Provide financial and non-financial support for vulnerable households through CBOs** (e.g., BPL cards).
- **Advocate for an agenda that promotes gender equality and is socially inclusive**, taking into account differentiated vulnerabilities among women from diverse social, economic and cultural backgrounds. Women should make up at least 50% of project participants.

* Strengthen access to and control over resources through the establishment of public land use plans, including training on engaging and empowering marginalized households, including Dalit women and landless women, and implement trainings to build skills along key value chains such as forest resource management, agroforestry, livestock production practices, trainings on climate change, SNRM and DRR, and trainings on business literacy and financial inclusion. E2 Mainstream GESI in policy, regulatory and institutional frameworks, and strengthen institutional and individual capacities on GESI

* Ensure that all project partners (including extension agents) are trained on GESI in the context of the BRCRN project. This will include training on gender-responsive and -sensitive planning, M&E and mainstreaming GESI into land use planning processes.
* Institutionalize gender-responsive measures and social inclusion in the forest and agroforestry sector. The institutionalization of gender considerations should be gender-inclusive and take into consideration potential social and environmental impacts.
* Develop a strategy and action plan to promote mainstreaming of climate resilient land use planning into river systems and local planning processes, ensuring that GESI considerations are integrated at all levels.

E3 Build the capacity of women, especially Dalit women, indigenous women and women from vulnerable and/or excluded households, and enable them to fully benefit from project activities

# Improve communication and knowledge dissemination

- **Improve knowledge sharing and data collection on climate change, NRM and DRR in the project area**: This can be achieved by establishing a Climate Knowledge Center (CKC). Ensure the integration of knowledge from diverse groups of people, including women, ethnic minorities, indigenous peoples, youth, and older persons. Consider traditional knowledge (see IPPF for more detailed information). Ensure local resource persons who manage the Churia Knowledge center include women, indigenous peoples, Dalit, and other marginalized groups.
- **Knowledge sharing**: This can be done through community-based training in literacy rates where certain groups of women within the project area exist, especially Terai-Dalits and Bhojpuri. Have literacy rates below the national average. Thus, it is important to engage local resource persons who manage the Churia Knowledge center include women, indigenous peoples, Dalit, and other marginalized groups.
- **Ensure regular communication between CBOs and supporting entities (government, civil society) regarding the implementation of gender equality measures**: This can be done by organizing meetings where such practices are shared. # Ensure participatory processes and ensure flexibility to identify suitable activities

- Implement activities that improve the food, water and energy security of communities, that are jeopardized under current business as usual (BAU) practices in the context of unfolding climate change.
- **Ensure the flexibility for CBOs and their members to decide what activities they want to adopt**: The project's approach of promoting a 'menu' of SMRM initiatives is a positive approach. It should be ensured that the menu includes options that are appropriate for their context (priorities, resources, time availability etc.). External agents, information providers, and facilitators should provide guidance on the potential risks associated with technologies and practices, as well as gender implications; if a potential activity could exacerbate time-poverty it should be clearly communicated, and discussion should take place to ensure that women's needs are met. Ensure that all people in the household, pooling resources and responsibilities with other CBO members.
- Provide targeted technical and financial support to promote the adoption of measures to increase the resilience of local communities to climate change, and/or that will mitigate the adverse effects of climate change on their livelihoods.

## Climate-resilient agriculture practices (related to GESI mainstreaming in training modules, and training extension trainers on GESI). This should include measures such as (see Appendix 1 for more detailed information) on the gender benefits, challenges and targeted interventions.

- Climate-resilient agriculture practices, such as reduced-impact tillage, improved water management, and integrated crop-livestock systems (ICLS), including integrating fodder production in cropping systems, agroforestry systems with fodder, and improved housing may alleviate time poverty, which is a major barrier to women's empowerment. However, the extent of poverty if clear roles and responsibilities of women and other household members are not discussed. It is thus important that extension agents are trained on gender equality principles, including ICLS practices and guidelines. In addition, to the greatest extent possible, extension agents should be trained in participatory approaches to identify roles and responsibilities between men and women (using culturally appropriate approaches), and identify potential ways to promote cooperation and collective action.

- Promotion of biogas, which can reduce fuelwood collection by women and alleviate time poverty, in addition to other benefits (see Feasibility study for more details).

- Restoration and sustainable management of degraded natural forests, critical river systems and wetlands to avoid emissions to restore forest quality and improve water quality.

- Tree planting on private, public and community lands to reduce pressure on natural forests for fuelwood, timber and forage. If activities such as free grazing and cutting are allowed in the project area, a Forest Resource Management Plan should be established, based on a participatory and inclusive decision-making process.

- On-farm planting of fodder, fuelwood and food-producing tree and grass species to reduce time spent by women in off-farm fodder collection.

- Clearly communicate the rights of beneficiaries throughout project implementation

- Hire gender-proper gender and safeguard specialists to oversee project monitoring and address related issues/concerns and complaints.

### Develop a Gender-Responsive and Socially Inclusive Monitoring and Evaluation System
* Design a gender-responsive M&E system to monitor the impacts from the project on women, and to support the achievement and monitoring of gender-specific targets. Include both quantitative and qualitative data.
* Develop a gender action plan, based on stakeholder input, to help overcome gender barriers, empower women and to strengthen the adaptive capacity of men and women. The GAE will be integrated into the BCRKN project’s M&E framework and into the project related measures within the BCRKN project, which will be integrated into the project's M&E.
* Within the PMU, the project’s gender and safeguards specialists will be responsible for overseeing the implementation of the Gender Action Plan, ensuring regular monitoring, evaluation and reporting. The gender officer from MoRT will serve as the gender focal


---
**Page 186**
---

point within the Project Steering Committee, and will provide support as needed for the implementation of the GAP. They will maintain close communication with the monitoring and reporting specialist and the relevant stakeholders.

• A baseline report to monitor and evaluate the proposed project will be established, which will be designed in a gender-responsive manner, ensuring that gender-specific and disaggregated statistics are included within the M&E framework.


| Gender Assessment and Action Plan BCRN | 34 |

# GENDER ACTION PLAN (GAP)

## Background

This Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been elaborated to mainstream gender-related measures into project activities so that gender inequalities are avoided or mitigated, and it maximizes climate and development co-benefits. It pays special attention to women, considering that women are not a homogenous group, and the additional challenges that women from indigenous communities face in accessing resources and services.

## Methodology

The GAP has been developed based on the following: available literature for the BRCRN project; available literature on gender-assessments of related projects and activities in the Churia region of Nepal; interviews with key experts and stakeholders working on gender empowerment in the Churia region, including representatives from government agencies, non-governmental organizations, and local women's groups in the proposed project area. In addition, a gender workshop was held in Kathmandu on February 21, 2018 to discuss and validate the results of the gender assessment. The outcomes of this workshop as well as the results of stakeholder consultations has been integrated into this document. Meeting minutes and attendance sheets for the consultations are available in Annex 3 of the project's ESMR.

# APPENDICES

## Appendix 1: Gender challenges and benefits of specific project investments

In the project region, women are highly engaged in land use activities, as described in the previous chapters. The feasibility study provides a detailed description of the different activities within each measure and the gender analysis carried out to identify the gender impacts with each of the measures.

The following paragraphs summarize the gender benefits and challenges, as well as the project's targeted measures associated with the main investments:

### Farmer Field Schools

**Current situation:** Women are less likely to participate in FFS than men.

**Expected situation:** Women participate in FFS and build their capacities on climate-resilient SNRM.

#### Gender Benefits:
1. Strengthened capacities and awareness for implementing climate-resilient SNRM
2. Improved access to information (production information, marketing information)
3. Empowerment of women
4. Engagement of women in higher earning livelihood activities
5. Improved mainstreaming of gender issues in extension and effective targeting of women's needs

#### Gender-specific Challenges:
1. Lack of extension services tailored for women (critical due to the feminization of agriculture) – lack of access, lack of female extension workers
2. Women are often less educated than men (lack of complementary knowledge)
3. Limited access to extension services because of their limited field presence; and women are often unable to travel long distances to access such services.
4. Women's productive work is not targeted by extension services
5. Time poverty

### How the project addresses this:
1. Awareness raising for extension services, targeting women
2. Mainstreaming GESI in all trainings
3. Hiring and training of female extension agents, including Dalit women, indigenous women, among others.
4. Training all extension workers on GESI; and key issues related to many women (e.g., time poverty)


---
**Page 198**
---

5. Supporting extension services to extend their reach (need for capacity building)
6. Designating local resource people (men and women) to encourage women’s engagement in climate-resilient SMRM and FFS
7. Taking into account women's work schedules and accessibility when planning trainings, taking into account women's work schedules and accessibility to ensure maximum participation and outreach; coordinate with local women's organizations to build on existing networks and-based approaches.
8. Respecting and documenting local knowledge from women, including women from indigenous nationalities, Dalit caste, and other marginalized groups

### Climate-resilient agricultural Practices
**Current situation:** Men and women do not adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices. Feminization trends in the agricultural sector increase time poverty for women and has resulted in the abandonment of some areas where there was insufficient labor.

**Expected situation:** Men and women adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices, with multiple social, environmental and economic benefits.

### Gender-benefits
9. Strengthened capacities and awareness for implementing climate-resilient SMRM
10. Improved access to information (production information, marketing information)
11. Empowerment of women, engagement of women in higher earning activities
12. Increased resilience of ecosystems and local people to climate change (see Feasibility study)
13. Increased productivity, livelihood diversification (e.g., agroforestry), economic benefits
14. Improved supply of different products (vegetables, fruit, NTPPs, etc.), which can substitute for those that are over-used, degraded or critical habitats (e.g., agroforestry)

### Gender-specific Challenges
1. Challenges associated with FFS (see above)
2. Lack of awareness of climate-resilient farming best practices (including time-saving practices and activities)
3. Physical challenges (e.g., heavy lifting, use of tools)
4. Inability to invest in inputs (seeds, materials)
5. Lack of awareness of marketing/value-adding opportunities
6. Lack of land (landless households)
7. Gender-based division of labor, with women often overburdened by men (e.g., cattle, cash crops)
8. Time poverty could be exacerbated by some activities, especially if roles are not clearly divided between men and women

How the project addresses this:


---
**Page 199**
---

9. Developing CBO management plans in a participatory manner for shared community resources, ensuring the active engagement of women.

10. Promoting membership in CBOs to improve the management of shared resources (e.g., forestland, water sources) and increase benefits, e.g., reduced time spent by women and girls to collect natural resources (e.g., fuelwood, fodder, NTFPs).

11. Improving accessibility and targeting of extension for women (see above)

12. Promoting climate-resilient SNMM (reduced tillage, etc.), with a focus on time-saving practices and improved livelihoods.

13. Providing a flexible menu of climate-resilient SNMM options, allowing the selection of practices that are suitable for the local context – supported by extension agents trained on GESI and NRM principles.

14. Promoting smart investments in sustainable equipment (e.g., hand tools), practices, and activities that can benefit CBO users

15. Establishing women user groups that have access to and use of land for highly vulnerable and marginalized households.

16. Implementing gender sensitization training for men and women in CBOs, including information on roles, responsibilities, rights, and opportunities.

17. Mainstreaming GESI in all training modules and FIs; all project staff and partners trained on GESI

18. Designating local resource people (men and women) to encourage women’s engagement in climate-resilient SNMM and FIS

19. Ensuring that the time and location of trainings consider women's work schedules and accessibility to ensure maximum participation and outreach; coordinate with local women's organizations and networks.

20. Respecting and documenting local knowledge from women, including women from indigenous nationalities, Dalit caste, and other marginalized groups

21. Raising awareness on climate-related disasters and risks and the link with unsustainable NRM, as well as SNMM/DRR measures.

### Restoration, maintenance and sustainable management of natural forest ecosystems

Current situation: Deforestation and forest degradation is accelerating in natural forest ecosystems.

Expected situation: Forest ecosystems are better maintained and protected resulting in reduced emissions from deforestation and degradation and increased resilience to climate change. Women’s participation in the restoration and management of degraded forests, critical watersheds and wetlands to avoid emissions, enhance sequestration and improve other ecosystem services (incl. biodiversity conservation).

### Gender-benefits

22. Strengthened capacities and awareness for implementing climate-resilient SNMM

23. Improved access to information (production information, marketing information)


---
**Page 200**
---

24. Empowerment of women
25. Engagement of women in higher earning activities

Gender-specific Challenges:
26. Challenges associated with FFS (see above)
27. Lack of awareness of climate change and lack of information on alternative land use systems; Lack of alternative sources of forest products, lack of alternative livestock systems
28. Inability to invest in inputs (seeds, materials, fencing)
29. Lack of land (landless households)
30. Insufficient tree nurseries to support restoration needs (in highly degraded areas)
31. Time constraints

How the project addresses this:
32. Developing CBO management plans in a participatory manner for shared community resources, ensuring the active engagement of women
33. Promoting membership in CBOs to ensure the management of shared resources (e.g., forests) and to provide access to other time-saving benefits, e.g., reduced time spent by women and girls to collect natural resources (e.g., fuelwood, fodder, NTFPs.)
34. Promoting climate-resilient SNM (reduced tillage, etc.), with a focus on time-saving practices and activities
35. Providing a flexible menu of climate resilient SNM options, allowing the selection of practices that are suitable for the local context - supported by extension agents trained on GESI and gender-sensitive approaches
36. Improving accessibility and targeting of extension for women (see above)
37. Promoting smart investments in sustainable equipment (e.g., hand tools), practices and activities that can benefit women
38. Establishing public land forest user groups that improve access to, and use of land for highly vulnerable and marginalized households
39. Implementing gender sensitivity training for men and women in CBOs, including climate-resilient SNM and FFS
40. Mainstreaming GESI in all training modules and FFS, by project staff and partners trained on GESI
41. Developing local resource people (men and women) to encourage women's engagement in climate-resilient SNM and FFS
42. Ensuring that the time and location of trainings consider women's work schedules and accommodate their needs for childcare and other out-of-home commitments, connecting rural women's organizations to build on existing networks and best practices for outreach
43. Respecting and documenting local knowledge from women, including women from indigenous nationalities, Dalit caste, and other marginalized groups


---
**Page 201**
---

### 44. Raising awareness on climate-induced disasters and risks and the link with unsustainable NRM, as well as SNIM, DRR measures

#### Establishment and operation of multi-purpose tree nurseries.
**Current situation:** Insufficient nurseries (in terms of production and quality) to support forest restoration and/or forest seedling production for agroforestry, horticulture.

**Expected situation:** Establishment and operation of multi-purpose tree nurseries.

**Gender-benefits:**
1. Strengthened capacities, preparation of high-quality seedlings.
2. Increased seed quality, yields/ promised quantities that can in turn support men and women to reduce the reliance on forests and/or support forest restoration that in turn increases their resilience to climate change
3. Empowerment of women.

48. Employment in nurseries

**Gender-specific Challenges:**
1. Challenges associated with FFS (see above)
2. Time poverty

#### How the project addresses this:
51. Hiring women in nurseries.
52. Mainstreaming GSP in nursery guidelines and protocols
53. Ensuring gender-responsive monitoring and evaluation.

### Investments in soil and water conservation
**Current situation:** The communities living within them, are vulnerable to climate-induced erosion, sedimentation and flooding risks.

**Expected situation:** Investments in soil and water conservation enhance the resilience of ecosystems and local men and women to climate-induced erosion, sedimentation and flooding risks.

#### Gender-benefits:
54. Strengthened capacities on climate-resilient land use planning, ecosystem-based adaptation, and soil and water conservation.
55. Increased resilience to climate-induced erosion, sedimentation and flooding risks, which in turn reduces damages, safeguards land/ infrastructure, and even reduces the risk of GBV (gender-based displacements/damaged infrastructure associated with natural disasters and hazards).


---
**Page 202**
---

Gender-specific Challenges:
56. Challenges associated with FFS (see above)
57. Time poverty

How the project addresses these:

* Developing a sub-system-level action plan to improve coordination of upstream and downstream investments through participatory processes that engage women, ensuring GESI is mainstreamed in each action plan.
* Engaging women in decision-making processes in identifying vulnerable areas, critical ecosystems and integration of women's issues in subriver system level SNRM planning; CBD management of shared resources (e.g. water conservation ponds, check dams, etc.) can reduce vulnerability to climate change.
* Developing provincial disaster risk reduction plans, ensuring GESI is mainstreamed
* Applying participatory land use planning processes to develop CBM management plans, ensuring the engagement of women in decision-making and resource allocation.
* Improving accessibility and targeting of extension for women (see above)
* Conducting gender sensitization trainings
* Mainstreaming GESI in all training modules and FFS
* Training all project staff and partners on GESI
* Developing a gender action plan (men vs. women) to support communication, knowledge dissemination and encourage women's engagement in climate-resilient SNRM and FFS
* Respect and document local knowledge from women, including women from indigenous communities, in the development of provincial DRR plans.
* Supporting awareness raising for women in vulnerable communities about climate-induced disasters and risks and the link with unsustainable NRM, as well as SNRM/DRR measures. Engage women including indigenous peoples and marginalized groups in the development of provincial DRR plan
